                                 Preface

TM 4-33.31 describes principles for planning, establishing, and executing maintenance operations in
protracted extreme cold weather environments. Furthermore, this publication describes principles of
maintenance operations and precautions for equipment operators and their supervisors.

The principal audience for this TM is all members of the profession of arms. Commanders and staffs of Army
headquarters serving as joint task force or multinational headquarters should also refer to applicable joint or
multinational doctrine concerning the range of military operations and joint or multinational forces. Trainers
and educators throughout the Army will also use this publication.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates need to ensure their decisions and actions comply with applicable
United States, international, and, in some cases, host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels
will ensure that their Service members operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and the rules of
engagement (see FM 6-27 for legal compliance).

TM 4-33.31 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both
the glossary and the text. TM 4-33.31 is not the proponent publication for any terms or definitions. For other
definitions shown in the text, the term is italicized, and the number of the proponent publication follows the
definition.

TM 4-33-31 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States
and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of TM 4-33.31 is the United States Army Ordnance School. The preparing agency is the
G-3/5/7 Doctrine Division, United States Army Combined Arms Support Command. Send comments and
recommendations on a DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to
Commander, United States Army Combined Arms Support Command, ATTN: ATCL-TDID (TM 4-33.31),
2221 Adams Ave, Building 5020, Fort Lee, VA, 23801-1809; or submit an electronic DA Form 2028 by e-
mail to usarmy.lee.tradoc.list.cascom-g3-5-7-tdid-doc@army.mil.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  v

                              Introduction

The Army’s fundamental purpose is to win wars through land force dominance regardless of location or
climate. This manual provides general principles and technical guidance for field maintenance, operation,
and repair of equipment under extreme cold weather conditions from freezing down to temperatures
below -65 degrees Fahrenheit (°F) or -54 degrees Celsius (°C).

Leaders must be familiar with ATP 3-90.96—the Army’s manual for cold weather operations. It provides the
doctrinal foundation to understand the Arctic region’s operational environment and how to conduct military
operations in it. The fundamentals of the manual are applicable to all cold weather environments.

Military operations in extreme cold conditions occur at the limit of what is safe and what is dangerous. TM
4-33.31 is focused on a combat environment where Soldiers cannot always count on the availability of
resources that sustain them in garrison. This manual assist unit commanders, supervisors (maintenance
officers, maintenance technicians, motor sergeants, and section leaders), and maintenance personnel in
anticipating and planning for the onset of extreme cold weather or an arctic deployment. Further, it is the
intent of this manual to stimulate creativity and forward thinking and support the development of mission-
focused standard operating procedures.

TM 4-33.31 is intended to familiarize equipment operators and maintainers on the unique difficulties of
maintenance in extreme cold weather. This TM shows ways to adjust maintenance operations in arctic and
extreme cold weather, and the expected performance of materials below freezing temperatures. It provides a
consolidated overview of the special and unique difficulties that Soldiers can expect to encounter while
performing field maintenance in extreme cold weather environments.

Maintainers and operators should refer to the equipment or weapon system specific TM or lubrication order
(LO) as the primary source of information and guidance for equipment maintenance and operation. Failure
to follow this guidance and the instructions provided in the item-specific TM can result in injury to personnel
and damage to equipment.

This TM provides general information on ground equipment maintenance in cold weather environments and
compliments ADP 4-0, FM 4-0, ATP 4-33, ATP 3-90.96, and other related technical publications.

TM 4-33.31 is written within a very narrow context. The focus of this TM is planning and execution of
maintenance operations in arctic and extreme cold environments. The tactical combat employment of
weapons and equipment is beyond the scope of this publication. Likewise, these manual addresses human
factor concerns in a general context, but the primary medical guidance source is the U.S. Army Medical
Department Center and School.

Although little is written here about preventive maintenance checks and services, conducting them at the
required interval remains a critical task. To avoid redundancy, the focus of this publication is more on general
material characteristics; it does not replace equipment TMs for equipment operations, servicing, or repair.
Considering the broad number of subjects covered here, readers should expect that advances in technology
will render some of the specifics found in this TM obsolete in a short time. Nonetheless, the principles that
underpin this TM should remain valid and relevant.

TM 4-33.31 contains eight chapters and five appendices. Each chapter is written as a stand-alone treatment
of the chapter subject material. There may be some repetition of information and guidance:

Chapter 1 provides commanders and leaders with key tasks and an overview of maintenance operations in
extreme cold conditions. This chapter discusses maintenance planning with a greater emphasis on risk
management, quality and safety.

Chapter 2 provides general information on the properties and performance of parts and materials in cold
environments.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  vii


---

Introduction

     Chapter 3 examines the best practices for each type of automotive battery technology as applicable to the
     Army.
     Chapter 4 discusses petroleum, oils and lubricant products and the Army’s single fuel policy.
     Chapter 5 highlights a variety of issues common to the operation of any vehicle or motor in a cold
     environment. Much of the discussion is focused on starting an engine without damaging the vehicle.
     Chapter 6 highlights a variety of issues common to maintaining the functional capability of any small arms
     in a cold environment.
     Chapter 7 provides an overview of crew-served weapons systems.
     Chapter 8 looks at ways that Soldiers can act to protect and operate electrical and electronic devices and
     equipment in cold.
     Appendix A provides considerations for unit maintenance standard operating procedures, policies, priorities
     and procedures.
     Appendix B provides a quick reference for battery maintenance personnel in the event of an emergency.
     Appendix C discusses best practices for starting a vehicle engine in cold weather.
     Appendix D provides information on the properties of common petroleum, oils and lubricant products used
     by U.S. ground forces.
     Appendix E serves as a reminder for units to comply with federal, state, local, and host-nation environmental
     regulations. Conformity to such regulations becomes a part of the command risk assessment and response
     planning.

viii  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025

                                                 Chapter 1

               Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

    This chapter provides an overview of arctic and cold weather maintenance and identifies
    maintenance challenges relating to cold weather operations. It provides guidance on risk
    management, quality, safety, and soldier welfare. The chapter also discusses fuel-burning
    personnel heaters, maintenance facilities, and commercial off-the-shelf equipment
    considerations.

THE COLD WEATHER CHALLENGE

     1-1. Operating in the cold has its own unique challenges. Leaders must ensure that weapons, vehicles, and
     any other equipment are fully winterized to withstand the cold as outlined in their respective technical
     manuals (TMs). Leaders will also have to draw upon experience and find viable and creative solutions to
     overcome maintenance challenges in the cold and successfully conduct operations.

     Note. Leaders must be familiar with ATP 3-90.96, the Army’s manual for cold weather operations.

     Maintainers and operators should refer to equipment TMs and lubrication orders (LOs) as the primary
     sources of information for equipment operations, component or system theories of operations,
     characteristics, limitations, and maintenance procedures.

     1-2. More than half of the earth's dry land surface encounters freezing temperatures for at least one month
     every year. In some cases, military forces may need to carry out sustained operations in temperatures as low
     as -65 Fahrenheit (°F) (-54 Celsius [°C]) or lower. For military purposes, a cold region is an area where snow
     or cold temperatures hinder movement or operations for more than one month per year. In such challenging
     conditions, personnel may suffer from decreased efficiency and are at risk of cold-related injuries.
     Additionally, vehicles, equipment, and devices are susceptible to frequent breakdowns, supply issues may
     become more prominent, and operations may be significantly constrained and complicated in extreme cold
     weather environments.

     1-3. Operations in extreme cold weather conditions have command-wide implications for equipment
     readiness. To say that something is cold-soaked means that it poses a risk to Soldier welfare or a risk of
     equipment damage occurring during moderate operation. Getting ahead of the cold challenge will require
     adaptive leadership and innovative or creative thinking. Collaboration will be required between the
     maintenance team and the unit leadership.

     1-4. Unit leaders are responsible for ensuring that personnel and equipment can withstand the challenges of
     extreme cold weather. Army equipment and devices are exceptional in performing in extreme cold climates.
     However, it is crucial for Soldiers and their leaders to understand the effects of extreme cold weather on
     equipment. Leaders must plan, train, and practice unit operations in harsh weather conditions.

             Note. Army regulations and doctrine standards do not specify a minimum temperature down to
             which military units must be capable of operating.

     1-5. About one quarter of the earth’s land mass may be termed severely cold. Another quarter of the earth is
     termed moderately cold. Areas (including most of North America and Eurasia) where the mean temperatures
     during the coldest months reach extreme cold and below are shown in Figure 1-1 on page 2. Although the
     following figure does not conform to general military temperature categories, it can help provide planners
     with historical weather data.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  1


---

Chapter 1

                                         Figure 1-1. Cold regions of the world

COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS

     1-6. Commanders and leaders understand that as temperatures drop below freezing, the unit’s ability to
     maintain its normal high degree of operational pace will decline due to both human factors and materiel
     factors. However, this decline can be managed, and units can still achieve mission success through deliberate
     planning, intentional realistic training, and effective leadership.

     1-7. Operating equipment in temperatures above 10°F (-12°C) presents few problems. Conditions are like
     those experienced during winter in the northern part of the continental United States (CONUS).

     1-8. ATP 3-90.96 classifies cold temperatures below the temperate weather range into five categories as
     depicted in Figure 1-2. The temperature categories are as follows:

          • Wet cold is between 39°F and 20°F, (4°C to -6°C).
          • Dry cold is between 19°F and -4°F, (-7°C to -20°C).
          • Intense cold is between -5°F and -25°F, (-21°C to -32°C).
          • Extreme cold is between -25°F and -40°F, (-32°C to -40°C).
          • Hazardous cold is -40°F (-40°C) and below.

             Note. All temperatures and temperature conversions between Fahrenheit and Celsius in this
             manual are approximate and rounded off to the nearest whole number.

     1-9. Wet-cold conditions occur when wet snow and rain often accompany cold conditions. A wet-cold
     environment poses a greater threat to troops and equipment than a dry and cold environment due to the ground
     becoming slushy and muddy and clothing and equipment becoming perpetually wet and damp. Under wet-
     cold conditions, the ground alternates between freezing and thawing as the temperatures fluctuate above and
     below the freezing point. The freezing point is the temperature at which a liquid or liquid-like substance or
     material changes its state to solid. As temperatures warm, heavy equipment can sink into softening ground
     or mud and become immobilized. Should the temperatures turn back to freezing, the immobilized vehicle
     can freeze in place as the ground hardens. This makes planning equipment recovery operations problematic.

     1-10. Operations become more difficult under dry cold and intense cold temperatures (from 19°F [-7°C]
     to -25°F [-32°C]). At the warmer end of this range, lack of winterization results in only a slight loss of
     operating efficiency. Winterization is a modification listed in the equipment TM that is made to vehicles,
     weapons, equipment, machines, or related apparatus to adapt to the environmental conditions and restore or
     extend serviceability.

2          TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                              Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

                        Figure 1-2. Categories of cold at various temperature ranges

     1-11. Non-acclimated troops usually show signs of having difficulty at cold temperatures above -10°F
     (-23°C). Operations become extreme when temperatures drop below -25°F (-32°C). Operations become
     hazardous at near -40°F (-40°C), and temperatures lower than that require maximum efforts of well-trained
     personnel to perform the simplest tasks. These difficulties occur even with fully winterized uniforms and
     equipment.

     1-12. The wind chill effect makes the atmospheric temperature feel colder than the actual air temperature. It
     is a concept that relates to the rate of heat loss from personnel under windy conditions. Wind chill removes
     heat from the body faster, driving down skin temperature and, eventually, internal body temperature.
     Commanders and staffs need to factor in the wind chill index during planning, and leaders must constantly
     evaluate weather conditions as operations are underway.

PLANNING

     1-13. Extreme-cold and hazardous-cold adversely affect the ability of a unit to accomplish its mission.
     Understanding the specific impacts of a cold environment on materiel and personnel performance can help
     units in planning measures to counter these effects. Maintenance leaders need to identify the probable
     duration and severity of the cold weather and adjust their plans and policies accordingly. Maintainers and
     operators performing maintenance are subjected to different risks than Soldiers on foot patrol. For example,
     bulky clothing is easily caught on jagged edges, moving parts, or in tight spaces, which can cause personal
     injury. Also, mechanics laying on their backs on frozen ground to get under vehicles can expose them to
     hypothermia or pneumonia risk that standing Soldiers would otherwise avoid.

     1-14. Planning for cold weather operations requires the full range of Army planning methodologies found in
     ADP 5-0 and ADP 4-0. When planning for missions in cold weather, leaders must realistically evaluate the
     impact that physical factors will have on both tactical and sustainment operations. For example, units are less
     maneuverable in icy conditions or deep snow. Deep snow reduces vehicle traction on hills and increases the
     chances of breakdown/damage caused by hidden obstacles. Wheeled vehicle units are restricted almost
     exclusively to roads. Trailers and towed artillery pieces have reduced mobility. Emplacement of artillery
     pieces for fire missions is very difficult. Operators must be very proficient in basic skills that in more
     temperate zones they might have taken for granted, including keeping moving parts free to cycle, equipment
     recovery, working with tire chains, and slave starting.

     1-15. Cold weather environments hinder mobility and restrict supply lines. Maintenance planners must
     account for potential delays in the resupply of repair parts and may consider advanced manufacturing
     solutions (where practical) to overcome supply shortfalls. See ATP 4-31 for more information on advanced
     manufacturing.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  3


---

Chapter 1

     1-16. Leaders should draft contingency plans for their units in case of a “no-notice” deployment into a cold
     region. For maintenance, leaders should develop standby requests for winterization equipment and draft
     procedures for cold weather operation of each weapon, vehicle, power generation system, item of equipment,
     and shelter. Even if deployment to a cold region is not anticipated, conducting cold weather operations
     rehearsal of capabilities (called ROC) drills is beneficial for developing unit leaders. Operators should train
     on using snow chains, alternative lubricants, cold starting procedures, and other related cold region
     supplements to maintenance. Such training helps Soldiers adjust to thinking analytically (outside the box)
     and provides the unit with a defined and rehearsed contingency plan.

     1-17. Planning is critical to the success and survival of units operating in cold weather. Planning for cold
     weather operations should begin long before the actual onset of freezing temperatures. Sufficient time must
     be allotted to inventory and order necessary winterization items. After the arrival of winterization items,
     additional time is needed for leaders to train and license (or certify) Soldiers in proper cold weather
     procedures and the use of specialized equipment. Planners should consider the following:

          • Plans must be both detailed and flexible.
          • Unit operating procedures must conform to command policy and local command supplements on

                Army safety management and risk management.
          • The cold will make mission command more difficult.
          • Personnel must be properly trained and prepared.
          • Cross training will be needed to maintain operating tempo.
          • All units, especially mechanized and armored, will require more time to accomplish tasks; the cold

                will increase fuel consumption and the demand for both wet-cell and dry-cell batteries.
          • Although equipment is designed to function in the cold, it will be less reliable, thereby increasing

                maintenance time and supply problems. Maintenance, supply, and engineer units may require an
                augmentation of additional personnel or accept a reduction in the achievable workload.
          • Anticipate a potential increase in medical problems (for example, injuries from fatigue, frostbite,
                hypothermia, pneumonia, dehydration, and even heat casualties). Parasites and vermin tend to
                congregate or proliferate in areas kept warm by Soldiers.
          • Prolonged exposure to cold and extended hours of darkness will lead to increased psychological
                stress.
          • Field feeding operations will need to include providing a continuous supply of warm supplemental
                rations to accommodate the increased caloric demands of working in the cold.
          • Leadership must establish a regimen to more carefully monitor Soldier hygiene to prevent Soldiers
                from cutting corners on personal care and hygiene due to fatigue and the discomfort of the cold.
          • Establish policy and procedures to ensure that documentation in Soldier medical records identifies
                all cold injury medical diagnoses.

     1-18. Commanders will need their maintainers’ expertise to assess the serviceability of cold weather
     protection equipment and devices. Commanders will also need the expertise of medical personnel to assess
     the procedures for Soldier care in cold weather. Considerations may include—

          • Heated shelter for equipment maintenance.
          • Individual heated storage for the components of end items, basic issue items, additional

                authorization list items, and radios for each vehicle in the fleet.
          • Proper clothing and tools for mechanics.
          • Adequate portable heaters.
          • Identifying equipment susceptible to condensation damage and providing anti-condensation

                containers.
          • Capability to store and issue antifreeze materials, winterized fuels, hydraulic fluids, lubricants, and

                other fluids.
          • Extra lighting to accommodate limited daylight in northern latitudes.
          • Increased velocity or volume to supply repair parts.
          • Sufficient equipment for snow and ice removal.
          • Direct medical support presence in maintenance areas.

4  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

          • Adequate first aid kits, eye wash stations, and toxic chemical rinse-off stations.
          • Hazardous materials (HAZMAT) storage. Understand current laws and command policy.
          • Survival kits and bags to protect against the cold.

TRAINING

     1-19. Since most U.S. units are stationed in temperate or tropical climates, Soldiers generally lack adequate
     training or experience in cold weather operations and maintenance. Commanders must ensure that Soldiers
     stationed in temperate climates are prepared to deploy to cold climates and conduct operations effectively.
     This preparation entails proper planning, training, and equipping. Expertise in cold weather operations will
     grow from experience and cannot be gained solely from reading a manual. Applicable training and
     operational experience are necessary to develop successful cold weather field techniques. Refer to ATP
     3-90.96 and TC 21-3.

     1-20. First-line supervisors will need to assess their personnel to determine what types of specialized cold
     weather training or refresher training may be necessary. Maintenance training for cold weather environments
     should focus on—

          • Soldier health and welfare.
          • Changes in the physical properties and performance of equipment and supplies caused by the cold.
          • Adaptations of processes and procedures to protect from the impact of the cold.

     1-21. Commanders should schedule a specific day or trigger event for the unit to convert from normal
     temperate weather operations to cold weather operations. A trigger event is a predetermined or predefined
     event that causes a change or something to happen. It is at this point that the rules change for personal care
     and the care of equipment. The unit must prepare for such an operational conversion prior to the trigger event.

Soldier Health and Welfare

     1-22. Cold injuries are most likely to occur when unprepared personnel are exposed to cold temperatures,
     even with the proper planning and equipment. The category of cold weather, experience and training of
     personnel, and the type of operation will help planners anticipate the severity of injuries that personnel may
     experience. The Soldier’s clothing, physical condition, and mental makeup are also determining factors. A
     well-disciplined and well-trained Soldier can be protected in even the most adverse conditions.

     1-23. Cold weather training begins when all unit elements have reviewed and updated their policies and plans
     that address cold weather operational standards and injury prevention. Commanders should ensure that a cold
     weather injury prevention program is established, and that training is provided to all personnel assigned to
     the command and subordinate commands. This task is complete when all unit elements have reported their
     compliance to the safety office.

Resources

     1-24. For maintenance to be effective, operators and maintainers need to be trained in anticipating the effects
     of the cold on their equipment. Severe cold can change the physical properties of metals and fluids. Metals
     become more brittle as temperatures drop below freezing. Petroleum products that are focused on
     withstanding high temperatures can break down and fail in the cold.

MAINTENANCE TEMPO

     1-25. Cold weather will cause maintenance work to take longer. Operators and maintenance personnel must
     be proficient in the use of all winterization and arctic kit items including heating devices and added insulated
     covers. The preventive maintenance checks and services (PMCS) instructions for winterization kits may be
     unfamiliar tasks for operators and require at least initial supervision and instruction by experienced and
     mechanically inclined leaders. Operators are responsible for knowing and understanding the procedures
     outlined in equipment TMs for operation under unusual conditions.

     1-26. Workflow seriously slows down in temperatures below -20°F (-29°C). Maintainers must allow
     equipment to thaw and warm up before making repairs. This time lag seriously impacts workflow and must
     be considered in all planning.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  5


---

Chapter 1

     1-27. Personnel efficiency is also reduced by the bulky and clumsy clothing worn in severely cold areas. It
     is dangerous to handle cold metal with bare hands; operators/mechanics must always wear mittens or gloves.
     Losing the sense of touch further reduces the Soldier's efficiency. Even most routine operations, including
     handling latches or opening engine compartments, can become time-consuming and frustrating when
     performed with protected hands. This can be offset by providing heated buildings or shelters to increase work
     efficiency and morale. Shelter can refer to anything from a building to a vehicle cab, tent, windbreak, or
     anything that can effectively mitigate the extremes of the exposed environment. At the very least, a more
     organized and intensive effort is required from maintainers to maintain workflow in the extreme cold.

     1-28. Commanders may want to try to obtain additional personnel to maintain maintenance tempo. They may
     consider providing on-the-job training for vehicle and equipment operators to perform some maintainer tasks.
     This training should be provided by a highly experienced maintainer and, upon completion, the personnel
     trained should receive a signed document affirming approval from the unit commander. Such a program
     requires a defined training curriculum and defined limits of what maintainer tasks supporting Soldiers can
     perform.

RISK MANAGEMENT AND SAFETY

     1-29. Units should prepare for hazards associated with the winter season or cold environments by reviewing
     and updating policies and plans that address cold weather operational techniques, cold injury prevention, and
     the preservation of equipment. Units should develop a cold weather deployment program as a quality
     management process.

     1-30. Units should conduct inventories of winterization kits, vehicle supplemental heating systems, space
     heaters, and other cold weather equipment well in advance of cold weather operations. This ensures operators
     and unit supply staff have sufficient lead time to process requests and receive maintenance supplies, tools,
     devices, and equipment. During these inventories, check that on-hand items are present, serviceable, and
     complete.

     1-31. Maintenance managers should be ready to train and certify at least 20% of Soldiers within the command
     in the proper assembly and operation of shelters in conjunction with shelter heaters. The training should give
     special attention to the proper handling of combustible heating fuels, avoiding runaway fires, and preventing
     carbon monoxide poisoning.

     1-32. Cold weather is a distraction for operators performing maintenance. Supervisors and section leaders of
     line elements should work in close coordination to prevent lax maintenance. They must ensure that otherwise
     well-trained equipment operators stay focused on performing preventive maintenance and operator
     maintenance according to TM standards.

SOLDIER WELFARE

     1-33. The commander establishes standards for medical personnel and combat lifesaver qualifications. The
     unit’s senior medical officer is responsible for implementing and applying these standards. Medical personnel
     and combat lifesaver certified Soldiers should receive medical refresher training focused on cold weather
     casualties and their proper treatment.

             Note. Only qualified medical personnel have authority to diagnose and treat cold injuries. Only
             the unit medical officer can verify who is qualified and competent to handle cold injuries.

     1-34. Cold weather training should include the proper wear of Army-issued cold weather protection clothing
     such as the extended cold weather clothing system (ECWCS). This system can be worn in any combination
     of layers depending on the mission requirements and the needs of the individual Soldier. However, first-line
     leaders must monitor each Soldier for overheating and excessive sweating. Sweating and moisture on clothing
     in cold weather can cause injuries to personnel.

     1-35. Cold weather operations require more rigorous preparation than those in temperate environments. The
     following is a list of a few factors that affect personnel performance.

          • Adequate heated shelter. Shelter from the elements is secondary only to defending against enemy
                actions.

6  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

          • Proper nutrition. Soldiers need to eat more food and drink more water than normal.
          • Preparedness for sudden weather changes. Weather change can alter behaviors, mood, and morale

                of individual Soldiers and the unit overall.
          • Training. Avoid cold injuries by using a buddy system and conduct frequent self-checks, especially

                when individuals are not active or their duties require them to remove their gloves.
          • Robust medical support. Immediately treat personnel showing any sign/symptom of cold injury.

                Sick, injured, and wounded individuals are susceptible to additional cold injuries.
          • Adequate planning and preparation. Each Soldier should be individually prepared to survive if

                separated from the unit:
                ▪ In wet-cold weather, Soldiers must have immediate access to their wet weather gear.
                ▪ In dry-cold or colder weather, Soldiers must have a survival kit attached to their person.
                ▪ Anytime Soldiers are traveling by vehicle, they must have their cold weather survival bag with

                     them to include a sleeping bag, water, and extra clothing (at least dry socks, underwear, and the
                     next level of ECWCS) in the vehicle.

Battle Buddy Protection

     1-36. Safety awareness is especially vital during the early part of a cold weather operation, or for newly
     arrived Soldiers who lack experience with the cold. Leaders should consider instituting a battle buddy
     program, having Soldiers working in small teams to keep tasks and projects moving in a timely manner.
     Individual safety, as well as mission accomplishment, has the highest chance of success when Soldiers are
     paired in battle buddy teams to watch over each other. This is especially true for Soldiers that are not fully
     acclimated and are still learning how to deal with an unusually cold environment. Both Soldiers may identify
     dangers and save a situation from going dangerously wrong.

Human Factors

     1-37. Soldiers must be kept reasonably warm to maintain normal metabolic processes. Rest and nourishment
     are also vital. This section highlights some key considerations that leaders and planners should bear in mind
     during maintenance operations.

     1-38. The correct approach to operating in cold weather will go a long way toward keeping Soldiers healthy
     and seeing them through difficult times. There are five basic principles to remember:

          • Know the hazards of exposure to the cold. Soldiers and their leaders must know the importance of
                personal hygiene, exercise, care of the feet and hands, and the use of ECWCS protective clothing.

          • Keep in shape. Cold weather clothing is heavy and an additional weight to carry. Along with the
                difficulty of walking in snow, Soldiers will expend a lot of energy just staying warm. The
                importance of being in excellent physical condition cannot be over emphasized.

          • Drink plenty of water. In the cold, Soldiers may only drink when thirsty. However, the cold can
                short circuit the thirst mechanism, so Soldiers drink less water than they need to prevent dehydration.
                Soldiers may also be reluctant to drink cold water because it detracts from keeping the core body
                temperature warm. Still, they need to drink plenty of water to avoid dehydration and the fatigue that
                will follow. Do not allow Soldiers to eat raw snow as a substitute for water; the moisture content of
                snow is relatively low and it will lower the body’s core temperature.

          • Eat to replenish energy. Ensuring adequate nutrition is crucial for maintaining high performance. It
                is important for Soldiers to consume regular, satisfying, and hot meals to replenish their energy.
                Even when they are not feeling hungry, it is essential for them to eat to replace lost calories and to
                sustain their fitness levels. It is also worth noting that this places an additional burden on the cooking
                staff.

          • Maintain a positive attitude. Soldiers will find themselves confronting many new and challenging
                problems, but none that a properly trained Soldier cannot overcome. Soldiers’ attitudes toward the
                cold will reflect that of their leader.

     1-39. Soldiers must consume more calories per day in cold climates than in temperate areas. Most of the food
     Soldiers eat and drink in cold weather goes towards maintaining their body heat. A smaller proportion is
     spent on producing energy for physical work. A minimum of 4,500 calories per day is necessary for personnel

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  7


---

Chapter 1

     performing hard work in the cold. Under conditions of severe stress, Soldiers may require as many as 6,000
     calories per day to maintain health and performance.

     1-40. The body loses liquid at an exceptional rate in cold conditions, up to 2 quarts of water per day by
     respiration alone. No matter how carefully the amount of clothing and ventilation is adjusted, any heavy
     exertion exacts a toll in sweat and loss of moisture through breathing. These liquids must be regularly
     replaced, preferably by hot drinks. Drinks that contain sugar have the additional advantage of providing extra
     calories. The daily requirement will vary from a minimum of 3½ to over 5 quarts of water a day in
     circumstances involving heavy exertion.

     1-41. Heat injuries are also a hazard in cold environments. Soldiers tend to overdress and not hydrate enough
     in the cold, increasing the likelihood of heat-related issues during periods of exertion. Leaders should allow
     extra time for clothing adjustments (up scaling or down scaling the layers of clothing as the temperature
     climbs or drops) and provide additional liquids for proper hydration.

     1-42. Leaders should be alert to changes in weather and drops in temperatures. This is when cold injuries
     unexpectedly occur. Such situations must be anticipated in unit policy, and on-call resources should be
     positioned for immediate activation. In a 24-hour operational setting, safety hazards are more likely to occur
     during the most inopportune times, particularly from the late hours of the night to daybreak when
     temperatures plummet to freezing temperatures.

Cold Injuries

     1-43. Personnel with prior cold weather injuries are more susceptible to additional injury to the affected area
     or limb. Cold injuries hinder blood circulation that regulates skin temperature in the affected area. Leaders
     should identify Soldiers who have previous cold weather injuries. A colored tape marking system can be used
     on outer garments and head gear to help other Soldiers and medical personnel identify individuals with prior
     cold injuries. Consult medical personnel to determine the degree of threat of cold weather, required
     precautions, and limitations in activities.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Any injury that draws blood, incurs bruising that breaks skin, shows any
                    discoloration, swelling of skin, or has pain that persists for over half an
                    hour is serious.

     1-44. An injury that seems minor in cold weather can rapidly turn serious issue if not promptly addressed. In
     cold environments, even seemingly mild injuries can have lasting effects on the Soldier. It is important to be
     proactive in treating cold injuries to prevent long-term consequences. Leaders should consult senior medical
     personnel for a full discussion on cold injuries.

     1-45. Whenever possible, medics should provide direct support within motor pools and maintenance
     activities during maintenance operations in sub-freezing conditions. Medical support is provided as specified
     by unit command policy and should be located at the point of highest risk. Medical support for maintenance
     operations should provide—

          • On-site diagnosis and medical treatment of injured personnel.
          • Referral or evacuation of personnel with serious cold injuries to higher medical services when

                warranted by the nature of the injury.
          • Appropriate recommendations to the commander regarding an injured Soldier returning to duty

                (with or without profile limitations).
          • Spot checks that Soldiers are practicing proper hygiene and injury prevention measures as specified

                by command policy and Army standards. These checks also include unit adherence to proper field
                sanitation and the adequacy of sanitation resources.
          • Weather monitoring as it applies to working conditions.
          • Documentation of all injuries treated, and filing of that documentation in the Soldier’s medical
                records.

8  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

          • Follow-up examination at the end of the duty period for Soldiers who incurred an initial injury. In
                those instances where the injury has gotten worse or exhibits more symptoms, the medic will
                reassess the correctness of a profile, provide the commander with updated information, and make
                appropriate annotations on the medical records.

     1-46. Once a cold injury has occurred, the commander and site leaders should immediately reassess all the
     protective measures that are in place and validate operating procedures. The site leader's concern is not only
     for the injured Soldier, but also for the rest of the force on duty. Where one injury has occurred, more can
     follow if the circumstances leading to the first injury are not addressed.

     1-47. When a cold injury occurs, medical personnel on site should make inquiries and provide a root cause
     analysis for the site leader. This information should be provided to the site leader as soon as possible. Leaders
     can then use it to help determine what corrective action should be taken to safeguard Soldiers and mission
     accomplishment on the work site.

     1-48. In worsening weather situations, further corrective action and protective measures may be warranted.
     Site leaders should expect that such events will occur at the most inconvenient times, like late at night. It is
     at these times that commanders or site leaders may have to draw additional resources to protect Soldiers on
     duty from the worsening cold. Where additional resources are not available or inadequate to mitigate the
     threat, the commander may consider procedural changes or mission adjustments.

Survival Equipment System for Stranded Soldiers

     1-49. Soldiers need to have survival gear during cold weather field operations. Leaders should develop
     survival kits and bags for Soldiers operating outside of base camps. In a base camp, garrison, or installation,
     there is only a limited risk of dire cold weather emergencies, and survival resources are more likely to be
     available in the event one occurs. However, in the field or a combat area, Soldiers can become isolated or
     separated from the sustainment resources of their unit, making survival gear necessary.

     1-50. The commander conducts a risk assessment to determine if survival kits or bags are required and their
     contents. Survival kits are carried on the Soldier for sudden emergencies of a short duration (36 hours or
     less). Survival bags are carried on a vehicle for emergencies which may last longer than 24 hours.

             Note. Army regulations do not require, nor does the Army have a defined military standard that
             prescribes a survival kit equipment list for ground forces.

     1-51. There are three functions of survival gear that remain the same regardless of geographical location and
     climate:

          • Sustain the Soldier.
          • Protect the Soldier from threats (mostly natural, and the enemy to a lesser extent).
          • Signal for help.

     1-52. Different situations can alter the exact components needed for a survival equipment system, but it
     should include first aid materials and means to keep the Soldier dry, fed, and warmed. It should also provide
     the Soldier with a way to call or signal for help as covertly as possible. Survival kits and bags need to be as
     small and as unobtrusive as possible while still maintaining their utility.

Rapid Temperature Shifts

     1-53. Personnel should go indoors as often as the mission allows, but they should avoid rapid or extended
     warming around heaters; this can cause perspiration on the skin and condensation in the clothing. Perspiration
     will compromise the ability of cold weather clothing to retain body heat. Overly heated shelters will cause
     Soldiers to suffer rapid cool-off when it is time to step out of the shelter. Repeated rapid shifts in temperature
     with large variations can contribute to metabolic fatigue, weakening the immune system and possibly making
     Soldiers sick. The principle is that after removing only the outer layer of their ECWCS, the Soldier does not
     get so warm as to begin sweating (even slightly). Soldiers should stay cool even while avoiding the strenuous
     cold.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  9


---

Chapter 1

     1-54. Soldiers expecting to return to the cold after a heated shelter break should not spend extended time in
     the shelter. The purpose of a break shelter is—

          • Temperature control. The break shelter should be kept above freezing, but only moderately warm at
                about 35°F (2°C) to 40°F (4°C).

          • Ventilate built up moisture. Soldiers are encouraged to take off the outer layer of the cold weather
                system parka and trousers and the extreme cold jacket and trousers. Shake the garments to remove
                moisture.

          • Air out feet. Soldiers are encouraged to take off socks and boots for a moment to dry off the skin.
                Further, at least once during a work cycle lasting over six hours, Soldiers should apply foot powder
                and fresh socks (as the mission allows).

          • Check health. Battle buddies should check their partners for signs of cold injuries. Those Soldiers
                with previous cold injuries should have the area of injury rechecked. Then, a quick show of face,
                hands, and feet should be sufficient.

          • Eat and drink. After the Soldiers have changed into clean and dry clothing, then they can take a
                moment to rest, eat a light snack, and drink a warm liquid.

     1-55. Following this process in sub-freezing temperatures helps to keep Soldiers dry and acclimated to the
     cold. Where a tent or structural shelter is not available, the cab of a vehicle can serve the same purpose. The
     only requirement is that the cab must have sufficient room to perform the above five tasks. Medical personnel
     should be consulted about further precautions that might be included in this process.

Flesh on Cold Metal

     1-56. Personnel should avoid touching or leaning on cold-soaked equipment (equipment that has been
     standing in below freezing temperatures for an extended period) or kneeling and lying on the ground. Body
     fluids at the skin surface can directly freeze and even adhere to frozen metal. Also, rapid body cooling caused
     by heat transfer to the equipment or ground may result in deep tissue cold injury. There are several basic
     protections that Soldiers can use to avoid these injuries.

Gloves

     1-57. Soldiers must protect their hands and fingers when using with tools, calibration equipment, or sighting
     and fire control devices in freezing temperatures. Consider wearing gloves with liners, trigger finger shells
     with liners, or arctic mitten sets to keep hands warm. Standard issue light-duty leather gloves are appropriate
     around 35°F (2°C) and higher, but the arctic mitten set or trigger finger mittens should be worn at colder
     temperatures. The movement of nuts, bolts, knobs, and levers will be less smooth at colder temperatures and
     small parts are more likely to be dropped and lost in the snow. Operators who are not used to working with
     gloved hands will require time to adjust.

                                                 WARNING

                    Handwear may become wet and cause injuries to personnel.
                    Carry extra handwear when performing maintenance under arctic
                    conditions.

Anticontact Gloves

     1-58. Anticontact gloves should be worn when touching cold metal equipment. Many vehicle bodies, tools,
     knobs, and handwheels are difficult to manipulate while wearing arctic mittens, so it may be necessary for
     operators to wear anticontact gloves. These gloves are constructed with a lightweight material body and a
     durable deerskin leather reinforcement patch that covers the front side of the fingers, palm, and thumb. They
     are designed to provide enhanced dexterity compared to regular gloves. These gloves offer effective
     protection for a limited time, after which it is recommended to switch to mittens.

10  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

     1-59. If anticontact gloves are not available and it is necessary to use bare hands, the discomfort of contact
     with cold metal can be lessened by wrapping adhesive tape around the tools, knobs, or handwheels. Also,
     common tools like pliers should have handles wrapped in plastic or electrical tape to provide protection.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Anticontact gloves and work gloves are sufficient insulation against
                    touching dangerously cold metal. However, they are not suitable for
                    protecting hands against protracted cold exposure.

Insulating the Body for Work

     1-60. ECWCS clothing is the principal way for Soldiers to stay warm in extreme cold conditions. However,
     coveralls and other sustainment military occupational specialty (MOS)-specific personal protective
     equipment (PPE) that fit over a Soldier’s uniform in temperate areas may not fit over the higher levels of a
     bulky ECWCS clothing system. Soldiers often find that standard PPE garments mixed with ECWCS are
     usually not effective in cold. Mixing PPE with ECWCS together becomes constrictive, obstructing the
     Soldier’s movement and reducing the insulating ability of the ECWCS. It is risky for Soldiers to take off
     items of the ECWCS so that they can fit into their PPE. Without the ECWCS, Soldiers become vulnerable to
     the physical and health risks of the cold weather. Likewise, if Soldiers try to work while not dressed in their
     PPE, they risk injury or ruining the protective qualities of their ECWCS. Do not use any garments over MOS-
     specific PPE that negates its flame resistance; anti-static; petroleum, oils, and lubricants (POL) chemical
     resistance; and other protective properties.

     1-61. PPE is any clothing, accessories, equipment, devices, or substance that protects personnel from
     exposure to hazards that cause serious workplace injuries, illnesses, or damage to personal clothing and
     equipment. When the normal supply chain cannot provide approved PPE on time, the commander may need
     to consider procuring commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) or custom-made PPE items. Substitute PPE must
     reasonably conform to flame resistant, anti-static, electric non-conductivity, HAZMAT corrosion resistance,
     or other Soldier protection standards as it relates to the work being done. Ensure that cold weather PPE is
     sized large enough to fit over all layers of body armor and the cold weather clothing system that allow body
     humidity to air evaporate.

     1-62. Mechanics should place insulating material such as fiber packing material, corrugated cardboard, rags,
     or tarpaulins between themselves and cold-soaked equipment or frozen ground. Use a box or pan to hold
     small parts when performing maintenance under severe winter conditions. A tarpaulin should be placed under
     each vehicle to catch parts that may be dropped. This prevents the parts from becoming lost in the snow and
     further limits rapid body cooling from contact with the ground.

             Note. The Occupational Safety and Health Agency (known as OSHA) holds the employer
             responsible for the availability of PPE and how it is used, even if the employee buys it.

FUEL BURNING PERSONNEL HEATERS

     1-63. Field tent heaters become a safety risk if TM procedures are not followed in detail. Improperly set up
     or operated tent heaters can cause death or injury:

          • Improperly vented heaters that expel their fumes directly into tents may lead to carbon monoxide
                poisoning.

          • Unsafe heaters can cause fires.

     1-64. Fuel-burning heating systems have three input requirements and four byproducts. The basic inputs are
     fuel (with a hydrogen component), oxygen (or a solid oxidizer), and an initial heat catalyst. Hydrocarbons
     (like liquid fuel or a solid combustible like wood) and an oxidizer (usually fresh air, but possibly a solid) are
     used because they are stable at normal temperatures and do not need special handling in the field. The
     byproducts of fuel-burning heating systems are heat energy, water (as moisture), unburned carbon-based

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  11


---

Chapter 1

     byproducts, and carbon monoxide. The safety risk is not only in the fire, but also in the other byproducts
     (including water as moisture) that can damage equipment or jeopardize the health of Soldiers.

     1-65. There are four types of common heating systems:
          • Direct heat generation.
          • Single stage heat generation.
          • Two stage heat generation.
          • Three stage heat transfer system.

     1-66. The heating systems illustrated in Figure 1-3 are most common for Army tents and temporary shelters.
     Each of these heating systems has its own advantages and operational constraints. Maintenance personnel
     and operators should understand the principles of each heating system to achieve the best performance while
     minimizing safety risks.

                     Figure 1-3. Heat generation systems most used in warm shelters

Direct Heat

     1-67. Direct heat is also referred to as an open flame. This is the oldest and most common way to use a
     controlled heat source. An open flame is considered direct because the heat source is exposed and not fully
     contained within an enclosure. Direct heat is used to cook food, warm frozen brakes and other frozen parts,
     and to warm Soldiers in a shelter. Examples of direct heat equipment include an open hearth, pit, burner, or
     gas flow barrel to support the flame; a radiator bowl to direct the heat; and a flue to catch and vent the fire
     exhaust. Bunsen burners or blowtorches represent mechanically complicated direct heating devices.

     1-68. Occasionally, units purchase powerful kerosene or multi-fueled forced air “jet heaters” to keep the large
     spaces of tactical operations centers (and other shelters) as warm as possible. These heaters produce a strong
     blast of heated air, but they also pose a safety risk due to the release of exhaust, carbon monoxide, and
     moisture into the enclosed area of a tent or shelter.

                                                 WARNING

                    Do not use open-flame direct heat, forced air jet heaters, or other
                    non-electric direct heat heaters inside a shelter. They release too
                    much moisture, unburned carbon-based byproducts, and carbon
                    monoxide.

                    Exhaust of hydrocarbon fueled heaters in a contained area must
                    be vented by a smokestack, chimney, or exhaust piping.

12  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

Single-Stage Heat Generation

     1-69. This equipment is designed to fully contain the primary heat source, like a fireplace with a chimney, a
     Franklin stove, or a potbelly stove. The stove can withstand high levels of heat without being damaged, and
     the heat output can be controlled by regulating the flow of fuel and air into the fire. Flame byproducts are
     kept contained and deliberately channeled out of the enclosed area into the open air with this system, usually
     by the convection of rising heat through a stove pipe or chimney.

Two-Stage Heat Generation

     1-70. This system, also called a “duct heater,” is commonly used to heat large shelters. Shelter or room air is
     moved through the air ducts from the shelter to the heat source without directly contacting the exhaust of the
     combustion process. The air is heated in a heating chamber segregated from the combustion chamber and
     then returned to the shelter or room (referred to as 100% recirculation). For the proper operation of two-stage
     heating systems, the heating chamber must be able to keep the room air completely segregated from the fuel
     combustion and its byproducts. They can be complicated, cumbersome, and some military versions require
     a trailer. The large ones can also take a considerable amount of time to set up and dismantle.

Three-Stage Heat Generation

     1-71. A three-stage heat generation system (Figure 1-4) uses a liquid or fluid medium to transfer heat from
     the point of heat production to the place of heat radiation. This is the engineering principle most commonly
     used for heating the passenger compartment of vehicles where the engine is the heating source. In the cold,
     this heating method is often used to keep batteries and fluids of a motor warm enough to restart after a vehicle
     has been turned off and cooled.

                                  Figure 1-4. Three stage heat generating system

Creosote

     1-72. Depending on the type of solid or liquid fuel used, carbon byproducts can form a creosote buildup.
     Creosote soot is a flammable and corrosive substance that can build up on the walls of exhaust pipes and
     chimneys. Smoke is released by the initial fire, or primary combustion. Burning wood or fuel, no matter how
     it is done, releases pollutants in the form of gases and particulate matter. Creosote is formed when unburned
     particles, soot and other volatile gases combine as they exit the fire. As smoke cools while exiting the chimney
     or exhaust pipe, the gases liquefy, combine, and solidify to form creosote.

                                                 WARNING

                    When operating a heater in solid fuel mode, a buildup of creosote
                    can cause a fire inside the stack. Such fires can result in severe
                    injury or death.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  13


---

Chapter 1

     1-73. If there is too much wood fuel for the available oxygen, the wood will smolder and create more smoke
     and carbon monoxide than heat. Likewise with a poor draft, unburned particles and gases can condense and
     build up on the walls of the exhaust, in the flue, or on the walls of the shelter. Restricted air supply,
     unseasoned or rain-soaked wood, and cool surface flue all encourage creosote to build up. Over time, creosote
     soot buildup can become a danger and possibly result in a chimney fire. Creosote buildup is the primary
     cause or a major contributing factor in over 70% of heating fires.

     1-74. Units should treat heaters like any vehicle or weapon system in terms of tracking weekly maintenance
     in the months leading up to winter weather. This will involve operators performing and reporting weekly
     PMCS of heating devices and conducting after-operation cleaning and maintenance. Heater equipment TMs
     or manufacturer’s manuals must be available to operators for each item.

Shelter and Heater Safety

     1-75. Operate all heaters and stoves in accordance with the applicable TM or owner’s manual. Ensure fire
     extinguishers are present and fireguards are posted. Before the onset of cold weather or entering a severely
     cold environment, leaders are responsible for cold weather planning, obtaining COTS fuel-burning personnel
     heating systems, performing risk assessments on all heater assets, defining operating procedures, and
     providing Soldier training. Soldiers must be licensed or certified to operate field shelter heaters.

     1-76. Follow the guidance of Department of Defense (DOD) regulations and Occupational Safety and Health
     Agency on limitations of personnel exposure to hazardous substances. From the practical standpoint of
     controlling health hazards, the critical contaminants are carbon monoxide, ammonia, nitrogen oxides, sulfur
     dioxide, and aldehydes (methane). In sufficient concentrations, these substances may incapacitate personnel
     or substantially reduce their performance through eye irritation, nausea, headache, reduced mental alertness,
     unconsciousness, and death.

                                                  DANGER

                    Heaters pose a risk of fire and carbon monoxide poisoning that can
                    cause injury and death.

     1-77. Exposure to carbon monoxide is more common during the cold months when doors and windows of
     buildings are closed to preserve heat. Vehicle and motor exhaust in garages and shops are a common source
     of carbon monoxide exposure. Furnaces and stoves that are improperly fired or are in poorly ventilated areas
     present a health hazard. Oxygen-starved flames of heating devices produce a greater amount of carbon
     monoxide than a well-ventilated heater. Excessive carbon monoxide buildup can occur in heaters that have
     chimney soot (creosote) buildup, improper ventilation, or are not fully burning the heating fuel. While
     working in sheltered maintenance areas, pay attention to room ventilation and the piping used to funnel
     exhaust out of the working area.

     1-78. The type and degree of ventilation control required must be adjusted for the specific operation and
     location. If only one small engine is operated for a short period of time in a large room, natural ventilation
     may be adequate to prevent an excessive carbon monoxide concentration. However, if three or four engines
     are operated in the same room, the amount of carbon monoxide in the atmosphere may rise to a dangerous
     level. Active mechanical exhaust ventilation may have to be installed if passive ventilation is not adequate
     due to the size of the area or the number of engines being operated. Flex hoses or tailpipe extensions are a
     common control in maintenance shops.

     1-79. Carbon monoxide results from the incomplete combustion of carbon-based fuels or other organic
     matter. When the smoke cools, carbon monoxide settles to ground level. As a Soldier inhales, this gas
     suffocates a person by obstructing the blood’s ability to carry oxygen through the body. Always read and
     follow the TM and follow these safety tips when using fuel-burning heaters and stoves:

          • Exhaust must be vented to the outside of an enclosed area.
          • Gasoline should never be used in any heater.
          • Do not use wood, paper or any solid fuel in heaters or stoves not equipped with an ember trap screen

                at the end of the exhaust pipe. Such embers will burn fabric, damaging tents and clothing.

14         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                 Cold and Arctic Weather Maintenance

          • Render unusable any military-issue shelter heater or stove that is lacking an exhaust pipe capable of
                venting fumes to the outside of the personnel space.

          • Do not use any type of fuel-burning heater in a shelter that vents its exhaust fumes into the shelter.
          • For those shelters (or tents) heated by combustible fuel heaters and stoves, a fully dressed and alert

                fireguard must be on duty before anyone is allowed to sleep in the shelter.
          • Provide proper ventilation in heated shelters to avoid the danger of carbon monoxide poisoning

                caused by exhaust gases from running engines and contaminated hot air from defective heaters.
          • Control loose stove pipe opening flaps to prevent contact with hot stove pipes. This is a leading

                cause of tent fires.
          • Ensure all fuel spillage control measures are in place before using a liquid fuel-burning heater.
          • For safe operation, be sure to allow at least two feet of space between the heater and the shelter wall.
          • Do not use any type of accelerant (gas, kerosene, jet fuel, or others) to help ignite solid fuel.

                Explosion or uncontrolled fire may result. Tinder should be used to ignite solid fuel or wood.
          • Do not use unauthorized fuels. Use only approved liquid and solid fuels. Using unauthorized fuels

                in a heater will create a fire hazard and the potential for an explosion.
          • Never use open flame direct heat or forced air heaters inside a shelter or tent. Heater exhaust in a

                contained area must be vented through a flue, chimney, or duct.
          • Stoves lacking the convection to draw enough oxygen into the flame cause an incomplete burn of

                hydrocarbons. These byproducts increase the levels of carbon monoxide.
          • If the fuel flow control valve assembly bracket is bent or improperly positioned, a fuel overflow

                could occur inside burner assembly and cause a fire or explosion. Deadline the heater and notify
                field level maintenance.
          • The stack assembly (exhaust pipe) should be cleaned daily when operating with solid fuel to prevent
                creosote buildup.
          • Heater components may warp from excessive heat caused by an over fueled fire. Wood and coal
                can burn extremely hot depending on the type and size of fuel used. If coal is being used as fuel,
                add only a small amount of coal at a time. Coal is very dense and provides high heat output.
                Overfilling the heater with coal will cause the heater to run extremely hot and it will be very difficult
                to control the heater’s temperature output.
          • Do not over-fuel wood or coal burning fires. Clean ashes and creosote frequently. Over-fueled or
                excessive soot buildup in heaters can cause an oxygen starved flame. This will result in a high carbon
                monoxide release with little heat. Eventually, it will lead to a flame out and the sudden release of
                excessive smoke. Operators should plan on shutting down a solid-fueled heater once every two to
                four hours to remove ash.
          • Fireguards of tent heaters (direct heat or single stage stove heaters) should have two heat protective
                gloves (of the type used to swap barrels on a machine gun) and a fire extinguisher with the heater.
                Inventory heaters and order sufficient supplemental items; at least one set for each heater.
          • Do not attempt to replenish the fuel supply while operating a heater.
          • When lighting or refueling an in-shelter heater, all personnel in the tent must be awake and prepared
                for emergency exit. A second fireguard must be standing by with a fire extinguisher at the ready.
          • Never re-light a heater when it is still hot. Be sure to allow the heater to cool completely before
                attempting to re light.
          • The use of propane-fueled lanterns during harsh cold weather operations is not recommended.
                Propane turns to liquid at approximately -44°F (-42°C). It may spray in a liquid state from its
                container when the valve is opened and create an extremely hazardous condition.

MAINTENANCE FACILITIES

     1-80. Operational variables and unit resources will always be the driving factor in any planning
     considerations. Where applicable, prior planning considerations for maintenance facilities should seek to—

          • Ensure that tents and supporting frames are complete and in good repair.
          • Ensure that shelter heaters are complete and operational.
          • Ensure that generators are complete and operational.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  15


---

Chapter 1

          • Obtain oversized ground covers to keep moisture from rising within the maintenance shelters.
          • Ensure that there are adequate personnel who are licensed to operate and repair heaters and

                generators.

     1-81. The availability of maintenance facilities, heated buildings, or shelters can be critical to the
     maintenance mission. Human engineering standards for facilities, and workspaces for Soldier use are set by
     MIL-STD-1472. Proper servicing is difficult unless personnel are working in reasonably comfortable
     temperatures. Maintenance in the field will require gloves that will impede repairs that require careful and
     precise servicing. A shortage of heated shelters will cause an increase in maintenance man-hours needed for
     any task. Without some type of permanent or temporary shelter, even routine maintenance in the open can
     become extremely difficult.

     1-82. Maintenance tents are a good temporary solution when buildings are not available. If possible, tents
     should have wood flooring and be heated by portable duct heaters or tent stoves. Tarpaulins or parachutes
     may be used to create overhead shelter and windbreaks. A framework of poles erected around a vehicle can
     support a tarpaulin. A parachute could be deployed over the vehicle, securely staked down at the bottom, and
     then inflated by the air from a portable duct heater. Ensure proper ventilation and take extreme care to avoid
     the danger of carbon monoxide poisoning from running engines or direct heat devices. Do not overheat the
     shelter as the frozen ground will thaw and may cause equipment to shift unexpectedly or get stuck in the mud
     and then freeze again, making it difficult to move equipment on its own power.

COMMERCIAL OFF-THE-SHELF EQUIPMENT

     1-83. In situations where a unit must deploy into an environment for which military-issued equipment is
     insufficient, the commander may feel compelled to spend discretionary funds to procure specialized items.
     Non-military equipment that is purchased locally is commonly known as COTS equipment. While COTS
     heaters and stoves may seem to be a good solution for heating problems in the field, Soldiers must be trained
     on proper operating procedures before using them. Although Army policy does not limit the use of COTS
     heaters, few COTS heaters on the market meet the Army’s demands for field environments.

     1-84. Military shelters and military-issued heaters are designed and have been tested to complement each
     other. Propane COTS heaters, though noted for their low carbon monoxide emissions, still produce a large
     quantity of water vapor that can contribute to condensation. Furthermore, propane gas may not be readily
     available in the area of operations.

     1-85. If COTS items are authorized to supplement Army equipment, a maintenance supervisor and medical
     person who is familiar with carbon monoxide should assess their proper use, preventive maintenance, and
     lifecycle upkeep. COTS items must also be included in the unit PMCS schedule. Planners must consider the
     availability of the fuel used to power COTS items in the area where the unit will be deployed and operating.
     Though COTS items might be readily maintained in home station garrison, it is important to ascertain that
     they can be serviced and will function during deployment.

     1-86. Soldiers who are specialized in their jobs usually have a pretty good idea when modified personnel
     gear or COTS PPE items are needed. Occupational Safety and Health Agency places the responsibility on
     employers to ensure that PPE is available and used correctly, even if it is purchased by the employee. Soldiers
     are advised to seek guidance from their leaders before buying their own PPE.

     1-87. Avoid buying various items for a specific type of function from different manufacturers. This will cause
     maintenance and supply lifecycle support problems. It will mean that replacement parts will have to be
     obtained from different sources and will not be interchangeable or compatible.

     1-88. Daylight will become limited during the winter in northern latitudes. Additional lighting equipment is
     usually required for maintenance services. Lights with ample power generation, cable extensions, attachment
     plugs, connectors, and spare bulbs are essential.

     1-89. Extra heaters may be needed when equipment including brake pads, weapon bolts, or similar moving
     parts becomes seized up with moisture. For outdoor work, consider using a COTS portable forced air heater.

16  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                 Chapter 2

                         Equipment Maintenance

    This chapter contains specific information about the performance of materials and component
    parts during equipment operation and maintenance in cold environments.

MAINTENANCE CONSIDERATIONS

     2-1. Cold weather will demand more from equipment and maintainers. Routine maintenance may be
     extremely difficult or in some situations impossible. The cold can cause problems with equipment not
     normally encountered during operations in more moderate or temperate weather conditions. The importance
     of maintenance, especially the correct performance of PMCS, must be emphasized for all Soldiers in cold
     regions. Daily maintenance in a cold weather environment requires forethought; it should not be taken for
     granted and done by routine. Take extra care during before-operations checks; this is the critical point for
     identifying and fixing potential issues before any damage occurs. Operators must anticipate that additional
     time will be required when performing PMCS in cold weather.

     2-2. TMs and LOs are the primary sources of reference for maintenance procedures. However, in cold
     weather environments, unique maintenance requirements can arise that are not covered in these manuals.
     Commanders need to first establish policy for when and how Soldiers may resort to an alternate procedure
     or solution. This will require selecting a maintainer subject matter expert who can assess when a specific and
     unique situation or event exceeds or is outside of published standard procedures. The use of an alternative
     procedure or solution must be based on the likelihood of a risk to Soldiers or equipment that is not addressed
     in TMs and other publications. Never use a non-TM solution just to simplify procedures or perform makeshift
     repairs when the requisite resources are available.

GENERAL EFFECTS OF COLD ON ITEM COMPOSITION

     2-3. The physical properties and functional characteristics of materials can change as they reach freezing
     temperatures. Maintainers and operators must anticipate the effects of cold on equipment and its components,
     as well as the resulting increase in manhours and maintenance challenges.

     2-4. Three general categories of issues specific to cold weather account for most maintenance issues:
          • Cold alters material properties. Operators must be very cautious when using severely cold
                equipment that has been inactive for a long time. For example, the contraction of metals at lower
                temperatures and expansion at higher temperatures may result in improper clearances between
                components and cause binding or excessive looseness. Many items become brittle in the cold and
                could break if forced.
          • Petroleum, oils, and lubricants can degrade under extremely cold conditions. If the cold leads to the
                congealing of lubricants in mechanical components, it can result in friction and eventual failure.
          • Condensation freezing. This subject is addressed later in the chapter.

Metals

     2-5. Metals become brittle in cold temperatures and cannot withstand the shock loads that they sustain at
     higher temperatures. For example, at -20°F (-29°C) certain steels can withstand only 50% of the shock load
     that they can sustain at room temperature. For a given change in temperature, various metals will expand or
     contract by different amounts. These characteristics specially affect bearings in which the bearings and shaft
     are of different metals bolted together, or the meshing gears are of different metals. In cold weather, special
     care should be taken in adjusting parts of this type, especially when adjusting bearing clearances.

Welding

     2-6. Welding and metalworking are very technical processes and much more complicated when performed
     in adverse weather conditions like freezing to subzero temperatures. In these environments, welding requires

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  17


---

Chapter 2

     additional equipment, protection, and high levels of experience. Welders must consider several factors before,
     during, and after a welding task, including—

          • Metal thickness.
          • Welding machine capabilities and limitations.
          • Moisture in metals and welding materials.
          • Welding method.
          • Preheating techniques.
          • Proper cooling techniques.

     2-7. Welding and metalworking are essential for regular equipment maintenance and are fundamental to
     battle damage assessment and repair (refer to ATP 4-31 for more information). Welders and machinists must
     consistently practice various welding methods, techniques, and processes in controlled or ideal environments
     to improve and maintain a high level of proficiency.

     2-8. Water in all forms (liquid, condensation, moisture) poses a significant risk to the electronic components
     of welding machines and many metalworking devices. High-quality equipment and devices are more resistant
     to weather conditions and do not require cumbersome protection. Pay special attention to protecting welding
     equipment; parts may not be readily available.

Compressed Gas (Cylinders)

     2-9. The Army uses compressed gas for maintenance activities, medical requirements, and various
     applications in different fields. Cold weather can have a significant effect on the metal cylinders, regulators,
     hoses, and auxiliary instruments associated with the use of gas cylinders.

Oxygen

     2-10. Liquid oxygen is a transparent liquid. Oxygen can be made into a liquid through pressurization, which
     involves lowering the temperature below -297°F (-183°C) where the molecules slow down and condense into
     a liquid state. This process is also known as liquefaction. The extreme temperatures in the Arctic region do
     not affect the quality of the oxygen; however, they do affect the condition of the container and metering
     devices. For general and industrial use, liquid oxygen is stored in a green cylinder bottle. When the pressure
