     inside oxygen cylinders drops to 50 pounds per square inch (psi) or below, consider them empty. This
     prevents air with water vapor from entering the cylinder, which could cause corrosion and potentially lead to
     ice formation in the metering devices. Store oxygen bottles in a protected storage and according to local and
     federal rules.

Nitrogen

     2-11. Nitrogen is used in many applications within the Army maintenance operations. Nitrogen gas is used
     in metal shops for various cutting or welding processes. It is also used to charge heavy equipment suspension
     systems, for purging the gas of optic devices and equipment, and for maintaining environmental control units.
     However, when handling nitrogen in cold environments, it is important to take certain precautions to ensure
     safety and prevent accidents. Here are some tips for nitrogen safe handling in cold environments:

          • Always wear appropriate PPE, including insulated gloves, face shields, and safety goggles.
          • Ensure proper ventilation in the work area where nitrogen is being used. Nitrogen can displace

                oxygen and lead to a lack of oxygen in the air, which can be dangerous to personnel.
          • Be aware of the potential for cold burns when handling nitrogen in cold environments. Prolonged

                exposure to extremely cold temperatures can cause frostbite or other injuries.
          • Never allow liquid nitrogen to touch the skin, eyes, or clothing. Nitrogen can cause severe burns

                and other injuries upon contact.
          • Train all personnel who handle nitrogen in cold environments on proper safety procedures and

                protocols. Regular training and refresher courses can help ensure that everyone is aware of the risks
                and knows how to handle nitrogen safely.

18  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

Acetylene

     2-12. Acetylene is a highly flammable gas that is commonly used in welding and cutting applications. When
     using acetylene in cold weather, it is important to take certain precautions to ensure safety and prevent
     accidents. Here are some tips for using and caring for acetylene in cold weather:

          • Always store acetylene cylinders in a well-ventilated area away from open flames, sparks, and other
                sources of ignition. In cold weather, ensure that the cylinders are not exposed to freezing
                temperatures; this can cause the cylinder’s pressure to drop and make it difficult to use.

          • Before using acetylene in cold weather, ensure that the cylinder and regulator are free of ice and
                snow. Ice and snow can interfere with the cylinder's pressure and cause leaks or other hazards.

          • Use a hose that is designed for cold weather use. Cold weather hoses are typically made of materials
                that are less likely to become stiff or brittle in low temperatures.

          • When using acetylene in cold weather, be aware of the potential for condensation to form inside the
                hose. Condensation can mix with the acetylene gas and create a flammable mixture that can ignite
                or explode. To prevent condensation from forming, use a hose with a water trap or a check valve.

          • When transporting acetylene cylinders in cold weather, ensure that they are properly secured and
                protected from freezing temperatures. This can help prevent damage to the cylinder and ensure that
                it is safe to use.

          • Always follow proper safety procedures when using acetylene, including wearing appropriate PPE
                and ensuring proper ventilation. In cold weather, it may be necessary to use additional heating
                equipment to maintain a safe working environment.

Rubber

     2-13. Besides natural rubber, there are hundreds of rubber substitutes. Synthetic rubbers look and usually
     react the same as natural rubber, although most do not become more flexible at high temperatures. Natural
     rubber gradually stiffens as it cools, but retains a large part of its elasticity until reaching temperatures below
     -20°F (-29°C).

     2-14. When rubber cools, it remains flexible until it reaches approximately -60°F (-51°C). At this point, it
     quickly loses its elasticity and becomes brittle. Additionally, if rubber consistently stays at a temperature just
     under -20°F (-29°C) for an extended period, it undergoes a crystallization effect causing it to become brittle.
     For example, fuel hoses may crack, increasing the potential for spills.

Rubber-Covered Electric Cables

     2-15. Take extreme care handling electric cables at low temperatures. If the rubber jackets become hard, the
     cables must be protected from shock loads and bending to prevent short circuits caused by breaks in the
     covering. If cables are to be bent, they must first be warmed. Neoprene jackets on cables become brittle and
     break easily at low temperatures.

Plastics

     2-16. Plastics expand and contract much more than metal or glass, causing them to be brittle in cold weather.
     Vehicular canvas covers with plastic windows may break due to a combination of cold and vibration. This
     has an impact on the reliability of electrical components supported on plastic. In cold, operators should be
     extra careful not to drop or rough handle equipment with plastic components.

     2-17. In dry cold environments (or colder), operators should be conscious that plastics have the potential to
     build up static charges on surfaces due to lower humidity. An increased possibility of static charge can cause
     potential hazards in explosive gas environments and potential problems with electronics and other electrical
     equipment. Additionally, static can cause devices including analog meters with plastic faces to give incorrect
     or erratic readings and can also affect the operation of sensitive electronic devices.

Glass

     2-18. Glass, porcelain, and other ceramics perform normally at low temperatures if handled carefully.
     However, cracking may result if windshields or other vehicle glass is heated too rapidly.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  19


---

Chapter 2

                                           CAUTION

           Splashing warm water on frozen (cold-soaked) glass to clear a frosted
           windshield can shatter glass. Use warm air to defrost glass.

Fabrics

     2-19. If kept dry, fabrics generally retain their flexibility, even at extremely low temperatures. However,
     tarpaulins may shrink, and wrinkles and are extremely difficult to smooth out at below freezing temperatures.
     Whenever possible, tarpaulins should be unfolded in heated enclosures or kept installed on equipment.

EQUIPMENT WINTERIZATION

     2-20. Almost all equipment procured by the Army functions correctly at the -25°F (-32°C) threshold, but it
     usually begins to malfunction at temperatures below -30°F (-34°C). The drop in temperature directly relates
     to a decline in materiel performance. Proper equipment winterization can mitigate some of these issues and
     help ensure proper operation in cold weather. Low temperatures should be anticipated far enough in advance
     to permit time to acquire and install winterization parts.

     2-21. Winterization is a specific set of tasks listed in the equipment TM that prepare equipment for cold
     weather operations. There are other winterization techniques in different areas of this publication, but the
     following are some typical winterization techniques and tasks:

          • Ensure that the equipment has a proper antifreeze/water mixture. Generally, a mix of 60% antifreeze
                and 40% distilled water provides the best protection against cold temperatures.

          • Inspect all belts and hoses for cracks, dry rot, or breaks and replace as necessary.
          • Install winterfronts, grille covers, or radiator shutters. These devices are installed on the equipment

                grille at the front of the radiator to help the engine retain heat at the optimum operating temperature.
          • Ensure that the vehicle personnel heater is mounted and operational.
          • Ensure that tire chains, engine heaters, ice scrapers, and other equipment required for operation in

                cold/icy/deep snow conditions are present, and that vehicle crews are thoroughly trained in their
                use.
          • Pre-fit tire chains or track cleats to vehicles, ensuring that kits are complete and in good condition.
                Their mounting and dismounting should be trained as a crew drill.
          • Install auxiliary engine block heaters.
          • Install solar battery chargers to maintain battery charge and prevent premature unserviceability.

             Note: Before cold weather operation of vehicles, crews should use the appropriate equipment TM
             and LO for operation, lubrication, PMCS, and maintenance. These manuals usually include a
             section titled Operations under Unusual Conditions. The following information in this subsection
             is not a substitute for the equipment TM.

ENGINE BELTS

     2-22. Rubber belts lengthen in severe deep cold. Operators must check belt tension by regularly looking for
     loose belts or listening for squealing when the engine is running.

TIRES

     2-23. Tires are made of multiple materials that each have their own properties. Tires may develop flat spots
     and become more rigid when parked in cold temperatures. These cold flats typically diminish as tires warm
     up with use. Adjust tire pressure as needed. Inflate tires to 10 psi above normal pressure in a warm
     environment such as a sheltered parking garage or shop. This allows for air contraction and pressure loss
     once the vehicle is out in the cold.

     2-24. In extreme cold, rubber expands while metal contracts; this could break tire seals. Tread rubber,
     especially on non-winter-rated tires, can lose grip and become stiff on winter surfaces at colder temperatures.

20         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

     Place barrier materials (branches, cardboard, or such similar material) under tires to prevent them from
     freezing to the ground when parked for long periods of time.

BATTERIES

     2-25. Cold weather can adversely affect batteries:
          • Battery power capacity is decreased at low temperatures.
          • Cold temperatures increase the vehicle power demands that the battery must provide.
          • Frozen vehicle batteries can burn or explode if the operator attempts to slave start or charge the
                vehicle.

     2-26. Ensure batteries are fully charged; they can freeze when partially or completely discharged. A fully
     charged battery can withstand -70°F (-57°C) to about -77°F (-60°C) without freezing. Use float chargers to
     ensure that a rechargeable battery maintains an adequate state of charge (SOC) for its given application.

             Note. Batteries are covered in greater detail in Chapter 3.

FUELS

     2-27. Most fuels react the same to the cold and are usually distinguished by the temperatures at which
     breakdown occurs. The following are some known facts about the effects of cold on fuel:

          • Jet propellant (JP), JP-8, under the single fuel policy (SFP), is usable down to -53°F (-47°C).
          • Motor gasoline is not significantly affected by cold temperatures.
          • Clouding (formation of waxes) occurs in diesel fuel at low temperature.
          • Additives can greatly inhibit icing and fuel line freezing.
          • The pumping, filtering, and tank filling of petroleum products, particularly refined distillates, can

                cause the generation and accumulation of electrostatic charges. Be familiar with static control safety
                procedures specified in the item TM or fuel handling procedures as appropriate.

             Note. Fuels are covered in greater detail in Chapter 4 and Appendix D.

LUBRICANTS

     2-28. The POL Products Guide for Ground Vehicle and Equipment Materiel Systems contains information
     on Army lubricants and serves as a quick reference for military personnel to correctly use petroleum and
     related products in the supply system. This guidance covers essential fuels, fuel additives, lubricants, and
     associated products that are required and used in DOD ground vehicles, equipment, and materiel. The Army
     qualifies heavy duty diesel engine oils against the requirements of specification MIL-PRF-32626.

     2-29. Transmissions are designed (in general) to use lower viscosity oils. The Society of Automotive
     Engineers (SAE) -10W oils are typically not recommended for engine applications but make good
     transmission fluids. Most MIL-PRF-32626 oils can be used at temperatures up to a high of 120°F (49°C).
     Use lubricant, arctic weight (LAW) in low temperatures for the lubrication and preservation of small arms
     and light caliber weapons up to 20mm. Lubricants should be stored in a warm place.

             Note. Lubricants are covered in greater detail in Chapter 4.

LOW OR NO OIL PRESSURE

     2-30. The two most common failures of the engine lubrication system are caused by low or no oil pressure,
     and the accumulation of sludge in the lubricating oil. Even though the pressure gauge indicates low or no oil
     pressure, a stick check may indicate the oil is at the correct mark. Here, fuel diluted oil, hot oil, or low
     viscosity oil may have caused a low oil pressure reading. No oil pressure may be caused by cold, congealed,
     high viscosity oil; a clogged strainer; a defective oil pump; the lack of lubricant; or engine component failure
     (like an engine bearing). In either case, the problem is serious and not resolvable by simply adding oil.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  21


---

Chapter 2

             Note. Do not overlook the possibility that the oil pressure gauge may be defective. If the gauge is
             working correctly, the oil is up to the full mark, and the oil filter element is not clogged, the failure
             is probably in the pump or line.

VEHICLE STARTING

     2-31. Starting a vehicle in cold weather is not the same as in a temperate weather. The following tips will
     help ensure personnel safety and optimum equipment performance:

          • Always PMCS vehicles using the TM before, during, and after vehicle operation. It is critical that
                operators must follow PMCS procedures and steps. Starting the engine is not the first step in PMCS.

          • Be ready to draw additional power from a supplemental energy source.
          • Start engine and allow vehicle to idle for approximately 20 to 30 minutes before moving.
          • Depress brake pedal and slowly cycle through transmission and transfer gears before moving the

                vehicle.
          • Drive slowly at first, allowing time for moving parts to reach operating temperatures before

                increasing engine speed.
          • Downgrade all hoist and winch capabilities. Certain metals will lose up to half of their shock load

                tensile strength at temperatures of -20°F (-29°C) or colder.
          • Ensure that the engine idle is set for proper battery recharging. This is usually done by engaging the

                equipment high idle switch. This usually brings the engine idle up to about 1100 to 1200 revolutions
                per minute (RPM).
          • The idle RPM may need to be increased on certain vehicles like the command or communications
                vehicle. High idling is done to produce enough electric power to run the engine while overcoming
                cold-induced resistance and still providing enough surplus power to recharge the electricity the
                battery spends to start and run the engine.

                                                 CAUTION

                    DO NOT engage high idle or excessively increase engine RPM before
                    engine is warm. Damage to engine or transmission may occur.

MOISTURE AND CONDENSATION

     2-32. Condensation is the process of moisture liquidizing within warm air when the air contacts a relatively
     colder item. It is a very common threat to equipment serviceability and even Soldier health in cold
     environments. Condensation is often called, “sweating” due to its appearance. This condition affects almost
     every type of material, but the effect on each item of material is varied and unique.

CAUSES AND SOURCES OF CONDENSATION

     2-33. The most common cause of condensation occurs when a cold-soaked item of equipment is brought into
     an above-freezing environment. When warm, humid air contacts cold equipment, the moisture in the air will
     condense on the surfaces, especially in hidden areas and between moving parts. When the equipment is
     returned to a cold environment, the condensed water freezes, leading to multiple issues and potential damage.

     2-34. There are two common ways to avoid the problems of freezing condensation on mechanical items:
          • The easiest method is to keep mechanical items (including weapons) outside in the freezing cold
                and avoid bringing them into a warm shelter.
          • The second solution is to warm the item to a temperature that is equal to or warmer than the shelter
                (for example, by using anti-condensation containers or hot air blowers), and then wiping them off
                before taking them back into the freezing cold. Allowing a metal item to sweat while it warms only
                encourages corrosion, rust, or oxidation to occur.

     2-35. When a cold-soaked item is used indoors, the moisture combines with the lubricants to form an
     emulsion. The emulsion then weakens the protective properties of the item. To address corrupted lubricant,

22         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

     it is necessary to completely remove all the contaminated lubricant and then perform a detailed cleaning
     process that often entails using a degreasing solvent, like type two [SD-2]. If the item is taken outside into
     low temperatures before the condensed moisture is removed or has evaporated, the parts may freeze and
     become inoperable.

Propane-Burning Heaters

     2-36. One of the most frequently overlooked causes of high humidity and condensation is open flame. When
     a fire burns, it produces a significant amount of water as a byproduct, contributing to increased humidity
     levels in the surrounding environment. Propane and natural gas produce a significant amount of moisture for
     their weight due to their molecular composition.

     2-37. In the outdoors, propane-thermal heaters and forced-air heaters are excellent for loosening frozen
     brakes or other mechanical components. However, in a tent or cold-soaked vehicle compartment, these fire
     sources can create an ice buildup on the walls. Twenty pounds of propane can produce almost three gallons
     of moisture. Ventilation is important to wick away moisture any time direct heating is done in an enclosed
     area.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Most direct heat sources, especially propane and natural gas heaters
                    discharge large quantities of moisture into the air. In cold-soaked vehicle
                    cabs, this can cause sweating on the walls, fogging of windows, and
                    water condensation to form within electrical equipment.

Breath or Perspiration

     2-38. Condensation can result from overheated people placed in a confined area. When enemy contact is
     imminent, the interior temperature of transportation platform troop compartments (especially aircraft) should
     be maintained close to freezing (32°F) [0°C]). This prevents troops dressed in heavy cold region uniforms
     from overheating. It also prevents moisture from condensing and refreezing on weapons as troops disembark
     into the cold from warm aircraft or vehicles.

     2-39. Soldiers and aircraft crews must exercise caution when loading Soldiers after periods of heavy exertion.
     Sweat buildup and body heat of Soldiers loaded on an aircraft can cause extreme and rapid fogging of the
     aircraft’s windshields, blocking pilot vision. Soldiers should focus on ventilating their uniforms and minimize
     body heat buildup. They can achieve this by loosening clothing and ensuring proper airflow.

     2-40. There is a significant risk of condensation that commonly occurs in vehicles and small tents. The walls
     of uninsulated heated crew compartments can become coated with condensation that is caused by the
     moisture from people breathing. Frosting from the breath of the operators can render microphones inoperable.

     2-41. It is important to keep doors, windows, or hatches open to allow for effective ventilation in the crew
     compartment and help moderate Soldiers’ body heat. Condensation in a crew compartment is a threat to not
     only the pilot or driver’s ability to see, but also a threat to electrical components in the crew cab.

     2-42. Condensation and ice from a Soldier breathing tends to form on a weapon during freezing temperatures.
     A combination of body warmth and breathing may cloud the optics of sights and fire control mechanisms.
     Moisture condensing on the eyepiece can render a fire control system useless. When a gunner initially tries
     to gain a sight picture, moisture from his breath and his body heat near the lens may cause condensation on
     the lens. Despite precautions, weapons parts may still freeze. If they do, slow and careful manual operation
     may free them and prevent breakage. Remember, plastic and metal become brittle in the freezing cold.

EFFECTS OF MOISTURE AND CONDENSATION

     2-43. Moisture and condensation may lead to malfunctions, corrosion, and decreased capability of equipment
     and weapon systems and affect munition efficiency. Combating moisture sources is paramount to preventing
     condensation.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  23


---

Chapter 2

Automotive Equipment

     2-44. In extremely cold conditions, the moisture in the air condenses and freezes into ice crystals that become
     suspended in fuel or POL products and degrade them. In the case of oils and lubricants, it can obstruct the
     free movement of equipment and lead to breakage. In the case of fuels, it can result in water contamination
     that causes an engine to stop running. Brush snow or wipe water from the tops of fuel and lubricant containers,
     spouts, and plugs before use to prevent contamination.

     2-45. Air compressors used in vehicle air brakes, air hydraulic brakes, and a central tire inflation system
     (CTIS) are especially prone to internal condensation. In the cold, moisture may freeze inside the compressor.
     Follow after-operations maintenance procedures by draining condensation from air compressor tanks. If the
     bleed valve is frozen, attempt to unfreeze it or report it immediately to maintenance personnel.

     2-46. Frozen moisture in an air brake or pneumatic system can significantly impact operation. Brake line, air
     brake filters, brake chambers, pushrods, valves, and seals are more susceptible to defects and failure in cold
     conditions. Condensation between brake shoes and brake drums may freeze, making it impossible for the
     vehicle to move. When this happens, portable heating equipment is needed to thaw the brake shoes from the
     drums or warm obstructed air passages.

     2-47. Low engine operating temperature results in excessive fuel consumption, dilution of engine oil by
     unburned fuel, and formation of sludge from condensation of water in cylinders and crankcases. This will
     degrade the useful life and effectiveness of engine lubricants and require more frequent oil changes.

Weapons

     2-48. When weapons are transferred from a cold environment to a warmer one, condensation forms on their
     surfaces due to the change in temperature. This occurs because the warmer air can hold more moisture,
     causing the moisture in the air to condense on the cooler surfaces of the weapons. Additionally, when these
     weapons are brought into heated shelters for cleaning purposes, the difference in temperature may cause the
     weapons to sweat (continue to produce condensation) for as long as an hour. When time permits, wait one
     hour, remove all condensation, and then clean the weapon. If weapons are taken back into the cold without
     removing the condensation, it can turn to ice and result in stoppages.

     2-49. Condensation on weapons poses a constant threat to their function in cold weather. It can lead to icing
     and corrosion on machined surfaces, raw metal surfaces, and especially in the bores of weapons. This type
     of condensation is often referred to as sweating, but it is not moisture coming out of the metal. When weapons,
     sighting and fire control materiel, parts, or assemblies are brought indoors after having been outside at low
     temperatures, water vapor in the warm air condenses on the cold parts. Any opening that allows air to enter
     will easily become a condensation problem area. Such condensation can freeze moving parts when the
     weapon is returned to the freezing cold. Condensation causes corrosion if not immediately removed.

     2-50. In extremely cold conditions, condensation can cause problems inside the weapon and lead to stoppages
     and malfunctions. Parts such as recoil and counterrecoil rods and variable recoil cams must be wiped dry and
     lubricated lightly with LAW every day.

     2-51. It is best to leave weapons outside when temperatures are below freezing. When left outside, weapons
     should be readily accessible, but sheltered, so that ice and snow will not get into the working parts of the
     weapon (sights, barrel).

Optical Devices

     2-52. A combination of body warmth and breathing may cloud the optics of sights and fire control
     mechanisms. For optical equipment, condensation can build up behind the lens and obstruct vision by
     streaking or frosting over. Special cleaning procedures reduce problems caused by condensation.

     2-53. Maintenance personnel should anticipate the buildup of condensation within optical devices that are
     not hermetically sealed or are leaking humid air. As part of field maintenance, personnel will be required to
     open lens assemblies, meticulously clean the glass, remove any corrosion, and reseal the assembly to ensure
     it remains free of internal moisture. If this cannot be done, supply personnel will have to arrange for exchange
     of the item.

24  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

     2-54. When storing fire control items, it is recommended to keep them outdoors in covered areas to shield
     them from snow. Store binoculars, telescopes, and other fire control equipment in snow-tight lockers or
     sealed containers issued with the materiel at outdoor temperatures. Leave aiming instruments outside or use
     anti-condensation containers to bring them into a heated shelter.

     2-55. When in the field, leave winterized cold-soaked weapons and ammunition outdoors and covered from
     precipitation (security is a separate concern). An alternative method is to place individual weapons between
     the tent and tent liner. This method allows for rapid access to the weapons, provides security, and still protects
     the weapon from excessive condensation.

     2-56. When storing equipment, it is important to avoid using plastic sheets or tarps as they tend to trap
     moisture and condensation against the equipment. Instead, use breathable cloth to cover or wrap up items as
     this will allow for better air circulation and prevent potential damage.

Munitions

     2-57. When preparing to fire a weapon, it is important to be aware that if there is condensation on a round
     that freezes in the breech, it could result in a misfire. At weapon ranges, do not bring ammunition into heated
     rooms for unpacking and distribution unless it has been given sufficient time to warm up and allow any
     condensation to evaporate. In a rapid ammunition distribution situation, it is better to keep the munitions cold
     or stored in dry cold storage.

     2-58. Taping over ends of detonation cord, caps, and igniters may prevent condensation contamination of an
     explosive charge’s firing system. Otherwise, moisture will travel through the exposed explosive materiel and
     degrade its performance.

Electronics

     2-59. Condensation damage is the primary threat to electronic equipment that is not sealed, waterproofed, or
     ruggedized. Condensation on electronic equipment occurs when a cold unit is brought into a warm area. The
     cold temperature of the equipment causes warm air moisture to condense on the item and its circuit wiring.
     Short circuiting may then happen when power is applied. Electrical equipment should be warmed to room
     temperature before being turned on. Even then, condensation may corrode the fine wires found on circuit
     boards causing intermittent failures.

             Note. Manufacturers of COTS electronics generally consider condensation damage to be post-
             manufacture damage and usually will not repair or replace items under warranty. Soldiers who
             carry non-ruggedized personal items (like cell phones) into cold weather environments are at
             personal financial risk. Commanders will need to weigh the risk to the unit when taking non-
             ruggedized COTS electronics into a cold weather environment.

ANTI-CONDENSATION CONTAINERS

     2-60. If it is necessary to bring weapons or other items from low outdoor temperatures into higher room
     temperatures, use anti-condensation containers to prevent condensation of moisture on metal and glass. These
     containers can be specially made boxes, covered cans, or other airtight containers with heat-conducting walls.
     Keep containers outside so that they are at the low outside temperature. When transitioning from a colder
     outdoor environment to a warmer indoor space, place items into the anti-condensation container and secure
     tightly. Once indoors, allow the container to reach the same temperature and equalize with the room’s
     temperature. Being near a stove can hasten a container’s warm-up. If the seal on a container is not airtight, it
     will not function as an anti-condensation container.

     2-61. Cold, dry air expands and presses outward when it is warmed. Therefore, no warm, humid air from the
     room can come in contact with items inside the container and cause condensation. When the items reach
     room temperature, they can be removed with no danger of condensation.

     2-62. If an anti-condensation container for an item is not available, wrap the item in blankets or similar
     materiel before it is brought into a heated enclosure. Airtight self-sealing plastic freezer bags make a good
     moisture barrier for small items. This will retard or impede the condensation process.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  25


---

Chapter 2

VEHICLE WINTERIZATION

     2-63. Special winterization equipment (provided as a part of specific kits) can protect both Soldiers and
     vehicles against cold weather. For vehicle protection when the temperature falls only a few degrees below
     freezing, only ordinary preparations are needed as specified by the TM. Though any temperature below
     freezing becomes an issue for vehicle fluids, most vehicle components are resistant to temperatures effects
     down to about 10°F (-12°C) for short periods of time.

     2-64. Human engineering standards for facilities, workspaces, and vehicles purchased by the Government
     for Soldier use are set forth by MIL-STD-1472. Vehicle crew compartments should have an adequate heating
     system that can maintain temperatures above 68°F (20°C) during vehicle occupancy. When arctic clothing is
     worn, cab heaters are expected to maintain temperature of not less than 41°F (5°C).

     2-65. Personnel heater kits and hardtop closures may be installed in anticipation of freezing temperatures.
     Do not attempt to conduct operations in areas where temperatures from -25°F (-32°C) to -65°F (-54°C) are
     likely without installing equipment winterization kits. Consult the appropriate TM for installing and using
     winterization kits.

VEHICLE CREW HEATERS

     2-66. In most wheeled vehicles, the crew compartment is heated by circulating antifreeze from the engine
     into the crew compartment heater in a three-stage heat transfer system. Generally, these vehicles heaters can
     effectively provide adequate heat until the ambient temperature drops below -25°F (-32°C). At extreme cold
     temperatures, the engine will struggle to generate enough heat to stay functional. Although arctic antifreeze
     solution remains liquid down to -90°F (-68°C), the crew compartment heater may not be effective in extreme
     cold. Electric or fuel-burning supplemental heaters (where available as a part of a winterization kit) are
     needed to provide more heat below -25°F (-32°C).

     2-67. Heavy equipment like tanks usually produce heat separately from the engine in a blower or turbine type
     burner module. These vehicles store more ambient cold temperature in their thick walls, so their heaters
     produce more heat than those of wheeled vehicles. The heat-producing burner modules can be mounted inside
     or outside of the crew compartment. If the burner is outside the crew compartment, it is a three-stage heating
     system. It may use either antifreeze or oil to transfer heat from the burner into a crew compartment heat
     radiator. If the burner is in the crew compartment it is a single stage heater system.

     2-68. Tracked vehicle operators operate and maintain the personnel heater system. Operators must ensure
     that soot, sludge, or creosote does not build up in the burner unit. Refer to the vehicle TM for PMCS and
     maintenance procedures before attempting to clean. For those vehicles with burner modules mounted in the
     crew compartment, operators must watch for leaking fuel feeds.

                                                  DANGER

                    Contact with burner modules can burn a person.

                                                 WARNING

                    Combustible fuel driven/powered personnel and crew cab heaters
                    should be shut off at least 20 minutes before bringing the vehicle
                    into a shelter. In the event of faulty heater components, this
                    procedure prevents the fire hazards created from accidental
                    pumping of fuel on building floors, or fuel vapors from hot heater
                    components.

26  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

ENGINE PREHEAT

     2-69. Starting a cold-soaked engine can be very difficult and may lead to damage. In temperatures
     below -20°F (-29°C), operators may need to start vehicle engines periodically to maintain an acceptable state
     of readiness. Periodic engine running should not be done if other preheating means are available, including
     engine heaters, external heat, or indoor parking. A properly winterized engine with some type of preheating
     component or device will stay ready for operation. There are four general strategies for keeping a vehicle
     engine warm enough for use:

          • Periodic or continuous engine running.
          • Warm shelter parking.
          • Electrical quick-heat systems.
          • Standby fuel-powered heaters.

     2-70. There are two major types of engine heaters: electrical and fuel-burning. These heaters are designed to
     warm up engines, drivetrain gears, batteries, and other essential components before or during the initial
     startup of equipment. These heaters can be as simple to operate as electrical oil-pan, heating pads, and as
     complex (and possibly dangerous) as fuel-powered heating devices. These devices circulate scolding hot
     antifreeze through a vehicle while it idles.

     2-71. Standby heaters are usually not a standard piece of equipment on a vehicle and are ordered as a part of
     a vehicle winterization kit.

             Note. Not all vehicles have military-designed engine heater systems as a part of a winterization
             kit. If the government-issued options are inadequate, notify the U.S. Army Tank Automotive and
             Armaments Command logistics assistance representative. Otherwise, the unit commander will
             have to decide about procuring COTS items.

Electrical Quick-Heat Systems

     2-72. Quick heat is a technique that uses electrical engine block heaters to heat the antifreeze or engine oil
     using external power. It allows quick warm-up and reliable starting when the engine block or transmission
     temperatures have dropped to as low as the cold ambient temperature. Prior to vehicle operation, electrical
     heat is applied to the engine, batteries, and components, raising their temperatures enough to ensure starting.
     Electrical quick heaters are dependent upon access to a facility’s power sources. Such electrical power
     sources would be easy to obtain in garrison but may be challenging to find in tactical situations.

     2-73. The time from initiating quick heat to achieving reliable starting temperatures can vary from a few
     minutes to one hour. These systems use heater elements inserted into the engine block or heating pads placed
     against the oil pan and battery to prepare the vehicle for starting. The goal is to achieve reliable starting within
     a maximum of one hour regardless of ambient temperature. There are several electromechanical devices for
     keeping a coolant from freezing in a vehicle.

Heater Harness

     2-74. A heater harness is a heavy appliance power cord attached to electrical heater systems. This cord is
     usually woven through the engine compartment to a point near the front fender. The heater cord typically
     ends with a standard three prong male connector, and only occasionally uses a polarized two prong. A
     protective cap is necessary for keeping the end plug connectors free of ice fouling or corrosion.

                                                WARNING:

                    Do not operate the engine unless the heater harness cord has
                    been disconnected from the power source and secured in place.
                    The heater harness power cord must be secured in its retaining
                    clips and routed away from exhaust manifolds and moving parts.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  27


---

Chapter 2

Battery Blankets or Battery Insulator

     2-75. These can range from an electric heating pad to a battery case equipped with a self-regulated,
     thermostat-controlled heating element designed to keep batteries warm. Chemical reactions within a battery
     release energy, and these reactions occur best within a warm battery. A battery blanket protects battery fluid
     from freezing.

Float charger

     2-76. This type of charger maintains a battery’s SOC in storage or when installed in an infrequently used
     vehicle and ensures the battery is charged to its rated capacity. Float chargers are critical in the bitter cold,
     ensuring that the vehicle or generator set engine will start. Only use float chargers designed to prevent battery
     over-charging. These chargers monitor the SOC and adjust the charging current automatically. If the charger
     detects a problem, it can prevent damage to the battery.

Engine Block Immersion Heater

     2-77. This is an electrical heating element that is mounted directly into the engine block through an engine’s
     core plug. The block mount can be threaded into the engine block or wedged into a core hole by using a
     compression gasket. There is no pump with this type of heater.

Inline Coolant Hose Heater

     2-78. The inline coolant hose heater consists of an electrical heating element installed along the hose
     connecting the engine and the radiator. Like the block heater, it is electrically powered. The circulating
     coolant inline heater heats coolant and circulates it through the engine by tapping the engine water jacket. It
     uses 400 to 1500 watts to heat antifreeze up to 135°F (57°C) to 175°F (80°C). The heater is spliced into the
     coolant hose (usually to the heater core hose returning to the engine) and uses a built-in pump to circulate
     coolant over the heating element and through the engine block antifreeze system. For vehicles, this means
     almost immediate crew cab heating.

Engine Oil Heater

     2-79. This electrical heating element is mounted to maintain contact with the engine oil or oil pan. This
     ensures that the engine oil is less viscous at startup, minimizing fuel and water accumulation in the crankcase.

Transmission Heaters

     2-80. These heaters keep transmission fluid thin enough (lowered viscosity) to avoid rupturing seals. They
     are usually electrical heater pads (like those used for oil heating) attached to the side of the transmission
     cowling that keep the transmission oil warm.

Heating Pad

     2-81. The simplest form of quick heating is provided by an electrical heating pad. These are pressed against
     the side of an oil pan or the side of a battery to keep them warmed.

Engine Standby Heaters

     2-82. Standby heaters are fuel-fired coolant heating units that run on the vehicle’s own fuel supply (internally
     powered heat source). This type of quick-heat system heater is becoming rare in newer military equipment.
     They are typically installed in the engine compartment and can heat and circulate coolant through the engine,
     a battery box heat exchanger, and even the crew compartment. After stopping a warm engine, the fuel-driven
     heater is started, and the auxiliary water circulation pump circulates warmed antifreeze through tubing into
     the engine and through a battery box heat exchanger. A hybrid of this heater system has an option for
     electrically powered heater coils to conserve on fuel when electrical power is available.

     2-83. Standby heating systems are designed to ensure that vehicle components do not fall below a certain
     minimum temperature. These systems use a small fuel-fired boiler and fuel-fed engine to pump antifreeze
     through the engine and also warm the transmission and battery. To ensure proper operation, the fuel-burning

28  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

     heater is continuously operated during the standby period. Continuous operation maintains engine and battery
     electrolyte temperatures at levels that provide adequate cranking and battery recharging potential.

     2-84. The standby system includes an engine coolant heater, hoses, control valves, fuel pump, battery heat
     exchanger (commonly known as a battery pad), and miscellaneous hardware. This system is not designed to
     start a vehicle engine after it has been off and the vehicle becomes cold-soaked for an extended period in
     extreme below freezing temperatures (-25°F [-32°C] or below). However, the standby system can help start
     an engine in an emergency if there is enough time and auxiliary power available.

Engine Pre-Startup Considerations

     2-85. Many winterization kits for starting below -25°F (-32°C) include a fuel-fired engine coolant standby
     heater. It is very important to understand the operating instructions of fuel-fired engine heaters before use.
     For example, the failure to turn on or ensure the auxiliary water circulation pump is running will most likely
     cause coolant hoses to burst.

     2-86. Once the engine is operating after starting, watch the temperature gauge to ensure that the temperature
     rises gradually. A sudden rise in temperature indicates either a frozen radiator, insufficient coolant, or an
     inoperative thermostat. If this occurs, stop the engine immediately and determine the cause.

     2-87. Technical problems with these heaters include—
          • Low voltage.
          • Contaminated fuel.
          • Already frozen components.
          • Inexperienced or inattentive operators and mechanics.

CHASSIS AND BODY COMPONENTS

     2-88. Though any temperature below freezing becomes an issue for vehicle fluids, most vehicles components
     are resistant to temperatures down to about 10°F (-12°C). Near that temperature, the tensile strength,
     brittleness, and thermal contraction of many materials will noticeably change a mechanical item’s
     performance properties.

     2-89. Ice, mud, and snow can build up to such an extent on vehicles that they overload vehicle components,
     reduce ground and other clearances, and prevent or interfere with the normal operation of moving
     components. Buildup and freezing of slush and water around the wheels of an operating vehicle can cause a
     loss of steering ability.

TIRES

     2-90. The expansion and contraction of tire rubber in response to temperature changes is important to note,
     as it is opposite to the behavior of metal rims. Tire rubber expands when exposed to cold temperatures, but
     it contracts in warmer environments. These temperature fluctuations directly impact tire pressure. Frequently
     monitor tire pressure to prevent issues like underinflation or overinflation; these directly affect vehicle
     handling and fuel efficiency. If radial tires are overinflated, the center of the tire will bow outward causing
     the tire edges to lose ground contact. If radial tires are underinflated, the center of the tire will bow inward
     causing the tires to run on their edges and lose ground contact in the center. In temperate weather, incorrect
     tire pressure causes uneven wear and shortens tire life; in icy or snow-covered roads, it can also lead to a life-
     threatening loss of traction.

     2-91. The air pressure in a tire will drop as temperatures cool. Increase tire pressure about 10 psi for overnight
     or extended parking to compensate for the cold effects on tires. Tires should be deflated to proper pressure
     before operations.

     2-92. Operators and maintainers need to pay attention to the matchup or dissimilarities between the tires and
     rims. It is possible for a tire (without a steel wire in the bead) and rim to break its seal at about -13°F (-25°C).
     If aluminum is in the mix of components, it contracts more than steel.

     2-93. Check valve stems and tighten cores. Replace cores if rubber seals are brittle or show wear. Examine
     tire casings for cuts, bruises, or breaks. Caps must be installed on all valve stems. At -50°F (-46°C), tires

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  29


---

Chapter 2

     become rigid enough to support a load at 0 pressure without apparent deflation. This is a problem because
     sidewalls become brittle and may crack.

     2-94. Using nitrogen gas to inflate tire is a good practice. Tires filled with nitrogen are not as prone to
     expansion and contraction as those filled with regular air.

     2-95. Winter-rated all-season tires are available on some tactical vehicles and should be used where
     appropriate. In some less severe conditions, the use of winter-rated tires may replace the need to install tire
     chains. Do not mix winter-rated all-season tires fitment with other non-winter rated tires. For applications
     where winter rated all season tires are not available, standard all season tires may be mixed if approved for
     mixed fitment under the same national stock number.

WHEEL BEARINGS

     2-96. Usually, no change of lubricants is required when wheel bearings are serviced with grease, automotive
     and artillery (known as GAA). It has a temperature range from -65°F (-54°C) to 225°F (107°C) for year-
     round operations.

SNOW CHAINS

     2-97. Supervisors should conduct a thorough inspection of vehicle chains prior to cold weather to ensure that
     they are serviceable. This inspection should include checking for signs of wear and tear like rust and weak
     links that may lead to chains becoming unserviceable. Inspect chain connector mechanisms to ensure that
     they function smoothly and will latch. Include fresh elastic chain adjusters when ordering replacement
     inventory. Have extra work gloves or rubberized gloves available, packed with the chains in a waterproof
     bag. Mounting chains while on the road will soak through a set of leather gloves.

     2-98. Chains always go on the drive wheels. Rear-wheel and front-wheel drive vehicles require two chains,
     one for each drive wheel. For all-wheel drive vehicles, each wheel needs a snow chain.

     2-99. Each vehicle’s TM should identify the size snow chains required and instruct Soldiers on how to install
     and adjust them. During cold weather periods, the operator inspection of tire chains should be done as a post
     operation check and as a part of a weekly PMCS. Drivers should practice mounting and removing chains;
     they can take an hour or more to install under normal (wet, snowy, dark) conditions. Drivers should deploy
     roadside safety warning markers during chain installation.

             Note. Always follows the equipment operator’s TM on tire chain installation procedures. Improper
             installation may damage the CTIS, brake lines, and other final drive components.

     2-100. Tire chains should only be applied hand tight to allow for tire creep and avoid gouging the tires. To
     minimize chain flapping when the vehicle is rolling, use chain adjuster bungees or rubber tensioner cords to
     snug down chains. Remove the chains when not in use. This simple practice helps keep them in good
     condition and reduces tire stress.

     2-101. When equipped with chains, keep vehicle speeds according to the operator TM. The speed is usually
     10 to 30 miles per hour or 15 miles per hour on average (16 to 45 kilometers per hour) while driving with
     snow chains.

     2-102. It is important to have the right size tire chains as one size does not fit all vehicles. Using the wrong
     size chain can lead to a lack of traction on slippery roads, Additionally, incorrect tire chains can damage the
     vehicle’s tires or even the body of the vehicle. Check the additional authorization list to determine the correct
     tire chains for a vehicle. Chains wear out after approximately 350 miles of asphalt road operation.

     2-103. Unless otherwise directed, do not run tire chains on bare clear pavement. The vehicle TM will specify
     chain type/size, allowed speed, and axle line installation positions. When using chains, maintain correct
     installation tension and avoid harsh acceleration/braking resulting in wheel spin or lockup whenever possible.

     2-104. Use the correct pressure setting and transfer case range for the usage conditions. If chains should fail,
     repair them immediately; broken chains could result in vehicle damage. When using tire chains on public
     roads, ensure it is legal and in-season. Install chains in safe, level areas free of traffic hazards. Some chains
     are heavy and require a two-man lift for installation.

30  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                    Equipment Maintenance

     2-105. The use of tire chains on fuel tankers is authorized. When used, they must be used on all four rear
     tanker trailer tires. Do not use tire chains for fuel tankers on dry clear pavement or during conditions that
     could create excessive sparks.

BRAKES

     2-106. When parking in freezing weather, avoid setting the brakes as they may freeze and become stuck.
     Instead, use chock blocks to secure the wheels or tracks in place. If brake components do freeze in the set
     position, use a portable heater for thawing to prevent damage to the vehicle powertrain.

Hydraulic Brakes

     2-107. Check the reservoir of hydraulic brakes for proper fluid level. No seasonal change of fluid for
     hydraulic brake systems is required. Use brake fluid, silicone, MIL-PRF-3270, Brake Fluid, Polyglycol,
     Automotive, All-Weather, Operational and Preservative.

Air Brakes

                                                 CAUTION

                    The pneumatic (air pressure based) systems of vehicles re-entering cold
                    weather after being in a warm shelter are prone to condensation
                    freezing. As the vehicle cools, the pneumatic systems should be
                    exercised to flush out moist air.

     2-108. Frozen moisture in the air brake system seriously affects operation. Brake lines, air brake filters, brake
     chambers, pushrods, valves, and seals are subject to more defects and failure in the cold. Condensation
     between brake shoes and brake drums may freeze, making it impossible for the vehicle to move. When this
     happens, use portable heating equipment to thaw the brake shoes from the drums. Ensure the alcohol
     evaporator kit (if part of the system) is functioning. Check brake lines, brake chambers, relay valves,
     pushrods, seals, and slack adjusters. Check air compressor, unloader valve, and governor for good condition
     and satisfactory operation. With the air pressure at the governed maximum and the brakes applied, stop the
     engine. Monitor the pressure closely for one minute to ensure that there is no noticeable drop in air pressure.

     2-109. The air dryer is equipped with a heating element and a desiccant cartridge designed to remove
     moisture from air before it enters the primary and secondary air tanks. If the air dryer is unable to eliminate
     all excess moisture, it is automatically released when the air system reaches 120 psi. Drain reservoirs
     immediately after operation, and close drain valves immediately after draining to prevent freezing in the open
     position. In the morning, build up pressure before moving the vehicle. Follow -10 TM PMCS procedures on
     air dryer proper operation and maintenance.

     2-110. Several tactical vehicles types are equipped with a CTIS. It is not unusual for air valves to freeze
     resulting in locked brakes or flat tires. For vehicles with CTIS, a few pounds of air should be vented from
     tires, and then be re-pressurized while in the cold to verify proper function.

STEERING GEAR

     2-111. Improper lubricants will congeal or harden and make steering difficult or impossible. Fill hydraulic
     power steering reservoirs with hydraulic fluid, petroleum base (military symbol supply code (OHT) [MIL-
     PRF-6083] and North Atlantic Treaty Organization [NATO] symbol C-635); original equipment/heavy duty
     oil (OE/HDO) synthetic multipurpose lubricant (SMPL); or oil, engine, arctic (OEA)-30 (do not use
     DEXRON VI). Examine arms, tie rods, drag links, seals and boots, pitman arm, gear column, and wheel for
     good condition and secure mounting.

SPRINGS

     2-112. Springs can become brittle and prone to breaking at low temperatures. Clips, leaves, U-bolts, hangers,
     and shackles must be in good condition and correctly and securely mounted. Spring leaves should not be

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  31


---

Chapter 2

     broken or shifted from their correct position. Ensure that all suspension spring retainers, assemblies, and
     mounting bolts are securely tightened. When starting out, proceed with caution to allow the springs enough
     time to attain flexibility and adjust to the load they are supporting. This will allow for smoother and safer
     operation of the equipment. Avoid driving into depressions or over obstacles; this practice may create shocks
     that could break springs in the cold.

SHOCK ABSORBERS

     2-113. When the temperature drops below freezing, the fluid in shock absorbers can thicken and cause the
     vehicle to ride hard or break the shock absorbers. Ensure that the shock absorber bodies are securely mounted
     to the frame. Replace shock absorbers that are leaking or not working properly. Operating the vehicle slowly
     for the first three to five miles allows the oil in the shocks to warm up and helps prevent damage to the shock
     absorber, the operating rods, and the mounting brackets.

TRACK AND SUSPENSION

     2-114. Check oil-lubricated roadwheel bearings to ensure that water has not collected during operation. If
     significant amounts of water have collected, seals may rupture as the water freezes and expands. If possible,
     avoid quick starts, stops, sharp turns, and side slippage on ice and snow, as they can throw tracks or cause
     loss of control.

     2-115. Ice and snow adhere to tracks, hindering operation. Cold contracts metal and makes rubber brittle.
     When operating in snow-covered and icy terrain, remove track pads as the commander directs, and employ
     traction aids (such as ice cleats or reversing every third track center guide). Remove dirt and ice and inspect
     for good condition and proper lubrication. Check track adjustment. Do not adjust tracks too tightly in a warm
     shelter because they contract and can break in temperatures of -40°F (-40°C) and below. Adjust track so that
     the slack is 50% greater than that specified for normal operation in the vehicle TM (Figure 2-1). Another
     method is to allow the vehicle and track to cool to outside temperature before adjusting.

                                          Figure 2-1. Cold weather adjustment

     2-116. Ice cleats improve traction on icy roads. However, these cleats are destructive to road surfaces. Limit
     their use to situations where the road surface is covered by a thick layer of ice and snow and not visible. Ice
     cleats designed for the T158 track of the M1 series tank are available to enhance performance on icy terrain.
     Install a set of cleats on every fifth track shoe for best performance. A total of 64 cleats and 64 new self-
     locking nuts are required per vehicle (32 per side). These cleats are for the T158 track only; they will not
     work with the T156 track (cleats are not yet available for the T156 track).

CAB CLOSURES

     2-117. Cab closures protect Soldiers from below freezing temperatures and wind. Ensure cab closure
     mountings are secure and in good condition. Replace broken windows and ensure that closures fit tightly.

FIRE EXTINGUISHERS

     2-118. Fire protection is essential for the survivability of equipment and personnel. Most up-armored vehicles
     are equipped with an automatic fire suppression system that protects the crew inside the vehicle and the
     powertrain. Perform PMCS of the equipment fire suppression system. Ensure nozzles are free of clogged ice
     or debris and that photo sensors are not covered, obstructed, or damaged. Winterize carbon dioxide (CO2)
     fire extinguishers according to the appropriate fire extinguisher technical bulletin (TB).

32  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025
