                                                 Chapter 3

              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

    This chapter provides general information on types of batteries and considerations for their
    storage, care, and use in a cold or arctic environment.

POWER STORAGE BATTERY MAINTENANCE

     3-1. One of the greatest hindrances to successful operations in cold or arctic environments is the effect of the
     cold on power storage or batteries. Battery power is as critical to mission capability as petroleum. Maintainers
     and operators face the challenges of dealing with different battery types that have distinct characteristics.
     Power storage or batteries are typically characterized by the materiel used for the electrodes (or plates) within
     each cell. They must be maintained in accordance with the TM or manufacturer’s recommendations.

     3-2. The cold usually increases the amount of power required to start an engine or power a device and reduces
     the cranking power of batteries. Maintenance managers and unit planners should anticipate an increased level
     of battery use, failure, and parts replacement. Proprietary COTS devices will have unique battery power
     requirements that must also be accounted for. For more information on Army power storage, visit
     https://battery.army.mil/.

BATTERY MAINTENANCE AND MANAGEMENT PROGRAM

     3-3. The Battery Maintenance and Management Program provides guidance and sets procedures for the use
     and maintenance of military or commercial standard non-rechargeable, rechargeable, and reusable batteries.
     It establishes actions of the operators, supervisors, and maintainers to optimize battery life in accordance with
     AR 750-1 and DA Pam 750-1. Supervisors of equipment operators will need local unit guidance on policies
     and procedures with listed references for each battery type in the inventory.

     3-4. Maintenance personnel will order and use valve-regulated lead-acid (VRLA) batteries as replacements
     for flooded wet-cell batteries. As an exception, flooded wet-cell batteries may only be used when VRLA
     batteries are not available or not yet approved for use by the TM. Do not mix flooded wet-cell and sealed
     batteries.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Only trained personnel are authorized to perform maintenance on
                    batteries in accordance with the equipment TM.

     3-5. Temperature ranges and battery performance values presented in this chapter are generalized. Actual
     battery properties will vary according to manufacturer design specifications and the specifics of the field
     situation. For detailed information on a specific battery, consult the appropriate item-specific TM or military
     standards performance specification document (manufacturer or military).

     3-6. Assume any communications-electronics batteries contain corrosive and/or flammable material and treat
     accordingly. In general, any lithium-ion type battery has more stringent handling, storage, and disposal
     requirements than that of batteries with more benign chemistries.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  33


---

Chapter 3

                                                 WARNING

                    • Never attempt to charge a frozen battery.
                    • Do not attempt to operate equipment with frozen batteries.
                    • Do not attempt to slave equipment with a battery that is or has

                       been frozen.

                    Attempting any of these actions may cause the batteries to
                    explode and may cause injuries to personnel.

     3-7. If a lithium battery overheats, hisses, or bulges, immediately move the device away from flammable
     materials and place it on a noncombustible surface. Move the device and battery outdoors if possible, but do
     not handle it if flames appear. Allow ample ventilation while the battery burns itself out.

             Note. Baking soda and household ammonia solution are two substances that can neutralize the
             acid contained in battery electrolytes. Always have baking soda available to clean up shop spills
             (not for first aid or medical treatment use). Mix equal parts of either one with water to create a
             solution that neutralizes the battery acid.

     3-8. A battery’s total energy storage capacity is reduced in cold environments. This not only applies to the
     energy that can be drawn out of a battery, but it also applies to the rate at which energy can be absorbed into
     the battery after it is depleted. Low temperatures can impact the charging time of rechargeable batteries.
     Specifically, cold lead-acid batteries in tactical vehicles can take up to twice as long to recharge compared to
     when operating in fair weather temperatures.

     3-9. The SOC of a battery indicates how well it is prepared to deliver electrical energy and is expressed as a
     percentage of the battery's highest energy rating. Understanding the SOC is important for optimizing battery
     usage and ensuring efficient energy management. The SOC shows how much energy a battery has remaining
     (full charge = 100% SOC, middle charge = 50% SOC, or fully discharged = 0% SOC). Conversely, the depth
     of discharge (DOD) is a measure of how deeply discharged a battery is (fully charged = 0% DOD, middle =
     50% DOD or fully discharged = 100% DOD). The DOD is the opposite of SOC, so as one increases, the
     other decreases.

     3-10. It is important for equipment operators to receive training on how to prevent situations that may lead
     to battery freezing. Even in hazardous cold situations, operators can still prevent batteries from freezing by
     following the TM (both the TM for the platform in general, and the TM specifically for the battery) and unit
     supplemental procedures developed by the maintainers.

     3-11. Maintainers and operators need to ask several questions when using batteries in cold weather
     operations. The answers to these questions should be found in unit supplemental procedures and presented
     as a part of Soldier training:

          • What are the operator’s responsibilities? What are the maintainer’s responsibilities?
          • What maintenance efforts could damage the battery, and how are they avoided?
          • How should batteries be maintained for optimal performance and longevity, especially in extremely

                cold weather?
          • At what temperature will battery power begin to degrade?
          • At what temperature will a battery stop functioning?
          • If the battery power degrades, how can it be restored to a useful power level without damaging the

                battery?
          • What hazards do batteries present and how can Soldiers avoid or respond appropriately to them?
          • What are the disposal considerations for a battery after use?

34  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

                                                 CAUTION

                    When starting and running an engine, disconnect float chargers, trickle
                    chargers, and related devices unless the TM says it can remain hooked
                    up. Failure to do so may cause damage to equipment.

     3-12. In cold, keeping a battery serviceable involves a different set of priorities from those that occur in
     temperate weather, even though the rules and procedures are the same. A cold-soaked (not frozen) battery
     takes longer to recharge because the cold will increase internal resistance, reducing its ability to accept a
     charge. This limitation applies to all battery chemistries in common use. The cold temperatures slow the
     electrochemical energy conversion process. However, as the process continues, waste heat caused by internal
     resistance during charging can warm the battery. This effect then improves the charging rate to some extent.
     The voltage regulator in the charging system plays a crucial role in maintaining a specific voltage intended
     for a given battery’s structure and chemistry. In the case of tactical vehicles, voltage regulators are designed
     to keep the charging system voltage at an optimum setting for temperate weather and prevent the alternator
     or a charger from overcharging the battery. However, such default settings may not be adequate for
     overcoming cold-induced resistance.

KEEPING A BATTERY CHARGED WHILE USING IT

     3-13. At normal temperatures, it takes an engine alternator approximately 20 to 30 minutes (40 to 50 minutes
     for absorbed glass mat batteries) of engine run time at normal idle to replace the energy that was used to start
     the engine. If the temperature is between 32°F (0°C) and 0°F (-18°C), double the run time; if the temperature
     is below 0°F (-18°C), it is recommended to triple the run time, possibly even four or five times longer, just
     to recover from starting an engine.

     3-14. An alternator is meant to maintain the optimum SOC of a battery, not to fully recharge a depleted
     battery. When the engine is idling, the alternator does not start recharge the battery. Thus, if the engine is
     turned off after a short trip, the partially recharged batteries might freeze due to a reduced SOC. Numerous
     short periods of operation (less than 20 minutes at a time) will eventually discharge the battery, even if the
     engine is operated at high speed.

     3-15. Regardless of the type of battery, all existing tactical vehicle batteries are based on lead-acid chemistry.
     When a vehicle or equipment will not start at low temperatures, turn off the starting switch. Check for a
     frozen battery before attempting to slave start or charge it. For traditional flooded cell batteries, remove the
     filler caps for each cell and check if the electrolyte is frozen. For the newer lead-acid gels to include gelatin
     silicate electrolyte (GEL) or AGM batteries, it is important to allow frozen batteries to warm-up naturally
     indoors as circumstances allow. If the battery case is swollen or cracked, a maintainer must verify if the
     battery is usable or unusable (frozen).

     3-16. Operators must never attempt to thaw and recharge frozen flooded cell type batteries. Instead, tag them
     as “frozen” and turn them in for proper disposal. Maintainers should be proactive in checking and testing
     batteries in cold environments. When recharging a battery that has been cold soaked (but not frozen), the
     battery will resist the flow of current. The amount of resistance to recharging will cause localized heating or
     overheating within the battery.

             Note. Unit maintenance trainers should regularly refresh all operators and maintainers who work
             with automotive batteries on all safety warnings pertaining to them. The full list of warnings can
             be found in TM 9-6140-200-13.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  35


---

Chapter 3

                                                 CAUTION

                    DO NOT mutilate, incinerate, or throw away batteries with trash.
                    Batteries are a potentially environmentally HAZMAT. The Defense
                    Property Disposal Service is responsible for disposal of batteries as
                    hazardous items.
                    Do not leave recharging batteries unattended if they have been
                    subjected to temperatures below their total discharge freezing point.
                    Check for electrolyte leakage or thermal runaway as appropriate.

Battery Self-Discharge
     3-17. Even if a battery is not in use, it is self-discharging. The challenge for the operator or maintainer is to
     keep the battery charge high enough to keep it from freezing and able to crank over a cold-soaked sluggish
     engine. This marginal zone between fully charged and the minimum voltage is called the floating voltage.
     3-18. Trickle chargers are only good for a spot situation to raise the energy level of a depleted battery that
     has not frozen. Trickle chargers are not apt to push too much charge through cold electrolyte that is resisting
     a recharge. However, an unattended trickle charger could overcharge a battery. They do not have the ability
     to detect a battery’s SOC and regulate the volume and rate of energy flowing into a battery.
     3-19. A float charger should be used to keep an idle or stored battery at its optimum charge. These types of
     chargers have circuitry to prevent batteries from overcharging. It means that they can be hooked up and left
     unattended almost indefinitely. Flooded battery fluid levels should be checked regularly for evaporation.

State of Charge
     3-20. The freezing point of a lead-acid battery (Figure 3-1) depends on its SOC. The SOC equates to the
     battery’s remaining energy at any given concentration of sulfuric acid in the electrolyte. The electrolyte
     begins as a solution of about 30% sulfuric acid and 70% distilled water. The specific gravity of the solution
     is a measure of the sulfuric acid suspended in the water after discharging or recharging.

            Figure 3-1. Freezing points of lead-acid battery at various specific gravities

    3-21. As a battery discharges, chemical reactions gradually remove the sulfuric acid from the electrolyte
    solution. This process reduces the concentration of sulfuric acid as the percentage of water increases. As the
    water content of electrolyte increases, the specific gravity of the solution approaches that of pure water (1.00).

36  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

     Therefore, the freezing point of the electrolyte depends on the ambient temperature and the water percentage
     as indicated by the specific gravity. In this way, the specific gravity indicates (indirectly) the available energy
     or SOC.

Hydrometer Electrolyte Specific Gravity Test
     3-22. The battery’s SOC is indicated by the specific gravity of the electrolyte. For instance, if the hydrometer
     reading is “1.150”, the water in the electrolyte can freeze at -5°F (-21°C). As the specific gravity decreases,
     the battery’s ability to start a vehicle or generator set engine also decreases. Additionally, the electrolyte will
     freeze at higher temperatures. A hydrometer can determine the battery’s SOC if one checks each cell. It can
     spot bad cells too. However, even under favorable temperature conditions the process is lengthy. A
     hydrometer measures the electrolyte’s specific gravity in flooded cell type batteries only.

             Note. One cannot use a hydrometer with the increasingly more prevalent sealed batteries.
             Determining the SOC of sealed GEL and AGM type batteries requires an electrical analyzer.

Refractometer Specific Gravity Test
     3-23. The refractometer (Figure 3-2) is the preferred testing tool for conducting specific gravity testing. Like
     the hydrometer, the refractometer is specifically designed to measure the electrolyte of flooded batteries.
     When testing the battery acid—
          • Use goggles or face shield to protect eyes.
          • Use rubber gloves to protect skin and clothing.

                            Figure 3-2. Refractometer for testing electrolyte

3-24. Refractometers need to be zero adjusted before use. This ensures that neither rough handling nor the
ambient temperature shifts have altered the readings from the tool:

     • Place one or two drops of distilled water on the prism (not tap water).
     • Make sure that the entire measuring window or prism plate is covered with the liquid (without dry

          spots or bubbles).
     • Close the plastic cover or cover plate (without bubbles).
     • Observe the location of the light/dark boundary underneath a light source, verifying that the

          boundary falls at the waterline. If not, use equipment instructions to locate the adjustment screw.
     • Rotate the adjusting screw so that the light/dark boundary lines up with the “waterline,” or 1.000

          kilograms per liter.
     • Once the zero adjustment is complete, clean the prism with a soft cloth.

3-25. Add distilled water as needed to properly fill each cell. The battery should be warm before checking
the battery fluid level. If water is added to a battery that is exposed to freezing temperatures, the layer of new
water will usually stay at the top and freeze before it has a chance to mix with the electrolyte. Warming a
battery to above 32°F (0°C) will allow water to evenly mix into the electrolyte. If the battery is cold (at or

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  37


---

Chapter 3

     below freezing), distilled or raw water will freeze into surface ice on top of the electrolyte or become ice
     crystals within the electrolyte. Frozen water within a battery will skew and invalidate the specific gravity
     testing.

     3-26. A fully charged battery will not freeze unless the ambient temperature falls below -70°F (-57°C). Thus,
     it is essential to keep batteries fully charged. Once a battery freezes, it is no longer serviceable. Frozen
     electrolyte will expand and damage the battery.

             Note. Maintainers must wear the appropriate PPE when handling sulfuric acid electrolyte and
             servicing flooded batteries.

Frozen Batteries

     3-27. Wet cell batteries may freeze if they are not fully charged, as the electrolyte fluid in a battery contains
     water and sulfuric acid, both of which can freeze. When a battery is not fully charged, the sulfuric acid and
     distilled water are not mixed properly; this could cause the distilled water to freeze. For example, the
     electrolyte of a lead-acid battery can freeze at approximately -16°F (-27°C) when the battery is only 40%
     charged, but at around -92°F (-69°C) when it is fully charged.

     3-28. If a flooded battery freezes, it can expand, break the plates, and crack the case. A frozen battery may
     need to be replaced and should not be slave-started or charged. If one of the following is present, refer to the
     equipment’s TM for removal and follow-on tasks. Notify the maintenance personnel if these conditions are
     present—

          • Bulging sides.
          • Cracks.
          • No liquid sound when moving battery.

     3-29. Other signs that the batteries are having cold weather issues include—
          • Electronic device errors in functions.
          • Warning lights. The charging system warning light or battery indicator light may be illuminated.
          • Engine starting difficulties. The engine may crank slowly or sluggishly when starting the vehicle.
          • Dim headlights. The headlights may dim when the vehicle is idling but brighten back up after the
                engine is revved.
          • Strange smell. A noticeable strange smell inside the vehicle that could smell like rotten eggs. This
                can happen when the battery's internal mechanism shorts out due to freezing or overheating.

     3-30. Operators and maintainers working with batteries in cold weather should observe the following
     guidelines:

          • Never charge a frozen battery.
          • Inspect a battery using the item-specific TM.
          • Maintainers should verify that new batteries are fully charged before their initial use.
          • Thoroughly clean new battery (tapered terminals and clamp terminals) connections before battery

                installation.
          • Make sure lead-acid batteries are fully charged after use to prevent sulfation. Do not leave low

                charged batteries sitting in a vehicle or stored with only a low charge.
          • Do not allow a lead-acid battery to freeze. A discharged battery freezes at higher temperatures

                compared to a fully charged one.
          • Do not charge batteries at temperatures above 120°F (49°C).
          • Lead-acid batteries being charged produce highly explosive hydrogen gas during the charging

                process.
          • Keep sparks, open flames, and other ignition sources away from the charging area.
          • Charge in a well-ventilated area.
          • Choose the appropriate charging program for flooded GEL and AGM batteries. Check the TM or

                manufacturer’s specifications on recommended voltage thresholds.

38  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

                                                 WARNING

                    Never add water to a cold battery (under 32°F [0°C]) because it
                    can separately freeze before mixing with the electrolyte. Ensure
                    the battery is warm before adding water.

     3-31. Operators should observe that flooded vented lead-acid (VLA) batteries are filled to the top-off marker
     with electrolyte and charged to a 1.280 specific gravity, not the temperate zone 1.250 specific gravity
     standard. Recharging batteries to meet this standard is a maintainer’s job. Unit operating procedures should
     specify when such changes are to be made, in accordance with item TMs.

     3-32. Only add distilled water into the batteries. The impurities of tap water will corrode the lead plates inside
     the battery and prevent the battery from charging to full capacity.

Battery Load Test

     3-33. It is not sufficient to only know if a battery is delivering the 12 volts for which it is rated. The question
     is whether the battery has the reserve power to overcome the cold engine’s resistance to starting.

     3-34. Load testing determines if the battery can supply enough current (in amperes) to start an engine. The
     tester simulates an electric starter’s current draw and evaluates the battery's ability to crank the engine. The
     tester draws current from the battery while measuring its voltage. The voltage level of a charged or otherwise
     good battery will remain relatively steady under load. A defective battery or one with a low SOC will show
     a rapid voltage drop. For example, a 12-volt battery voltage could drop to 10.5 volts or less.

     3-35. A battery’s cold cranking amps (CCA) rating and the ambient temperature can affect the test results.
     CCA refers to the maximum current the battery can continuously provide for 30 seconds at 0°F (-18°C)
     before its voltage drops too low. The CCA rating is usually printed on the battery’s label. After the test, a
     good battery will show an open circuit voltage above 10.5 volts. If the voltage drops below 10.5 volts, add
     more charge, then retest the battery. In addition, the maintainer performing this test must compensate for low
     temperatures. For instance, at 0°F (-18°C) subtract 300 CCA from the battery’s CCA rating. It is crucial to
     carefully follow the load tester’s operating instructions.

     3-36. Using a handheld load tester (Figure 3-3), maintainers can tell the actual voltage being delivered, and
     if the available amperage will hold up long enough for the starter motor to turn over the engine without
     damaging the battery. If the battery needs the amperage boosted, let the maintainers charge the battery or
     slave start the engine.

                               Figure 3-3. Battery load testers (digital and analog)

BATTERY FIRE PREVENTION

     3-37. Batteries generate heat due to chemical reactions during charging and discharging. If energy is pushed
     through a battery at a rate that is higher than it can absorb, or the heat builds faster than it can shed to remain

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  39


---

Chapter 3

     cool, the extra heat can burn or ignite the battery’s material. This battery overheating is known as thermal
     runaway. It is a catastrophic event for a rechargeable battery that can result in a rapid increase in temperature
     and potential fire or explosion. In a flooded cell battery, the water boils causing a gas emission of hydrogen
     in large quantities, eventually burning the electrodes that store energy. In sealed batteries, it can rupture the
     casing spilling the electrolyte. In lithium batteries, it can cause them to erupt on fire. Battery fires can happen
     under any weather condition, but they are most associated with excessive energy being pushed across the
     available battery plates at a rate that exceeds the battery’s ability to dissipate. This is commonly referred to
     as an exothermic reaction; it typically happens in extremely high or low temperature environments or when
     the battery is not maintained properly. These reactions can happen to both automotive and non-automotive
     batteries. Thermal runaway does not occur abruptly; it is typically preceded by warning signs (except for
     lithium batteries). When a depleted battery is cold soaked, it is important to warm the battery back up as soon
     as circumstances allow to avoid sulfation.

BATTERY SULFATION

     3-38. Sulfation is a major threat to any fluid filled battery (flooded wet-cell or sealed). Sulfation occurs in
     discharged flooded electrolyte type lead-acid batteries regardless of whether they are in storage or installed
     in a vehicle. “Sulfation” describes the formation of lead sulfate crystals (PbSO4) on the battery’s electrodes
     (plates) over time. These crystals raise the battery’s internal resistance to energy absorption. When a battery
     has lost its charge in the cold, crystals form on the battery plates acting as a physical barrier to recharge. As
     a result, longer charging times are required. Once sulfation or crystal growth becomes severe, it can damage
     the battery permanently. Lead-acid GEL and AGM type batteries are not as prone to sulfation based on their
     physical construction; however, they are still at risk for sulfation damage and thermal runaway.

             Note. If the operator finds that a battery case is buckled, swollen, or cracked, or if ice or frost can
             be seen inside any cell, they should notify maintenance that the battery is possibly frozen.

CAUSES OF THERMAL RUNAWAY

     3-39. Thermal runaway refers to a dangerous, self-sustaining chain reaction where a battery rapidly heats up
     due to internal chemical reactions, causing a significant increase in temperature and pressure and potentially
     leading to fire or explosion if not contained. It occurs when the heat generated within the battery exceeds its
     ability to dissipate heat, creating a positive feedback loop where rising temperature further increases the
     reaction rate and causes even more heat generation.

     3-40. In theory, the initial cause for a thermal runaway differs between high heat and cold weather. Electrical
     energy is a product of the chemical interactions between the electrolyte and plates within the battery.
     However, another byproduct of this chemical interaction is heat. The process of heat generation and
     accumulation within a battery is referred to as an exothermic reaction. In temperate weather, a properly filled
     and charged battery can generate electrical power and dissipate the excess heat of the exothermic reaction
     through the battery walls as fast as the heat is produced.

     3-41. This heat cannot be allowed to get so hot that it burns the electrical plates within the battery. The heat
     must be able to dissipate. If the air temperature is too hot, the battery will not be able to shed heat as fast as
     it is generated, causing heat to build up on the plates. As the heat builds, lead and sulfur in the electrolyte
     separates and crystalizes on the battery plates, causing sulfation. When the chemical interaction cannot
     dissipate energy as electricity through the battery plates, it bakes the sulfation crystals onto the surface of the
     plates and blocks the electrical charge from reaching the plates.

     3-42. If the electrolyte freezes, it causes the energy of the electro-chemical reaction to build up on localized
     areas of the plates, that again converts directly into a localized exothermic heat that damages the plates. In
     situations of overcharging, high heat, or freezing, the resulting excessive heat generation is followed by
     thermal runaway. The following situations, whether in hot, temperate, or cold weather, can cause the battery
     to rupture, explode, or catch on fire:

          • When the whole battery is surrounded by too much outside heat, preventing the heat of chemical
                reaction from dissipating as fast as it is being generated.

40  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

          • When a normal charge is forced through a limited surface area of available negative plates used to
                perform ion bonding, preventing the heat of chemical reaction from dissipating as fast as it is being
                generated:
                ▪ If electrolyte is not properly filled or has leaked out of the battery, limiting the available surface
                     area of the plates.
                ▪ When sulfation occurs, limiting the available surface area of the plates.
                ▪ When frozen electrolyte covers plate surfaces, limiting the available area of the plates.

          • If excessive charge voltage forces a high overcharge current into the whole battery.
          • If there is a dysfunctional voltage regulator on the charger, vehicle, or equipment, forcing a high

                overcharge current into the whole battery.

PROCESS OF THERMAL RUNAWAY

     3-43. One of the primary risks to battery life in the cold temperatures is the occurrence of “thermal runaway.”
     Thermal runaway commonly occurs in the following sequence in subfreezing temperatures:

          1) If a battery is partially frozen or corroded by sulfation, it will limit the available surface area on the
                negative plates. This causes the recharging current to not be absorbable evenly throughout the whole
                of the plates.

          2) Next, the current gets redirected through the spots on the available plate areas that have contact with
                active electrolyte.

          3) The recharge current then overloads the limited available plate surfaces with more internal heat than
                the battery can effectively dissipate.

          4) The excess heat scorches the lead sulfate crystal into the plates and boils off the hydrogen of the
                electrolyte, expanding the obstructed area of the plates. This reduces the plate area available to
                support the heat of the recharge current.

          5) This forces the available areas of the plates to carry an increasingly greater volume of current and
                heat. It cooks even more lead sulfate crystals into the plates.

          6) As less and less plate surface area remains available to support the charge and dissipate the heat,
                more heat becomes concentrated on the remaining plate area and becomes a cascading plate failure
                at an ever-increasing temperature, at an ever-increasing speed.

          7) Within a short period of time, this heat will cause sulfation, leading to the formation of non-
                conductive lead sulfur crystals on the battery plates. This will cause an increase in battery
                deterioration, potentially causing it to overheat, melt, or catch on fire.

             Note. The initial cause (steps 1 and 2) of thermal runaway is theoretically the same, though
             structurally different between cold weather and hot weather battery damage. However, the latter
             stages and end results are the same.

PREVENTION OF THERMAL RUNAWAY

     3-44. Operators should not attempt to start an engine with a depleted battery. After three failed attempts to
     start an engine, operators should find help from a maintainer. If the battery can deliver enough energy to turn
     on the lights (even if only for a moment), the battery might still be recovered and recharged. Otherwise,
     operators must assume the battery is frozen and treat it as such in accordance with unit policy. Forcing a
     depleted battery to start an engine uses the last measure of energy that is holding the battery back from fully
     freezing. An operator risks permanently destroying the battery if any more than three attempts are made to
     start the engine without the assistance of supplemental power.

     3-45. Both operators and maintainers can help prevent battery thermal runaway in cold weather:
          • Use the TM to perform regular preventive maintenance.
          • Maintain a full charge on the battery.
          • Record float voltage, checking for unusual increases over time.
          • Check that the battery has not leaked electrolyte or that it is not lacking electrolyte.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  41


---

Chapter 3

          • Check that the battery casing is not deformed or cracked.
          • Warm a battery to above freezing before attempting to recharge it.
          • Use the battery charger that is specified by the TM. These chargers will indicate in the instructions

                that they are capable (or not) of electronically detecting the start of a thermal runaway condition.
                Those chargers that can will automatically adjust the power flow or shut off the charger once the
                battery is ready.

VEHICLE BATTERIES

     3-46. There are two basic types of lead-acid storage batteries in four types of construction technologies
     commonly found in Army automotive equipment:

          • Flooded batteries (also known as wet-cell batteries) contain free-flowing electrolyte fluid:
                ▪ VLA batteries (the traditional “flooded” cell car battery).
                ▪ Nickel Cadmium wet-cell batteries (not commonly used for ground vehicles).

          • VRLA batteries, commonly known as sealed batteries or maintenance-free batteries, are designed
                with immobilized electrolytes to prevent spills:
                ▪ Gel batteries.
                ▪ AGM batteries.
                ▪ Deep-cycle lead-acid batteries are specifically designed for deep cycling applications. These
                     batteries are typically discharged to 50% of their capacity and recharged again. This type of
                     battery is generally used in applications where constant power is required for long periods of
                     time like in the case of most heavy construction equipment.

     3-47. Generally, a well charged battery can be stored in uncontrolled temperature conditions of -40°F to
     100°F (-40°C to 38°C) for at least 18 months without damage. On the other hand, wet-cell batteries have
     high self-discharge rates and tend to die much sooner. Therefore, it is important for battery maintainers to
     know the specific storage requirements based on the manufacturer’s specifications.

FLOODED BATTERIES

     3-48. Flooded batteries require routine maintenance. Flooded batteries use lead plates, a sulfuric acid as
     electrolyte, and plate separators. Most flooded batteries are not sealed and do not recombine the gases to
     liquids internally. Instead, these gases are vented out to the atmosphere. Acid, steam, and condensation can
     flow through these same vents causing possible maintenance issues.

     3-49. The normal flooded batteries require extra care and regular maintenance in the form of watering,
     equalizing charges, and keeping the terminals clean. Flooded cell batteries must be mounted the right way
     up and can be susceptible to spillage.

Vented Lead-Acid Batteries

     3-50. Known properly as a VLA battery, a wet-cell battery is equipped with vents to allow gas to escape to
     the atmosphere. VLA batteries have a longer lifespan and require more maintenance over the life of the
     battery. These batteries vent electrolyte to the atmosphere; it is essential to monitor electrolytic fluid on a
     continual basis. Add distilled water periodically to properly maintain fluid levels.

     3-51. VLA battery installations are physically larger than most other battery solutions and require a spill
     containment system or a floor coating to manage the liquid electrolyte as this liquid is corrosive and
     environmentally hazardous.

             Note. Due to advances in battery technology, the traditional flooded VLA batteries are being
             replaced and upgraded with VRLA (sealed) batteries. Flooded VLA batteries are no longer
             common in the Army. They are noted here due to the complexity of their service and maintenance.

     3-52. Unless a storage battery is warmed above 32°F (0°C), it will resist receiving a full charge from the
     vehicle’s generator. If the vehicle has a battery preheater, use it as described in the operator’s TM. A battery
     preheater is a pad, wrap or blanket made of heat-resistant synthetic material with wire heating elements that
     plugs into an external electrical power source. If the vehicle does not have a preheater, an insulated battery

42  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                        Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

box can help maintain specific gravity at higher levels for longer periods. It is important to ensure that the
starting batteries remain fully charged.

                                             CAUTION

               Check flooded VLA battery vents. Batteries produced hydrogen gas.
               Hydrogen gas can cause a battery casing to explode due to blockage of
               the battery vent by ice.

3-53. When it is necessary to recharge a flooded battery at low temperatures (under 32°F [0°C]), reduce the
normal charging current to prevent excessive gassing and boiling of the electrolyte. The charging process
takes longer at low temperatures due to lower electrochemical reaction rates. Follow the instructions of the
battery charger for low-temperature operation of all types of starting batteries, including flooded, AGM, and
GEL. This will reduce the permissible charging current, thereby increasing the charging time.

       Note. If adding distilled water to a battery at temperatures of 32°F (0°C) to approximately 50°F
       (10°C), do not fill to level indicated on cell cover or vent plug. The electrolyte will expand as it is
       heated, and the battery will flood. The acid of electrolyte should never be added to a battery once
       initiated into service. Use only distilled water.

3-54. Consider these tips when working with flooded batteries include to avoid premature unserviceability,
and accidents.

     • Before charging, check that the plates of flooded batteries are fully submerged in electrolyte.
     • Never add the acid to the electrolyte solution. Operators are not authorized to add electrolyte to

          flooded batteries, only distilled water.
     • Do not add water to a battery with an ambient temperature below 32°F (0°F).
     • Fill battery with distilled or deionized water to cover the plates if low. In emergencies, check with

          the maintenance supervisor before using tap water.
     • Formation of gas bubbles in a flooded battery indicates that the battery is reaching full SOC

          (hydrogen on negative plate and oxygen on positive plate).
     • Check again and fill water level to designated level after charging. Overfilling when the battery is

          empty can cause acid spillage.

3-55. The available energy in vehicle batteries decreases sharply when temperatures fall (See Figure 3-4). At
the same time, the power requirements for starting an engine increase by about two or three times when the
battery is least capable of delivering that power.

Figure 3-4. Flooded battery power availability at varying temperatures

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31                                            43


---

Chapter 3

Nickel-Cadmium Batteries, Wet-Cell
     3-56. Nickel-cadmium wet-cell batteries are commonly found in aviation (MIL-PRF-81757 and MIL-PRF-
     8565), maritime, or industrial uninterrupted power supply (commonly known as UPS) backup systems. They
     are almost never used in ground vehicles because they cannot be serviced together with lead-acid batteries.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not transport, store, or service lead-acid and nickel-cadmium (wet or
                    sealed) batteries in the same area. Introduction of acid electrolytes by
                    vapor emissions into alkaline electrolyte corrodes or destroys both lead-
                    acid and nickel-cadmium batteries

     3-57. The only accurate way to determine the SOC of a nickel-cadmium battery is by a measured discharge
     with a nickel-cadmium battery charger. A nickel-cadmium battery’s SOC does not affect electrolyte specific
     gravity. Measuring the specific gravity of the potassium hydroxide electrolyte will not reveal anything about
     its SOC. The potassium hydroxide in the battery is not prone to freeze damage due to limited chemical change
     between charged and discharged states. However, the water within the electrolyte solution can freeze at
     approximately -75°F (-59°C), potentially rupturing the battery cell.

SEALED BATTERIES

     3-58. These batteries are known properly as VRLA batteries (also known as maintenance-free batteries).
     They feature immobilized electrolytes that prevent leakage in the event of a cracked case. When fully
     charged, VRLA batteries can withstand severe cold conditions without sustaining damage. However, under
     low SOC or when discharged, these batteries may freeze (as shown in Figure 3-5). Temperatures below -
     70°F (-57°C) will freeze VRLA batteries regardless of SOC.

    Figure 3-5. SOC required for VRLA batteries to prevent freezing at a given temperature

    3-59. Maintenance requirements for VRLA batteries are lighter compared to wet-cell types. However, they
    still have a water-based electrolyte; severe cold can still damage discharged GEL or AGM batteries. Ensuring
    they are fully charged will prevent them from freezing. Again, monitoring and maintaining these batteries
    properly is essential in cold environments.

44  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

                                                 CAUTION

                    Never leave a VRLA battery stored in a discharged state. It will cause
                    battery sulfation, interfering with the battery’s ability to be charged, and
                    making it vulnerable to thermal runaway.

Gel Batteries

     3-60. Gel batteries contain a gel-like electrolyte that is mixture of sulfuric acid and silica fume. These
     batteries are completely maintenance-free and non-spilling. These batteries do not need to be kept or installed
     upright and do not require special handing when shipping. They are resistant to electrolyte evaporation,
     spillage and corrosion. They are resistant to extreme temperatures, shock, and vibration. They are capable of
     withstanding over-discharging.

     3-61. Even though GEL batteries are not flooded in the traditional sense, they are still lead-acid batteries that
     incorporate silica or polymer particles to create a gel-like agent into hydroxide-based electrolytes. They may
     have up to 15% greater energy output compared to flooded VLA batteries of the same size.

     3-62. The freeze point for GEL batteries is the same as any VRLA battery using sulfuric acid electrolyte.
     Therefore, temperatures below -70°F (-57°C) will freeze GEL batteries regardless of SOC. If the operator
     suspects the battery is frozen, do not try to charge it and contact a field maintenance level maintainer. A
     qualified maintainer can determine if the battery is serviceable.

     3-63. By using a silicon dust (silica) to suspend the electrolyte, there is very little water expansion that could
     rupture a GEL battery. Though not impossible, it is unlikely that the cold will rupture the battery case. Still,
     it should be checked by an authorized maintainer. Once warmed to normal operating temperatures of about
     65°F (18°C) to 70°F (21°C), they can usually be recharged. There are three causes for GEL battery damage:

          • Batteries sitting for too long in a frozen and discharged state.
          • Charging a battery with partially frozen GEL.
          • Overcharging.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Only use battery chargers intended for GEL batteries. Follow the
                    charger’s operating instructions for GEL cell batteries. Use a constant
                    voltage charger and restrict charging voltage from 13.5 to 14.5 volts
                    (maximum) for 12-volt batteries. When charging two series-connected
                    12-volt batteries (24-volt system), ensure charging voltage is from 27 to
                    29 volts. Using the wrong charger will damage the battery and shorten
                    its life.

     3-64. A partially frozen gel will localize the recharging into those plates in the thawed areas of the battery,
     overheating the active plates. Under these conditions the heat produced inside the battery can lead to thermal
     runaway due to the increasingly localized electrochemical reaction within the battery.

     3-65. Not all equipment has a charging system to maintain its battery’s SOC as vehicles do. For example, a
     GEL battery in a piece of equipment is subject to self-discharge over time; cooler temperatures slow this
     process, but it still occurs. Once the GEL battery is discharged, its gelatin electrolyte can freeze. A frozen
     battery is useless and there is a strong likelihood it is no longer serviceable. A qualified maintainer can
     determine if the battery is serviceable.

     3-66. If the maintainer suspects a battery is frozen (or with a freezing external temperature and discharged),
     maintainers should do the following:

          • Ascertain that the battery is both frozen and discharged.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  45


---

Chapter 3

          • Move the battery to an area designated by the maintenance shop for gel battery warming to slow the
                sulfation process. The battery might be recoverable.

          • If the frozen battery was part of a set, keep the whole set together.
          • Record that the batteries were pulled from service as a set, and which ones were found to have been

                frozen.
          • Let a qualified mechanic identify which batteries to return to service. Unrepairable batteries should

                be removed from service and sent to the supply support activity.

     3-67. Dispose of unserviceable batteries according to Army regulations. This type of loss is avoidable by
     performing regular PMCS and keeping the batteries properly charged.

Absorbed Glass Mat

     3-68. AGM batteries are a type of sealed lead-acid battery featuring a fiberglass mat situated between the
     plates. The fibers that compose the fine glass mat suspend and support the electrolyte but do not absorb, nor
     are affected by the acidic nature of the electrolyte. AGM batteries are characterized by a superior resistance
     to vibration and shock due to their construction. Packing the plates together with a glass mat reduces plate
     movement and vibration to nearly zero, making these batteries very rugged. Since glass mats are not totally
     saturated, the liquid does not expand to cause plate or case damage.

     3-69. AGM lead-acid batteries offer the best price point in the VRLA variety. These batteries provide much
     larger capacity in a smaller size and can be mounted on their side and shipped using standard shipping
     processes. A typical AGM battery can last 12 to 18 months in storage without being attended to, but some
     manufacturers claim that an AGM battery can last up to two years.

     3-70. AGM batteries are not nearly as susceptible to sulfation compared to their flooded VLA counterparts.
     AGM batteries can freeze when stored in a discharged state, but a fully charged one can withstand
     temperatures as low as -70°F (-57°C). If discharged, the same battery can freeze at 10°F (-12°C). Properly
     maintained AGM batteries are capable of a cyclical 80% depth of discharge without damage. In comparison,
     automotive flooded VLA batteries can only endure a 20% depth of discharge before damage can occur.

     3-71. AGM battery features make them a good choice for the applications below:
          • Tactical vehicles and generator sets are prone to experience shock and vibration routinely during
                transport operations.
          • These batteries are designed for deep discharge applications and can provide back up and “silent
                watch” power.
          • Use AGM batteries for applications where acid electrolyte leakage is not acceptable under any
                circumstances. These batteries will not leak electrolyte even when punctured or cracked. They are
                also not classified as “hazardous” in contrast to flooded VLA batteries.
          • Suitable for cold temperature operations (below 32°F [0°C]) when charged and maintained properly.

COMMUNICATIONS ELECTRONIC BATTERIES

     3-72. The communications electronics category represents many battery types used in a vast number of Army
     and joint systems. Communications electronics batteries refer to any type of non-rechargeable, rechargeable,
     dry cell, and wet-cell batteries. The applications are too numerous to mention here, but they include
     everything from flashlights to aircraft radios. Communications electronics batteries consist of various
     electrochemical systems, each with its own unique response to cold temperatures. Some battery chemistries
     are better suited for cold weather operations than others. Typically, cold weather climatic limitations are
     addressed in individual equipment TMs. Most batteries perform to their specifications at a room temperature
     of 68°F (20°C), or in temperate weather.

     3-73. Like other type of batteries, communications electronics battery performance also degrades at low
     temperatures. Internal resistance increases as temperatures lower, rendering them less able to provide their
     rated output current. The resistance is the decreasing speed of chemical interactions that acquire or release
     energy as the temperature decreases. In addition, cold temperatures reduce the battery’s energy storage
     capacity. The capacity decrease depends on the battery chemistry. For example, a given battery’s capacity
     can drop to about 50% at 0°F (-18°C) compared to warmer ambient temperatures. For example, if the
     communications electronics battery provides power to a load for 10 hours at 68°F (20°C), it would only

46         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                        Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

power the same load for only 5 hours at 0°F (-18°C). Warming a battery to room temperature will help restore
much of the battery’s capacity and performance. Any battery should be treated with care. Issues include the
following:

     • Communications electronics batteries are composed of “dry cells”, but they still contain caustic
          electrolytes (such as potassium or sodium hydroxide). They are corrosive and can cause chemical
          burns.

     • Only use the charger intended for a specific rechargeable battery. Universal chargers in the DOD
          inventory are designed to charge different battery types simultaneously. Follow the charger’s TM.

     • Never attempt to charge a non-rechargeable battery; it can ignite or explode.
     • Protect battery terminals from short circuiting on a metal surface or on other batteries (applies to

          coin cells). If short circuited, batteries can cause burns, explode, or ignite flammable materials.
     • Do not attempt to disassemble batteries; the materials inside are corrosive and some are toxic.
     • Always follow the warning and caution statements for batteries in the end item’s TM.

                                            WARNING

               Do not cut, puncture, open, or otherwise mutilate
               communications electronics batteries.

               Do not subject batteries to fire; they can ignite or explode.

               Do not use CO2 directed into a battery compartment to effect
               cooling.

3-74. Batteries are prone to leaking potassium hydroxide or sodium hydroxide, which are used as the
electrolyte in various types of batteries. These hydroxide-based electrolytes are caustic, highly corrosive
agents that can cause respiratory, eye and skin irritation. Assume any communications electronics battery
contains corrosive and/or flammable materials and treat accordingly. If exposure occurs in a cold situation,
treat and seek medical attention. To reduce the risk of battery electrolyte leakage or minimize
contamination—

     • Store batteries in a dry place.
     • Never attempt to recharge a disposable battery.
     • Do not mix different battery types in the same device.
     • Replace all the batteries at the same time as a group for equipment with multiple batteries.
     • Remove batteries before storage of devices.
     • Use a CO2 agent fire extinguisher in case of fire. CO2 is an acceptable fire extinguishing agent for

          hydroxide-based electrolyte.
     • Follow first aid instructions when any caustic substance has spilled on personnel. If batteries show

          signs of leaking, use rubber gloves, rubber apron and protective goggles.
     • Provide careful handling of any lithium-ion type battery.

3-75. For planning purposes, the following procedures for conserving batteries in cold weather environments
apply to operators, maintainers, and stock control handlers:

     • Dry batteries (non-rechargeable types) must be stored at temperatures above 10°F (-12°C) and must
          be warmed gradually, either with body or vehicle heat, before use.

     • In subzero weather (32°F [0°C]), additional battery chargers (especially float chargers) must be on
          hand to meet heavy requirements for rechargeable battery maintenance.

     • Supply controllers should expect battery stocks (both non-rechargeable and rechargeable) to need
          replenishment more often.

     • While in storage (on the vehicle, platform, in the equipment, or on the shelf), pay particular attention
          to the unique proprieties of the specific battery type.

     • While in storage or when not in use, automatic float chargers should be used to keep rechargeable
          batteries fully charged.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  47


---

Chapter 3

NON-RECHARGEABLE DRY CELL BATTERIES

     3-76. Also known as “primary batteries,” these batteries do not draw energy (recharge) from another energy
     source. They are very useful in situations where recharging is impractical or impossible, including combat,
     rescue missions, implanted medical devices, and other austere situations (MIL-PRF-49471). High specific
     energy, long storage times, and instant readiness give primary batteries a unique advantage over other power
     sources. They can be carried to remote locations and used immediately, even after extended storage.

     3-77. Advances in non-rechargeable dry cell battery technology has resulted in dozens of different
     chemistries for making different types of batteries. Despite scientific advances, most are not cost effective
     for commercial or military use. Batteries today are patterned on legacy technology called a voltaic cell. Any
     primary battery’s performance can be affected by excessive heat, cold, high humidity, and high altitude. The
     battery casing is crucial for protecting its performance. It helps shield the battery from these environmental
     factors. Still, almost none of the new battery chemistries (especially those that use water in the electrolyte)
     offer any distinct advantage in resisting the cold-induced slowdown of the electrochemical energy generation
     process.

     3-78. Traditional wisdom suggests keeping batteries warm by putting them under a coat or jacket. While this
     may work for flashlight batteries, most military equipment today uses rather large battery packs that do not
     fit under clothing. Consequently, unit maintainers will have to think or plan for ways to keep primary batteries
     viable for equipment use in a wet or cold field environment.

LITHIUM BATTERY

     3-79. There are several different types of primary lithium batteries that are commonly used. In the consumer
     market, Li/CFX (poly carbon monofluoride) and Li/MnO2 (manganese dioxide) batteries are found in
     cameras, calculators, and watches. In the military community, Li/SO2 (sulfur dioxide) batteries are used in
     high-power radios (MIL-PRF-32271 [series]). Li/SOCl2 (thionyl chloride) and LiI2 (lithium iodine) are
     commonly utilized in industrial and medical applications.

     3-80. Lithium sulfur dioxide (Li/SO2) is the most common primary battery chemistry used by the military,
     followed by Lithium manganese dioxide (Li/MnO2). They have an estimated shelf life of 10 to 15 years at
     68°F (20°C). Lithium batteries can usually provide a somewhat satisfactory power performance down to
     about -4°F (-20°C) with a 40-60% decrease in battery life. Performance is greatly reduced at temperatures
     below that. For planning purposes:

          • Li/SO2 batteries have an operating temperature range of -58°F to 140°F (-50°C to 60°C).
          • Li/MnO2 is not as cold tolerant, with an operating range of -22°F to 140°F (-30°C to 60°C). Below

                that range, the lithium electrolyte stops functioning. Above that, it will catch on fire.

     3-81. Lithium batteries begin aging as soon as they are manufactured. Yet, lithium batteries have a low self-
     discharge rate and long shelf life, maintaining over 85% of their charge, even after 10 to 15 years in storage.

ALKALINE BATTERY, NON-RECHARGEABLE

     3-82. Better than almost any other primary battery, alkaline manganese dioxide (/MnO2) batteries resist
     degradation of performance in the cold. Their energy output remains constant and undiminished by the cold
     until they freeze. Alkaline batteries freeze somewhere around -4°F (-20°C). They have an estimated shelf life
     of 5-7 years at 68°F (20°C).

     3-83. Many localities around the world consider spent alkaline batteries to be hazardous waste. They must
     be disposed of according to theater or installation policy, which follows host-nation, federal, state, and local
     environmental laws and regulations.

DISCONTINUED BATTERIES

     3-84. Discontinued battery types may be required to support COTS devices. Carbon Zinc batteries and
     Mercury Oxide batteries (both non-rechargeable) have been discontinued from inventory and are classified
     as hazardous waste.

48  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

RECHARGEABLE DRY CELL BATTERIES

     3-85. The technology behind power storage and dry cell batteries is constantly evolving, and the technical
     specifics in this TM will become obsolete as battery technology improves. Operators and maintainers should
     rely primarily on the item-specific TM and check current battery technology, trends, and applications.

     3-86. Military acquisition standards for rechargeable batteries require storage temperatures that may vary
     between the limits of -4°F (-20°C) to 122°F (50°C). The recommended storage temperature for most batteries
     is around 59°F (15°C). Lead-acid batteries require maintenance. They should always be kept at full charge
     when in storage. On the other hand, nickel and lithium-based batteries should be stored at around a 40%
     SOC. Partial discharge of these batteries (ranging from above 35%, to as much as 80% of their total capacity)
     is acceptable. Following these guidelines will keep nickel and lithium-based batteries operational while
     minimizing capacity loss due to self-discharge during storage. Nickel-based batteries can be stored in a fully
     discharged state with no apparent side effect. However, priming becomes necessary if the voltage drops
     below 1 volt per cell. Lithium-ion batteries cannot dip below 2 volts in each cell for any length of time, or
     they will need to be disposed of properly.

     3-87. For thermal shock (resilience to physical damage at extreme temperatures) in the range of -75°F (-59°C)
     to 167°F (75°C), batteries are not expected to bulge, crack, break, leak electrolyte, rupture, burn, or explode
     due to varying rates of thermal expansion or contraction of cell and battery components when subjected to
     thermal shock temperature cycling.

     3-88. Different battery chemistries often demand different storage conditions. Rechargeable military batteries
     are capable of more than 3 years of warehouse storage without any maintenance during storage. To determine
     the age of a battery, the military uses a four-digit number to represent the month/year of manufacture (for
     example, 01/25 indicates January 2025). Knowing the age of a battery helps estimate the remaining battery
     life, even for a rechargeable.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not store a fully charged or fully discharged lithium battery.

GENERAL OPERATION OF RECHARGEABLE BATTERIES

     3-89. The service life of rechargeable batteries is affected by several factors: ambient operating temperatures,
     physical use and abuse, and depth of discharge. In general, rechargeable batteries will experience a longer
     service life at shallow depths of discharge. In other words, a battery that is discharged to around 40% to 50%
     of its capacity before recharging will last longer compared to one that is consistently discharged to near zero
     capacity. However, the depth of discharge depends on the application’s (load) duty cycle and whether it is
     practical to recharge the battery well before discharging it completely. Using only a small fraction of the
     battery’s capacity before recharging would require more batteries for a given application. The strategy of
     recharging after a shallow depth of discharge is not always practical.

     3-90. In cold climates, low ambient temperatures can increase the demand for batteries. The military
     performance specification for rechargeable batteries specifies they must withstand no less than 224
     discharge/charge cycles without significant performance degradation. A “cycle” refers to a fully charged
     battery being drained to almost no charge (without reversing polarity) and then being brought back to full
     charge. The number of cycles a rechargeable battery can withstand depends on various factors including
     battery chemistry and usage conditions. For example, rechargeable communications electronics batteries can
     often withstand over 500 cycles.

     3-91. Though new technology and better chemistries promise broader temperature ranges and longer life
     expectancies, the above parameters represent the most reliable planning information. When using
     rechargeable batteries daily in cold temperatures, equipment maintainers should expect a lifespan of less than
     two years for most batteries. Most equipment designers prefer lithium batteries due to their higher power
     capacity. However, these batteries have a shorter life expectancy that starts declining from the time of
     manufacture. As a result, the Army has discontinued including batteries with replacement items. Even if the

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  49


---

Chapter 3

     equipment sat in a warehouse for years, it is important to ensure the battery is fresh. Planners should expect
     to order fresh lithium batteries along with the related devices.

     3-92. Some rechargeable battery packs have control circuitry inside their cases to control their performance.
     Most rechargeable battery packs must be at temperatures above 30°F (-1°C) before the control circuits will
     allow the recharging process of the individual cells within the case. Some cases have heating pads that engage
     during a low-temperature recharge. The item-specific TM will identify temperature ranges for recharging
     and should indicate if the case has a built-in heating element. If the battery doesn’t have a functioning heating
     element, temperatures below freezing will obstruct the chemical reaction processes for recharging.

     3-93. If a rechargeable battery discharges into reverse polarity, it can heat up and may even catch fire.
     Normally, the control circuitry will detect unusual heat and shut down the battery pack before this occurs.
     Operators can check for localized hot spots on a discharged battery pack to ascertain if the pack is failing. If
     a localized hot-spot is detected during the recharging process, deadline the battery and turn it in to the unit
     maintenance activity for evaluation, servicing, or disposal. This during-operations check is very important to
     prevent fire damage.

     3-94. Rechargeable batteries are divided by Army specifications (MIL-PRF-32383A) into the following four
     categories:

          • Type I – Batteries with a nickel-cadmium electrochemical system. Nickel-cadmium is now the older
                technology and are cheap because they suffer “memory” problems. The golden rule with nickel-
                cadmium batteries is to completely discharge them every time before charging them again, that way
                they will always be at their best performance. nickel-cadmium batteries can be cycled around 1000
                times or charged once a day for around 3 years before they become unserviceable.

          • Type II – Batteries with a nickel-metal hydride (NiMH or Ni-MH) electrochemical system. These
                can be partially discharged and charged as many as 1000 cycles and provide full capacity. They
                have much larger capacity than nickel-cadmium batteries.

          • Type III – Batteries with a lithium-ion electrochemical system. These batteries solve both the
                problems associated with the other two types of batteries (full voltage and no memory problems).

          • Type IV – Batteries with a lithium-polymer (LiPo) electrochemical system.

     3-95. Army specifications for Type I and II rechargeable device batteries (MIL-PRF-32052) can operate
     above normal temperature range of -4°F (-20°C) to 122°F (50°C). Type III has broader temperature range
     specifications of -22°F (-30°C) to 131°F (55°C).

TYPE I NICKEL-CADMIUM BATTERIES, RECHARGEABLE

     3-96. These batteries are made in a wide range of sizes and capacities, from portable sealed types
     interchangeable with carbon zinc dry cells, to large ventilated wet cells used for standby power and mobility
     power. Nickel-cadmium batteries are used in a variety of Army equipment including range finders, night
     vision sights, and ground surveillance radars. These batteries can perform at their rated capacity when the
     ambient temperature of the battery is in the range of approximately -70°F (-57°C) to 90° F (32°C). When
     properly maintained, they are very effective low-temperature power sources (Figure 3-6). However, below
     40°F, these batteries need to be warmed to help maintain their current for meeting energy demands. At 25°F
     (-4°C), these batteries lose about 30% of their useful life or service time before needing a recharge.

50  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

                   Figure 3-6. Nickel-cadmium power capacity at various temperatures

     3-97. When nickel-cadmium batteries are first pulled from storage, it is important to prime them. This process
     involves connecting the batteries to establish a habitual relationship and trickle charging them for 16 to 24
     hours when they are new or after a long period of storage. This allows the cells to adjust to each other and to
     bring them to an equal charge level. A slow charge also helps to redistribute the electrolyte to eliminate dry
     spots on the separator that might have developed by gravitation. Follow the item specific TM instructions for
     this procedure.

     3-98. Nickel-cadmium batteries have what is called, battery memory. To get maximum effectiveness when
     charging nickel-cadmium batteries, one must destroy the charging memory characteristic of the battery. For
     example, if a nickel-cadmium battery is continually called upon to delivery an average of only 25% of its
     capacity before it is recharged, it will eventually “memorize” this fact and become incapable of supplying
     the remaining 70% of its capacity. Operators must condition the battery to get past the memory. Conditioning
     entails discharging nickel-cadmium batteries down to their lowest operating levels, then recharge them fully,
     then discharge them again to their lowest operating levels, then once again recharge them fully to destroy the
     memory. Follow the item specific TM instructions for this procedure.

TYPE II NICKEL-METAL HYDRIDE BATTERIES, RECHARGEABLE

                                                 CAUTION

                    Nickel-cadmium chargers will overcharge NiMH. (NiMH chargers can
                    charge nickel-cadmium, but not the other way).

     3-99. The NiMH or Ni-MH battery is similar to the nickel-cadmium. A NiMH battery can have two to three
     times the capacity of an equivalent size nickel-cadmium battery. The significant disadvantage of NiMH
     batteries is the high rate of self-discharge when in storage or not in use. They have a life expectancy of two
     to five years. Battery memory might be a problem if the battery has a cadmium electrode, otherwise it is not
     an issue.

     3-100. At low temperatures, the NiMH provides a consistent level of power, all the way through discharge.
     At freezing, NiMH batteries only provide 80% of the capacity, where at -4°F (-20°C) these batteries only
     provide 20% of their normal power capacity or service time before needing a recharge (Figure 3-7 on page
     52).

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  51


---

Chapter 3

               Figure 3-7. Nickel–metal hydride power capacity at various temperatures

RECHARGEABLE LITHIUM BATTERIES (GENERAL)

     3-101. Lithium rechargeable batteries are the most common type of rechargeable batteries found in small
     electrical devices. Army specifications (MIL-PRF-32383) include the following:

          • Discharges energy over a temperature range of -22°F (-30°C) to 131°F (55°C).
          • Thermal shock resistance (the ability to avoid temperature caused physical damage) ranges

                from -75°F (-59°C) to 167°F (75°C).
          • Rechargeable range limits are between 40°F (4°C) to 110°F (43°C).

             Note. Never attempt to charge lithium batteries below 41°F (5°C). Doing so can cause plating to
             form on the anode and lead to a permanent loss of capacity.

     3-102. Most lithium battery electrolytes freeze at approximately -40°F (-40°C). Freezing can damage a cell
     by damaging material bonds or causing dendrites (crystallization) in the electrolyte components that can
     pierce a separator element. Though new technology and better chemistries promise broader temperature
     ranges, the above parameters represent the most reliable planning figures.

     3-103. Modern lithium batteries do not contain solid lithium metal. Instead, they use lithium ions suspended
     in a polymer and electrolyte gel. Older batteries with solid lithium metal could overheat after being used
     repeatedly. Today’s lithium-ion batteries can have a variety of materials for their positive and negative
     electrodes and have varying energy density and voltage.

     3-104. These batteries have the following drawbacks:
          • Limited battery life. They begin degrading as soon as they are manufactured, regardless of whether
                they are sitting in storage or being used. The rate of capacity degradation for a maintained battery
                (having been properly charged) is less than 5% per year. If stored without charge, they can be dead
                in a year. Their effective lifespan is about two or five years.
          • Computer chip monitor. A battery pack needs a small computer (microchip) to manage the battery’s
                energy charge and temperature. Some are built into the battery pack; some are regulated by circuitry
                in the device.
          • Sensitive to high temperatures. High temperatures increase the rate of battery life degradation.
                Lithium batteries should be kept cool. Aging will take its toll much faster at higher temperatures.
                The high temperatures found in vehicles may cause lithium batteries to degrade rapidly.
          • Requires a constant positive charge. Lithium batteries need to maintain a positive charge. The
                battery can become ruined if cell voltage drops too low. Then a subsequent charging may result in
                thermal runaway.

52         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                              Batteries and Electrical Energy Storage

          • Flammable. If the battery overheats, it can burst into flames. Although this is uncommon, it does
                happen.

TYPE III LITHIUM-ION BATTERIES, RECHARGEABLE

     3-105. Type III lithium-ion batteries are distinguishable by their hard case enclosures (typical of any liquid
     filled battery). These batteries are filled with an electrolyte gelatin, or a porous separator that is soaked with
     electrolyte. Never open a lithium-ion battery's casing. These batteries contain safety devices that protect the
     cells inside from abuse. If damaged, these safety devices may allow the battery to ignite or explode.

                                                 WARNING

                    Lithium-ion batteries can rupture, ignite, or explode when
                    exposed to high temperatures. They should not be stored in a
                    vehicle during hot weather. Short circuiting a lithium-ion battery
                    can cause it to ignite or explode.

TYPE IV LITHIUM POLYMER BATTERIES, RECHARGEABLE

     3-106. Lithium polymer batteries operate on the same electrochemistry as the lithium-ion battery. It is
     distinguished by using a dry electrolyte separator material that is only a millimeter thick. It allows the ions
     to pass through but keeps the cathode and anode plates separated. The dry electrolyte medium resists leakage
     and can be packaged in soft and light weight polymer pouches. This type of packaging results in a battery
     that is 20% lighter than the equivalent Type III lithium-ion battery. The drawback is the dry electrolyte
     separator’s lower conductivity due to the electrolyte’s molecular stability that impedes the movement
     (compared to the gelatin of a type III) of chemical interactions that release energy.
