                                                 Chapter 4

          Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

    This chapter is focused on the properties of petroleum-based fuel oils, lubricants, grease
    products, and solvents. These four products are available as crude oil-based products, synthetic
    oil-derivative products, and bio-synthetic oils and lubricants. This chapter discussed each of
    the four products.

MAINTENANCE CONSIDERATIONS

     4-1. Both maintainers and operators must understand the limitations of petroleum, oils, and lubricant products
     in cold or frigid environments. They must know where the point of non-functionality will occur and how to
     minimize adverse effects.

             Note. Performance characteristics of the POL products presented in this manual are general in
             nature, for planning purposes or general education on the subject. The true POL product
             performance is subject to evolving technologies. A product’s manufacturer will have to be
             contacted for the actual low temperature threshold performance characteristics if it cannot be
             found in manufacture’s safety data sheets documents.

     4-2. Commanders and maintenance leaders should establish a trigger event or turnover point when POL
     products will be transitioned to support operations in cold weather. Units which are otherwise not originally
     programed for a cold region deployment should set a trigger event as part of cold weather contingency plans
     and policies. Units should identify what resources would be needed for cold weather deployment and where
     they can be rapidly procured. Once the trigger event or turnover point is established, maintenance leaders
     should develop a transition program for the unit’s equipment and vehicle fleet. Then once deployed, units
     operating in a cold environment can expect a dramatic increase in POL and fuel requirements due to changes
     in POL properties, movement difficulty, extended idling, and heating requirements. Prior planning is critical
     to mission success.

     4-3. Maintenance Soldiers and equipment operators need to understand how the physical and performance
     properties of POL products change as their temperatures become increasingly colder. Always consult the
     appropriate TM or LO for any piece of equipment. Identify any special fuel and lubricants that must be used
     to prevent freezing or congealing. Different grades of antifreezes and hydraulic fluids are usually necessary
     for cold equipment operation. Although crude oil-based petroleum products are economical and easy to
     procure, synthetic oil-derivative products are more resilient over a broader temperature range.

     4-4. Synthetic oils and lubricants are the preferred choice in cold situations. They are made from pure
     component chemicals that are free of the impurities or molecular inconsistencies found in crude oil. The
     range between flash points and pour points (discussed in Appendix D) is broader for synthetics compared to
     conventional oil. The added resilience to high temperatures results in synthetics having two to three times
     greater mileage or extended lubrication life. 0W/20 oil qualified to MIL-PRF-32626 standards is capable of
     pumping through a crankcase at -67°F (-55°C) and may still flow at even lower temperatures. Conventional
     oils usually freeze into a gelatinous solid at those temperatures. Synthetic oils can be mixed with conventional
     oils, but then the gap between the upper and lower lubricating limitations becomes governed by the lower
     capabilities of the two products. When synthetic oils are mixed with crude oils, the crude oil properties
     dominate.

     4-5. The DOD tests POL products for extreme temperatures down to -65°F (-54°C). However, personnel
     using these products may need to know—

          • How safe is it to use (most of which can NOT be found on data sheets)?
          • How cold can it get and still work normally (information which only the manufacturers or qualifying

                DOD activities have)?
          • What procedures and precautions are needed to keep a POL product working in cold environments?

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  55


---

Chapter 4

     4-6. These questions are usually answered by the results of four tests:
          • The flash/fire point is a commercial or manufacturer’s test to determine the lowest temperature
                above which the application of a flame will cause fuel or lubricant vapors to ignite (flash point) and
                sustain burning for five seconds (fire point). POL products with higher flash and fire points exhibit
                more stable volatility characteristics and are safer to use and transport.
          • The pour point test determines the lowest temperature at which it still flows (before crystallization
                [freezing]). The lower a lubricant or diesel fuel’s pour point, the better functional performance it
                provides in low-temperature service.
          • The freeze point test for fuels is the lowest temperature at which fuel remains free of solid
                hydrocarbon crystals. Below this temperature, the fuel begins to turn into a waxy slush (which is
                well before the whole substance hardens). At this temperature, fuel flow disruptions could be
                expected to occur with equipment.
          • The cloud point is the temperature at which paraffinic wax crystals start to appear. It is the lower
                operating limit for diesel fuel. The cloud point of diesel fuel varies with location and season. The
                cloud point of the fuel should be below the expected lowest ambient temperature. Diesel fuels with
                too high a cloud point may be blended with similar or kerosene fuels (1-K kerosene, JP-5, JP-8)
                with lower cloud points to obtain a usable product. Blending for cloud point also takes into
                consideration the locality sulfur requirements as EPA-mandated sulfur maximums cannot be
                exceeded.
          • The viscosity test determines the fluidity at various temperatures where oil flows and properly
                lubricates in a system. As temperatures drop to their lower limits, the oil’s stiffness interferes with
                lubrication or hydraulic functions in a machine or gear box. This test is also applied to fuels.

             Note: For viscosity, the specific test result is not as important to the operator as is the availability
             through supply of a more fluid low-viscosity POL products. Operators must use the TMs to direct
             the conversion to substitute lubricants for lower temperatures.

PETROLEUM AND ENGINE FUEL PLANNING

     4-7. The DOD has adopted the SFP as outlined in AR 70-12. This policy has the goal of focusing on a single
     kerosene-based fuel for use in all equipment. This is considerably easier to manage than multiple fuels,
     allowing the functions of fuel storage, transportation, and distribution to be tailored for maximum efficiency.

     4-8. In coordination with the respective Service policy guidance, the theater Joint Petroleum Office sets
     policy for any deviation from the SFP. In expeditionary situations units may have to deviate from JP-8 and
     rely upon secondary fuels or commercial sources with multiple petroleum products whose performance can
     be seriously affected by the cold. When authorized to deviate from the SFP, the order of preference is as
     follows:

          • JP-8 (military detail specification [MIL-DTL] -83133 Turbine Fuel, Aviation, Kerosene Type, JP-
                8 [NATO F-34 and F-35]) outside CONUS, or F-24 within CONUS (the standard, not a deviation).
                ▪ F-24 (STANAG 3747, Guide Specifications [Minimum Quality Standards]).
                ▪ F-24, F-27, F-34, F-37, F-40 and F-44 (AFLP 3747) for Aviation Turbine Fuel.

          • JP-5 (MIL- DTL-5624 Turbine Fuel, Aviation, Grades JP-5 [NATO F-44]).
          • Commercial jet fuels with additives:

                ▪ Fuel System Icing Inhibitor, (MIL-DTL-85470 Inhibitor Icing, Fuel System, High Flash NATO
                     Code Number S-1745).

                ▪ Static Dissipater Additive (MIL-DTL-83133).
                ▪ Corrosion Inhibitor/Lubricity Improver (MIL-PRF-25017 Inhibitor, Corrosion Lubricity

                     Improver, Fuel Soluble [NATO S-1747]).
          • Commercial jet fuels without additives (limited to one tank of fuel without additives, ground use

                only).
          • Commercial Diesel (recommended to consume diesel fuel within 6 months).

     4-9. Command planning staff and maintainers should familiarize with what to expect from the assortment of
     military standard and commercial fuels they may encounter. The information in this manual on petroleum

56  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                      Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

     and fuels is intended to be limited in scope to cold environments. Further fuel planning considerations can
     be found in Appendix D, under the listed fuel type.

     4-10. Safety is of utmost importance when dealing with petroleum products, given their toxicity,
     explosiveness, flammability, and potential environmental impact. Fuel handlers and equipment operators
     need to be familiar with fuel characteristics and follow prescribed safety precautions found in equipment-
     specific TMs and facility, military, or governmental regulations to protect personnel, equipment, and the
     environment.

     4-11. The freezing point of fuel is the temperature where the fuel first begins to turn into a waxy slush. In
     petroleum fuel, the crystallization process does not solidify the fluid all at once at a set temperature in the
     way that water does. Operators focus on the fuel’s freeze point temperature (as different from the point of
     solidification), because it is the point were problems getting the fuel to flow through the fuel system begin to
     occur.

             Note. It is important for the commander and purchasing agents of COTS equipment to consider
             the type of fuel needed to operate it. It is best for COTS to function on SFP fuels common to the
             unit’s fleet. This will make it easier to obtain fuel for COTS equipment and avoid causing supply
             to carry a larger list of fuels.

FUEL STORAGE

     4-12. Water in fuel can cause serious problems so it is important to remove water accumulation as soon as
     possible. Daily inspections of water content in vehicle water filter separators and draining will go a long way
     to prevent problems. Engines may experience operational issues even when operating at temperatures above
     the freezing point if fuel other than JP-8 fuel is used. For example, diesel fuel is a seasonal fuel that can
     gelatinize if used in the wrong season, as the cloud point of fuel varies across seasons.

                                                 WARNING

                    Fuel flowing over a surface generates static that can cause a
                    spark unless means are provided to ground the electricity. A
                    metallic contact between the dispensing tank and the container
                    being filled must be provided to ensure an effective bond. This
                    hazard is particularly increased in cold, dry air.

     4-13. Moisture and condensation can contaminate fuel and cause many issues. Moisture may come from
     snow entering the fuel, condensation from a partially filled container being taken outside from a warmer
     environment, or from fuel consumption during regular operation. Follow these precautions to help prevent
     fuel contamination:

          • Store barrels with outlet end slightly higher than the other end to allow sediment and water to settle
                out (Figure 4-1 on page 58). Avoid upright storage of barrels to keep water and snow from
                accumulating around the fill cap. The last few gallons that remain in the barrel should be dumped
                into collecting barrels where foreign accumulation can settle, and the usable fuel can be salvaged.
                Make sure all containers are thoroughly dry, clean, and rust-free before storing fuel in them.

          • Wipe all snow or ice from dispensing equipment and around the fill cap of fuel tanks before
                removing the cap. After filling the tank, replace the cap securely.

          • Always keep the fuel tank filled to the proper markings. Refuel only to the expansion marking
                immediately after halting to reduce condensation in the fuel tank. The more fuel there is in the tank,
                the smaller the volume of air from which moisture can be condensed.

             Note. Secondary containment must be used under barrels to collect any spillage and to prevent
             contamination of the soil.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  57


---

Chapter 4

                                              Figure 4-1. POL barrel storage

     4-14. Arctic fuels for gasoline or diesel engines are developed and selected to obtain the proper atomization
     necessary for a combustible fuel-air mixture. It is essential that units be provided with JP-8 or appropriate
     cold-weather-grade fuel if other that JP-8 use has been approved. Standard JP-8 fuel has a minimum
     specification requirement of -52°F (-47°C), which ensures gelling will usually not occur at extreme-cold or
     even hazardous-cold temperatures.

     4-15. Where commercial fuels must be used, cold weather calls for the use of a special grade of diesel fuel
     called DF-1. Diesel fuel gets thicker and cloudier at lower temperatures. Under extreme conditions, it can
     gelatinize and refuse to flow at all. Diesel fuel grade 1-D should be used in abnormally low operating
     temperatures.

FUEL SYSTEMS

     4-16. For a satisfactory start, engine fuel must produce a combustible mixture with air. Atomization, which
     increases the rate of vaporization of the fuel to produce combustible mixtures, is adversely affected by low
     temperatures. Engines using commercial diesel fuels are particularly difficult to start in cold weather. Many
     fuels, such as DF-2, contain waxes that congeal at temperatures below 0°F (-18°C). If this occurs, the filter
     will clog, and the fuel will not flow. Diesel fuel grade 1-D is designed for use in cold regions. Military
     specified JP-8 is the preferred fuel for all systems.

             Note. Using additive in greater proportions than two pints of additive to 40 gallons of fuel results
             in poor engine performance and possible engine damage.

     4-17. Water can accumulate in tanks, drums, containers, and fuel pumps due to the condensation of water
     vapor from the air. At low temperatures, this water forms ice crystals that clog fuel lines, fuel filters, fuel
     pumps, injector nozzles, and carburetor jets.

     4-18. Fuel filters should be cleaned or changed at frequent intervals. Conditions may require daily cleaning
     under field use. Take special care to ensure 5-gallon fuel cans used in refueling are clean and serviceable.
     Fuel nozzles may contain screens designed to catch solid contaminates; clean them daily. Nozzles used to
     refuel aircrafts are required to have #100 mesh screens.

     4-19. Remove and service all engine and air compressor air cleaner elements. Dry-type air cleaners, both felt
     and paper, should either be cleaned with low pressure air or replaced.

     4-20. Check for any indication of fuel leaks. Trace all leaks to their source and correct or replace parts. Follow
     the TMs for proper maintenance and operation in cold weather.

FUEL SYSTEM MAINTENANCE

     4-21. Add Fuel System Icing Inhibitor (Diethylene-Glycol Monomethyl Ether) to diesel fuels if necessary
     due to water in fuel systems and when using diesel fuels as approved. It will prevent water from freezing, but
     it will not convert the fuel to an arctic or low temperature fuel.

     4-22. Order the appropriate grade of fuel for low temperatures. Mix additive with the diesel fuel prior to
     refueling, normally at a ratio of one pint of additive to 40 gallons of fuel.

58         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                      Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

                                                 WARNING

                    Fuel handlers and maintainers must be extra careful to avoid
                    getting fuel on exposed skin. Spilled fuel has a super cooling
                    effect upon the skin and may cause rapid frostbite.

     4-23. Satisfactory cold weather performance of fuel-related systems depends on careful servicing. Proper
     maintenance by unit mechanics prevents many malfunctions and failures that would otherwise occur in sub-
     freezing temperatures. Conduct the following maintenance checks during cold weather:

          • The throttle controls may operate with difficulty at low temperatures. If the engine does not respond
                properly to the operation of controls, check for loose or broken controls and for loose or broken
                control linkages or cables. Adjust linkages or replace defective parts as required according to the
                applicable TM. Clean wires with environmentally safe cleaning solvents and thoroughly dry them.
                Newer fuel injected systems are computer-controlled electronically, with no mechanical links to
                control throttle speed. These systems can be found on the palletized load system, family of medium
                tactical vehicles (FMTV), heavy equipment tractors (Heavy Equipment Transport Systems), and
                other tactical and combat vehicles. Operators must become thoroughly familiar with cold weather
                starting procedures for these vehicles. For example, when starting and operating these vehicles, there
                is little or no throttle response until the engine temperature sensors send information to the computer
                that the engine is warm enough to operate. Other steps are also required, such as the use of an exhaust
                restrictor for the FMTV when starting in the cold.

          • Inspect fuel filters for good condition and replace contaminated elements. Dispose of unserviceable
                fuel filters in an environmentally safe manner as outlined in the unit operating procedures. Clean
                metal disc-type filters with dry-cleaning solvent, dry, and install. Make sure there are no leaks.

          • Drain fuel filters at the end of each day of operation. Do not assume that filters are dry if nothing
                flows from the drain cock. If water is present, it could have frozen solid overnight. Drain filters into
                an approved and appropriately marked and labeled container and turn in according to unit operating
                procedures for disposal.

          • Check the diesel fuel injection pump (including transfer pump) to ensure that it is in good condition,
                correctly assembled, and securely mounted, and that connections are not leaking.

          • Check diesel fuel nozzles and lines to ensure good condition.
          • Check whether the fuel gauge is operating and registering the amount of fuel in the tank.
          • Identify any special host-nation disposal requirements in advance.

LUBRICATION OIL

     4-24. Non-fuel oils and lubricants are intended to reduce the wear and heat resulting in friction between
     moving parts. In normal conditions, the viscosity of lubricants creates a protective layer between the moving
     parts of an engine or weapon. The primary challenge is determining the proper lubricants that possess enough
     viscosity to protect working parts while remaining sufficiently fluid to allow the smooth operation of weapon
     systems, cold engine startups, gear shifts in transmissions, and clutch engagements.

             Note. The Army only qualifies SMPL for use in many diesel-powered vehicles and equipment.
             SMPL is a direct replacement for the former Single Common Powertrain Lubricant. The grades
             5W-20, 10W-30, and 10W-40 are for gasoline engine grades and are not for use in military
             equipment with diesel engines. In the event of an emergency where an engine oil intended for
             gasoline engines is used in a diesel engine, it is important to reduce drain interval by half. This is
             necessary as oils designed for gasoline engine blocks are incapable of retaining soot.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  59


---

Chapter 4

                                                 WARNING

                    If the lubricant (oil or grease) in a vehicle or weapon system has frozen
                    or solidified, the lubricant must be brought to a temperature high enough
                    to flow or be dissolved, removed, and replaced by a lubricant with better
                    low temperature properties.

     4-25. Severe driving cycles encompass a range of conditions including frequent short trips (particularly
     during cold weather); stop-and-go driving; cold start; and high-speed, high-load driving. Each of these
     driving cycles impacts the different types of lubrication mechanisms and ultimately promotes oil degradation.

     4-26. The lubrication required for any specific piece of Army equipment is specified in the LO or TM. The
     provisions of Army LOs are mandatory and followed unless the Department of the Army authorizes a
     deviation. LOs usually prescribe lubricants based on three anticipated temperature ranges:

          • 15°F (-9°C) and above.
          • 40°F (4°C) to -15°F (-26°C).
          • 40°F (4°C) to -65°F (-54°C).

     4-27. If a vehicle with temperate grade automatic transmission fluids moves into a cold weather area and
     becomes cold-soaked (when a vehicle’s hardware temperature drops as low as the existing freezing ambient
     temperature), shifting gears may occur very slowly or not at all. Additionally, seals may leak temporarily
     until the transmission has reached operating temperature. Trying to operate the vehicle at temperatures below
     the pour point of the transmission fluid may result in severe damage to the transmission.

     4-28. In cold weather, water and fuel can accumulate in the crankcase because the engine does not reach
     operating temperature quickly enough or maintain it at a high level long enough to cause evaporation. These
     contaminants can degrade the lubrication between moving parts. Additionally, accumulated water accelerates
     the oxidation of the base oil and quickly reduces the additive content. Water will also extract acids and
     additives out of the oil, trapping them and forming emulsions. Emulsions restrict oil flow by blocking
     passages in the oil gallery, which leads to premature wear on engine parts due to insufficient amounts of oil.
     By-products from oxidation will eventually lead to depletion of additives and cause corrosion on engine part
     surfaces.

     4-29. When fuel contaminates oil, it reduces the oil film thickness and leads to cylinder, ring, and piston
     wear. Then, compounds generated by oil oxidation may deposit on the piston rings and grooves, causing ring-
     sticking and promoting blow-by. Excessive blow-by will in turn decrease the engine power and increase fuel
     consumption.

VISCOSITY

     4-30. Viscosity refers to the thickness of a lubricant. Fluids with low viscosity are thinner and flow more
     easily. High viscosity fluids are thicker and have a greater resistance to flow. The thicker the fluid, the higher
     the viscosity. Cold weather increases the fluid viscosity of lubricants. Equipment operators should understand
     that in extreme environments, oils and greases can either thin out or thicken due to age, contamination, and
     temperature.

     4-31. Cold temperatures can increase a lubricant’s viscosity and push it outside of equipment acceptable
     operating tolerances. Maintainers will need to check the appropriate TM to identify equipment viscosity
     requirements and limitations for proper operation. Then, for environments where lubricants are operating at
     the margin of their limits, they will need to determine a method of identifying the threat of lubricant failure
     or expect an increase in broken parts.

     4-32. A significant reduction in viscosity (thinning of the lubricant) can result in—
          • Loss of oil film between moving parts causing excessive wear.
          • Increased mechanical friction causing excessive energy consumption.
          • Heat generation due to mechanical friction.
          • Internal or external leakage.

60         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                      Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

          • Increased sensitivity to particle contamination due to reduced oil film.
          • Oil film failure at high temperatures allowing parts to rub directly against each other.
          • Damage under loads, during startups, or coast downs.

     4-33. Too high a viscosity (thickening of the lubricant) can cause—
          • Excessive heat generation resulting in oil oxidation, sludge, and varnish build up.
          • Gaseous cavitation (air pockets) due to inadequate oil flow to pumps and bearings.
          • Lubrication starvation due to inadequate oil flow.
          • Oil whip in journal bearings.
          • Excess energy consumption to overcome thickened fluid resistance.
          • Poor air detrainment (regulation of air flow) or poor demulsibility (separation of water from oil).
          • Poor cold start pumpability.

ACCUMULATION OF SLUDGE IN LUBRICATING OIL

     4-34. Cold weather tends to prevent engines from reaching normal operating temperatures, increases the
     development of carbon in the engine, and increases oil dilution and condensation. These factors combine to
     create engine sludge (Figure 4-2). The Army Oil Analysis Program is the activity primarily responsible for
     identifying contaminated or degraded oil needing to be changed. To correct a sludge condition, drain oil
     while the engine is warm (not hot or cold-soaked) enough for oil to flow and refill with the appropriate
     lubricant.

     4-35. After operation, inspect the oil pan, valve cover, timing gear cover and gaskets, and external oil lines
     for oil leaks and correct as necessary. To protect the environment, drip pans must be placed under vehicles.
     Turn in all used oil according to host-nation, federal, state, and local environmental laws and regulations and
     unit operating procedures.

     4-36. The best way to prevent sludge buildup within a vehicle fleet is for the maintenance manager to ensure
     oil and filter changes occur according to the schedule provided in the TM or LO. Proactive participation in
     the Army Oil Analysis Program can also help to identify the propensity of an oil to produce sludge.

                  Figure 4-2. Engine sludge

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31                 61


---

Chapter 4

AUTOMOTIVE LUBRICANTS

     4-37. In low temperatures, conventional oil thickens and increases the fluid friction within an engine’s
     cylinders and bearings to the extent that it will not run. Using an appropriate oil in cold weather as prescribed
     by the LO (like OEA-30 or OE/HDO-SMPL) will help prevent issues during cold starts. OEA-30 and SMPL
     are synthetic lubricants specifically made for cold environments.

     4-38. The Army established SMPL as of 15 January 2016. It is now expressed under MIL-PRF-32626
     qualification standards. SMPL is a multipurpose, all season (0W-20), full-synthetic, heavy-duty powertrain
     lubricant that reduces fuel consumption and increases oil life. The MIL-PRF-32626 revisions have four
     grades:

          • OE/HDO-15/40.
          • OE/HDO-40.
          • OE/HDO-30.
          • OE/HDO-10.

     4-39. SMPL serves as a suitable replacement for equipment designed for the older MIL-PRF-32626 standard
     military oil. SMPL is generally best for cold weather operations and can be used in both the engine and
     transmission for both arctic and temperate conditions. This allows vehicles to be winterized prior to deploying
     from a temperate to a cold region. Always refer to the equipment TM and LO for recommended lubricants.
     The following considerations apply when using SMPL oils:

          • Use of SMPL in high-output 2-cycle diesel engines is not recommended when the ambient
                temperature exceeds 90°F (32°C); use 15W-40 or SAE 40 grade oil under those conditions.

          • SMPL is not a replacement for DEXRON VI in the transmissions of any tactical vehicles except in
                arctic conditions.

          • SMPL is not recommended for use in the transmission of the M1 Abrams main battle tank except
                in arctic conditions.

             Note. OEA engine oil is listed as EOS (meaning, engine oil supplement) in some older LOs. The
             supplement being referred to is the synthetic component of the oil. Equipment being prepared for
             future cold weather operations must use arctic-type lubricants, even if considerable operation in
             warmer climates is involved prior to cold weather operation. This gets ahead of equipment
             components being disabled because of lubrication failure when low temperatures are encountered.

             Note. When engine oil (SAE-10 or OE/HDO-10) is prescribed for gear cases, drain and fill with
             OEA-30 or OE/HDO-SMPL lubricating oil or GO-75/90 gear oil as prescribed by LO.

     4-40. Follow these general procedures for preparing the lubrication system for cold weather:
          • If possible, store lubricating oils and grease in a warm place. Lubricants are much easier to pour or
                apply if they are warm.
          • Prevent snow or moisture from entering the crankcase when lubricants are added. Use only newly
                opened cans of oil to eliminate the possibility of using contaminated oil.
          • Keep lubricating equipment free of moisture, snow, ice, or dirt to avoid contamination.
          • Inspect the engine oil pan and gasket for leaks.
          • Drain the engine lubrication system when the weather is warm (temperate weather). Replace the oil
                filter element. When the system is clean, fill with OEA-30 or OE/HDO SMPL lubrication oil in the
                amount specified in the operator TM or LO. Run the engine for 10 minutes and check for oil leaks.
                Stop the engine and wait 1-5 minutes before checking the oil level. This reading will be approximate
                only and should be between the ADD and FULL marks.

     4-41. When a temporary rise in temperature occurs, drivers should not change or vary the maintenance of
     their vehicles. However, they should closely monitor lubricant levels and lube points and replenish lubricants
     as necessary. Instructions in LOs apply when a definite change to higher environmental temperatures is
     expected, including the change of seasons.

62  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                      Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

GREASE LUBRICATION

     4-42. Grease is a gelatinous lubricant (gel) that clings to the surface of an item or inhabits a cavity without
     draining out, unlike fluid oils. Greases are applied to mechanisms in which a lubricant cannot stay in position
     or for which re-lubrication is infrequent, difficult, or inefficient. Grease must remain in contact with moving
     surfaces and lubricate without leaking out under the force of gravity, centrifugal action, or by being squeezed
     out under pressure. It should retain its basic properties under shear forces at all temperatures it experiences
     during use.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not mix different types of grease. Incompatibility of greases may
                    cause premature wear of equipment components.

     4-43. The increasing presence of COTS equipment in military inventory requires a wide variety of new
     lubricating greases to maintain it. In cold environments, it is critical for unit planners and maintenance
     managers to know the required greases for each equipment type within the unit inventory. Grease, automotive
     and artillery is no longer the dominant solution to Army grease applications. Operators and maintainers need
     a deep well-rounded understanding of the technical attributes and application principles of the different
     greases that they can expect to encounter. Maintainers will need to know the pour point of the base oils within
     the greases being used to avoid cold stiffening of these greases. They will also need to know the next
     alternative grease in line and how to transition from one grease to the next.

     4-44. Greases are typically formulated with three components: base oil, thickener, and additives. It is
     important to understand how well each of the most common base oils can withstand extreme temperature
     environments as they account for more than 90% of the total product. What makes grease effective in cold is
     its ability to maintain viscosity and flow as temperatures drop. As they get cold, all greases will naturally
     stiffen and harden to some degree. As a result, the cold reduces and eventually prevents grease from
     protecting equipment. Most base oils and grease can withstand moderate temperature dips to 32°F (0°C), and
     many to 15°F (-9°C), without too much of a decrease in performance. At -5°F (-21°C) and below, some
     lubricants become fully nonfunctional. There are three temperature characteristics related to grease that
     maintainers should be familiar with: dropping point, oxidation, and stiffening.

DROPPING POINT

     4-45. Grease dropping point is the upper temperature that the grease will fail to be of any further use as a
     lubricant. This is the point when the gel structure breaks down and the grease becomes semi-liquid. Once
     this happens, even after the grease is allowed to cool, it will never fully recover its gel form and will no
     longer provide the lubrication required for the equipment. If the grease is made from multiple products (such
     as both distilled oil and synthetic oil) and the dropping point temperature of just one of the components is
     reached, the whole grease is likewise ruined.

OXIDATION

     4-46. Normally, with correct application, grease oxidation is a slow ongoing process that shouldn’t reduce
     effectiveness in less than a year. When the grease oxidizes, it usually darkens as acidic oxidation products
     build up. These products can have a damaging effect on the grease and cause it to soften, allowing oils to
     weep or even bleed out of the grease. Once grease has oxidized, it must be cleaned out and replaced. Grease
     does not conduct heat easily. Serious oxidation can begin at a hot point and spread slowly through the grease.
     A heavily oxidized grease usually has a distinct black color and burned odor. This produces carbonization
     that gets stiffer in composition and leaves a crusty surface formation.

STIFFENING

     4-47. This is the lower temperature where the grease becomes too hard to be of practical use. Stiffened grease
     can become so viscous that it could be classified as a hard grease but is no longer useful for its intended use.
     As a general principle, the base oil’s pour point is considered the low temperature limit of a grease. The base

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  63


---

Chapter 4

     oil of the grease for low temperature service must be made from oils that can maintain a low viscosity at the
     lower temperatures.

SOLID FILM LUBRICANTS

     4-48. A variety of solid materials with inherent lubricating properties are available for use in solid-film
     lubricants. The most commonly used are molybdenum disulfide, graphite, and polytetrafluoroethylene. Solid
     lubricants are used primarily as extreme pressure or anti-wear type additives and are applied in one of three
     ways.

     4-49. The first and most popular application for solid lubricants, particularly molybdenum disulfide and
     graphite, is as an extreme pressure additive in grease formulations. The plate-like molecular structure of these
     solid lubricant particles reduces friction by allowing the surfaces in motion to easily slide over each other.

     4-50. Molybdenum disulfide generally has the highest load-carrying capability with a corresponding low
     coefficient of friction. However, in an oxidative atmosphere (air with oxygen) more than 750°F (399°C), it
     begins to decompose.

     4-51. Graphite is particularly beneficial where moisture is present. In fact, the presence of moisture is
     necessary to ensure graphite's full benefit as a friction reducer.

HYDRAULIC FLUIDS

     4-52. There are several hydraulic fluids that maintenance organizations use to maintain their vehicle fleets
     and weapon systems. The Army’s pursuit of hydraulic fluids reflects an effort to minimize flammability and
     corrosiveness. Hydraulic fluids are expressed as three letter symbols used to represent OHT, fire resistant
     hydraulic (FRH), and OHA hydraulic fluids, which are supply codes that may or may not be acronyms. OHA
     is the supply code for MIL-H-5606, hydraulic oil.

             Note: OHT and FRH should not be used in aviation assets.

COOLING SYSTEM

     4-53. Engines are cooled in two ways: liquid cooled and air cooled. The objective of a cooling system is to
     regulate the heat generated within the engine’s combustion cylinders. For vehicles, generators, and other
     motor-driven equipment going into cold environments, operators and maintenance personnel must take
     precautions to keep liquid cooling systems from freezing when the engine is not running. In cold conditions,
     liquids and automotive materials can behave and interact in ways that operators and maintainers of limited
     experience might not expect. If the liquid coolant becomes frozen in the radiator, the engine block, or the
     hoses conducting the coolant flow, the system might rupture.

     4-54. Operators must be vigilant and check the applicable TM. Once a discrepancy or defect is discovered,
     maintaining a cooling system usually turns to the maintainers. Managing the proper balance between water
     and antifreeze is important to engine care, regardless of the weather’s temperature.

ANTIFREEZE COOLANTS

     4-55. Antifreeze is typically added to distilled water in the cooling system of internal combustion engines so
     that they can be used below the freezing point of pure distilled water (32°F [0°C]) without freezing. Ethylene
     glycol is the most widely used automotive cooling system antifreeze. In automotive windshield washer fluids,
     an alcohol (commonly methanol) is usually added to keep the mixture from freezing; it also acts as a solvent
     to help clean the glass. The brine used in some commercial refrigeration systems is an antifreeze mixture; it
     is typically a distilled water solution of calcium chloride or propylene glycol.

     4-56. The antifreeze materials specified in TB 750-651 are critical for cold operations. Engine cooling
     systems usually use distilled water circulating around the combustion cylinder heads to absorb and carry
     away heat. Antifreeze serves as a corrosion inhibiter to prevent the distilled water from rusting the engine. In
     freezing temperatures, it reduces the freeze point of distilled water.

64  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

4-57. The overuse of ethylene glycol antifreeze (above 68% of the cooling system’s total capacity) reduces
the cooling system’s effectiveness at removing piston heat from the engine. It is the distilled water in the
system that is the foremost means of keeping an engine from overheating while running. Though antifreeze
does wick some heat from the engine, it is not as effective as distilled water. Antifreeze is instrumental in
deterring the corrosive effects of distilled water on metal, as well as keeping distilled water from freezing
when the engine is not running.

       Note. The Army is shifting away from Soldier-mixed antifreeze to pre-mixed antifreeze. Always
       refer to the item specific TM or LO for servicing an item of equipment with antifreeze. The depth
       of background information presented here is only to help leaders understand underlying principles
       of antifreeze properties for decision making in emergency situations.

4-58. There are three major types of antifreeze that the General Services Administration has authorized and
are commonly used by the military. They are specified by A-A-53624, Antifreeze, Multi Engine Type, and
TB 750-651:

     • Ethylene glycol, for mixing with distilled water to make an antifreeze solution. Ethylene glycol
          distilled water mixed solution is the most common antifreeze agent used for both military and
          commercial engines. It is used for temperatures of 32°F (0°C) down to -50°F (-46°C). To use, mix
          with distilled water. Verify antifreeze capability to -50°F (-46°C) with a refractometer.

     • Arctic antifreeze solution, premixed with 60% ethylene glycol. Arctic antifreeze solution is an
          ethylene glycol-based antifreeze with several additives used mostly by the military. It is used for
          temperatures of -40°F (-40°C) down to -90°F (-68°C). To use, do not dilute; use full strength as
          packaged. Do not add an antifreeze extender additive with arctic antifreeze.

     • Propylene glycol, for mixing with distilled water to make an antifreeze solution. This solution is
          only used by the military in commercial products under warranty, as prescribed by manufacturer’s
          instructions. It is used for temperatures of 32°F (0°C) down to -76°F (-60°C). Propylene Glycol is
          of a different composition from ethylene glycol. It is non-toxic. Commercially available propylene
          glycol-based antifreezes should never be mixed with ethylene glycol-based antifreeze. Commercial
          items that are under warranty are to follow the manufacturer’s recommendations until the warranty
          has expired. Do not use it in Army vehicles past the warranty period. When switching over to
          military standard automotive antifreeze, flush the cooling system to remove all the commercial
          antifreeze.

                                             DANGER

               Do not drink ethylene glycol-based antifreeze. It is harmful or fatal
               if swallowed. If swallowed, induce vomiting immediately and call a
               physician. Do not store in open or unlabeled containers.

4-59. Waste antifreeze contains heavy metals including lead, cadmium, and chromium in high enough levels
to make it a regulated hazardous waste. Most states strictly regulate antifreeze disposal. Never dump spent
antifreeze on the ground or discharge it into municipal sewers, storm drains, ditched, dry wells, or septic
systems. It can cause serious water quality problems and might harm people, pets, or wildlife. Any type of
antifreeze is considered hazardous and should be reused, recycled, or disposed of properly.

4-60. Cooling systems containing deteriorated antifreeze or lacking solution should be drained and flushed
before the onset of freezing temperatures. Soldiers should then add the correct antifreeze solution. If premixed
antifreeze is not available, use Table 4-1 on page 64 as a guide for mixing concentrated antifreeze. The
antifreeze should then be tested with an antifreeze refractometer. The actual sight appearance may vary in
different refractometers. Consider the following ratio when mixing antifreeze with distilled water:

     • A 68/32 ratio provides maximum low temperature protection.
     • A 25/75 ratio provides protection against rust and corrosion.
     • A 50/50 ratio in normal weather conditions.
     • A 60/40 ratio provides protection in arctic conditions.

    4-61. To fill the engine, first pour in the antifreeze according to the prescribed number of quarts indicated by
    table 4-1. The antifreeze should equal 50% to 60% of the cooling system’s total capacity. Next add distilled
    water to fill the remainder of the system. The guide to the table is below:

         • First, identify the total cooling system capacity in quarts for the vehicle or item of equipment.
         • Then go down the left column of the table to locate the number that equates to the vehicle or item’s

              cooling system’s total capacity in quarts.
         • Scan to the right on the row until reaching the level of how cold the protection must reach.
         • Look up that column to find the number of quarts of antifreeze will be needed for mixing with the

              distilled water.

    4-62. A refractometer (Figure 4-3) is a device that measures the efficacy of antifreeze solution by determining
    its ability to regulate temperature or the set level of protection. It is also used to measure battery acid (sulfuric
    acid) capacity to hold charge.

    4-63. When adding (topping off) coolant level with arctic antifreeze, add coolant as it is without mixing with
    water, directly into the reservoir or radiator. If using concentrated ethylene glycol-based antifreeze, it must
    be mixed with distilled water.

66                                     TM 4−33.31                                12 December 2025


---

                                                                                      Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants Management

          Figure 4-3. Antifreeze and battery refractometer sight picture and testing device
     4-64. All engines with antifreeze must be labeled with a heat-resistant sticker or string tag indicating the type
     of antifreeze and its level of cold protection. An antifreeze tag should read:

          • The cooling system is filled with ethylene glycol antifreeze solution. Protected to -62°F (-52°C)
                (or whatever the correct protection temperature should be) or,

          • This cooling system is filled with arctic-type antifreeze. Protected to -90°F (-68°C) (or whatever
                the correct protection temperature should be). Caution: do not add distilled water or any other
                type of antifreeze.

     4-65. The equipment’s maintenance records should then be annotated with the date that work was completed,
     the condition of the cooling system and the degree of freeze protection.

AIR-COOLED ENGINE SYSTEMS

     4-66. Since an air-cooled system does not use an antifreeze liquid coolant, it is often assumed that air alone
     acts as the cooling medium. This is not true. The lubrication system also helps in cooling the engine and
     transmission; it prevents heat buildup from friction. The cooling process often includes oil pumps that
     circulate the oil between the engine and the coolers and between the transmission and the cooler, removing
     heat from the engine and transmission. Some engine cooling also results from the fuel contacting metal parts
     prior to combustion. The effects of cold on an air-cooled system are basically the same as the effects on the
     engine with an antifreeze-cooled system.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  67
