                                                 Chapter 5

                             Vehicle Operation

    This chapter provides automotive-focused considerations for dealing with vehicles or engine-
    driven equipment before, during, and after operations. The primary challenge with vehicles in
    cold environments is getting them started. This chapter is intended to supplement operator and
    maintainer TMs.

OPERATING VEHICLE ENGINES IN COLD WEATHER

     5-1. Vehicles need unique preparation for the harsh conditions of cold weather. Operators must be aware of
     the special problems cold weather can cause with starting and operating the vehicle or equipment. Completing
     operations takes longer due to additional storage precautions needed to protect the equipment and maintain
     its readiness in cold conditions.

     5-2. Normalizing an engine is a state of vehicle operation where, in an engineering context, all the component
     engine features that contribute to its proper function are performing according to their intended design
     parameters. For instance, the coolant and antifreeze must effectively regulate the engine's temperature, while
     the battery must be fully prepared to power the starter motor and glow plugs. It means that the lubricants are
     effectively preventing friction damage. As an engine transitions through various states of operation (off, start,
     idling, moving, stopping), electro-mechanical controls act to normalize the engine for optimal performance.
     This normalizing function is performed by the vehicle and monitored by the operator. In cold, normalizing
     takes longer and may require patience until complete.

     5-3. Maintaining vehicle and equipment readiness extends to fully stimulating the movement of all hydraulic
     fluids, antifreeze, oils, or lubricants. In cold weather, power production and full system stimulation must be
     incorporated into unit regular PMCS maintenance policies. This may involve having vehicles driven for half
     an hour on a circuit around the installation or camp. It may involve having engineering equipment engage in
     earth moving exercises. Cranes, wenches, and turrets may have to be system stimulated by two or three
     repetitions of moving heavy items. Generators or power production equipment can be load tested for thirty
     minutes to verify readiness while keeping fluids functional. Different types of equipment will need different
     plans to keep them mission ready in cold weather situations. It is not enough to turn on an engine and let it
     idle.

     5-4. Dust, sand, and dirt are commonly the major causes of clogged air filters. However, in cold weather
     conditions, moisture resulting from rain or snow can also introduce solid particles that restrict the airflow
     through a filter. Operators must exercise caution following instances of rain or snowfall while the engine is
     in operation, particularly when accompanied by freezing temperatures. In these situations, it’s important to
     check the filters often to ensure that ice has not become embedded into the filter fibers. Additionally, when
     the engine is at rest, operators should use a tarp to protect the filter from both rain and snow that could drift
     into the filter intake.

STARTING PROCEDURES

     5-5. Starting a vehicle in cold can present several challenges. Unlike temperate weather, drivers must give
     engines and transmissions ample time to warm up in stages before engaging in normal driving. Follow proper
     procedures to safeguard personnel and equipment.

     5-6. Maintenance leaders should plan on a systematic way to start the unit's vehicle fleet in a cold
     environment. Severe cold weather can leech battery power, making supplemental power necessary to start
     vehicles. Such a system may involve having a warm vehicle on standby to slave start other vehicles. It may
     involve having extra battery jump starters. Added supplies of battery chargers are often necessary to meet
     the heavy requirements for battery maintenance in below freezing temperatures. If the situation permits,
     motor pool electrical sockets should be used to power preheaters or trickle chargers.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  69


---

Chapter 5

     5-7. The storage battery functions are the heart of the electrical system, especially during the starting phase.
     Pay particular attention to the battery terminals and clamps during cold weather. A loose connection or a
     small amount of corrosion will add enough resistance to obstruct vehicle starting and reduce performance.

     5-8. In extreme-cold (-25°F to -40°F [-32°C to -40°C]) or hazardous-cold (below -40°F [-40°C]), a storage
     battery that otherwise has viable liquid electrolyte might be too cold to produce enough electrical current to
     turn over the engine’s starter motor. A drop in current that would be modest in warm weather can keep the
     starter from turning over a cold-soaked engine. Just the battery’s effort to push power into the starter motor
     could cause the electrolyte to lose specific gravity and freeze. When equipment with conventional wet
     batteries will not start at low temperatures, turn off all starting switches and check for a frozen battery before
     attempting to slave start or boost charge. Take off all filler caps and visually examine the electrolyte. For
     GEL or AGM batteries, a battery load tester is needed because their filler caps are not removable.

     5-9. If the battery cannot engage the starter motor or the electrolyte appears frozen, mark the battery and
     notify the unit is maintenance section. A qualified battery mechanic must make the determination if the
     battery is usable.

OPERATOR TASKS

     5-10. Check battery electrolyte, engine oil, and coolant levels daily prior to initial starting of a vehicle or any
     other engine. To high idle the vehicle, adjust the hand throttle to the engine speed specified in the operator
     TM until the engine is running smoothly. When engine operation is required for battery recharging or engine
     warm-up, maintain a high engine idle speed as prescribed in the vehicle TM (occasional variance of speed
     for short periods is allowed). Cold batteries (that are not frozen) can take over 20 minutes of engine idling
     before the alternator can recover energy that the battery lost in trying to start the engine.

     5-11. Operators must coordinate with supervisors on any deviations from unit policy and TM-defined
     procedures, and must also consider the following:

          • Vehicles should only be cold started using the procedures outlined in the TM. This is particularly
                important for operating the engine starter and induction manifold (when present). Most equipment
                publications contain cautions related to the time of engine starter engagement. However, it is
                sometimes necessary to exceed the recommended starter time due to the cold-starting characteristics
                of most internal combustion engines. For example, if a starter time limit is 30 seconds and engine
                firing is intermittent during cranking, it may be necessary to briefly extend the starter engagement
                time (consult with supervisors first and use caution when attempting).

          • Vehicles (especially the engine) should not be allowed to become cold-soaked.
          • Engines should be warmed by engine heaters or be started periodically to keep lubricants warm.
          • Batteries must be kept warm and fully charged.

MAJOR COMPONENT WARM-UP

     5-12. There are various methods operators can use to safely bring vehicles up to operating temperature in
     cold weather environments. Operators should know the warm-up procedures for the vehicle’s major
     components and understand that effectively warming one component does not mean the vehicle is safe to
     operate. For example, immediately operating a cold vehicle after the engine has warmed up may damage the
     cold transmission if it has not also been properly warmed.

Transmissions

     5-13. Automatic transmissions warm up differently depending on the type. Some warm up in park, others
     only warm up if the selector lever is placed in neutral, and others must be placed in gear during warm-up. In
     cold (-25°F [-32°C] and below), many transmissions will not warm up unless placed in gear to allow the
     torque converter to circulate and preheat the transmission fluid. Forced movement of the vehicle prior to
     warm-up can cause transmission failure. Always consult the TM for proper transmission warm-up
     procedures.

70  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                             Vehicle Operation

             Note. If a vehicle is equipped with a transfer or torque converter having a selector lever, the
             component’s lubricant may be heated the same way by placing selector level in neutral and
             transmission in low.

     5-14. Standard transmissions have been mostly removed from military inventory but may occur with COTS
     equipment. To start a standard transmission, depress the clutch pedal while starting the engine with the gear
     shift in neutral. Once the engine is running smoothly, release the clutch cautiously and maintain the engine
     at idle for two minutes or longer to warm lubricant in the transmission.

     5-15. The driver must be extremely careful when placing the vehicle in motion when gear case lubricants or
     wheel bearing greases are congealed and tires are frozen to the ground. Trying to operate under these
     conditions may damage powertrain components such as clutch facings, universal joints, or gear teeth. When
     placing the vehicle in motion, put the transmission in low gear and transfer unit in low range. Drive the
     vehicle about 100 meters (being careful not to stall the engine) then upshift. Continue slowly in the higher
     gears until the vehicle moves freely and the tire thumping ceases.

Diesel Engines

     5-16. Diesel engines are particularly difficult to start in cold weather without preheating the intake air.
     Because the air is heated by compression, it must get hot enough to ignite the injected fuel. This preheating
     can be accomplished as follows:

          • Use air manifold heaters when the ambient temperature drops below 32°F (0°C). Use this device
                only when turning the engine over. Switch off the air manifold heater when the engine starts.

          • Warm the engine with external heat (like electric heater pads) to preheat the engine.
          • If so equipped, operate the engine coolant fuel-fired preheater for the prescribed amount of time

                before starting.
          • Ether cold starts (for temperatures below 32°F [0°C]) is done ONLY while cranking the engine.

                (Use sparingly, excessive ether can cause piston and ring damage.) Ether is used as an emergency
                starting aid only if auxiliary power and/or portable heaters are unavailable for preheating. Exercise
                extreme caution and use ether only as a last resort to prevent mission failure. Always refer to the
                appropriate operator’s manual when utilizing emergency starting aids.
          • In dire circumstances, the use of direct fire or any heat-producing sources may be used. Employ
                good judgement, risk assessment, and viable safety techniques.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Ether should be injected into the engine air intake system only while the
                    engine is being turned over. Otherwise, a flashback may occur.

Gasoline Engines

     5-17. The Army has largely phased out gasoline-powered equipment. However, some local commands still
     use gasoline-powered COTS equipment (for example, rental vehicles). To successfully start gasoline engines
     in cold weather, make sure the following conditions exist:

          • The engine oil must splash and be distributed easily by the oil pump to the various parts and
                bearings.

          • The battery is fully charged and warm enough to supply current to crank the engine and proved the
                spark needed for ignition.

          • Supplemental battery power is available.
          • The ignition primary and secondary circuits are clean and free of cracks, frost, and moisture to

                prevent shorts or current leaks.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  71


---

Chapter 5

Auxiliary Engines and Generators

     5-18. The auxiliary engine and generator are operated when—
          • Supporting communications and electronics equipment.
          • Other types of auxiliary equipment are being used.
          • The main engine is not running.
          • Batteries are being charged.
          • The current furnished by the main engine is inadequate for the imposed load.

     5-19. The auxiliary engine, generator, spark plugs, and magneto should be prepared in a manner similar to
     the main engine and components. Carefully warm the engine block with the duct from a portable heater to
     assist in starting. Many small engines have a summer/winter air intake diverter that allows air to be pulled
     over the exhaust and warmed in winter and directly from the outside in summer. If the engine is so equipped,
     ensure the diverter is set for winter operation.

             Note: Some small engines have a low oil pressure shutoff switch to stop the engine in case of low
             oil pressure. The oil in the engine block must be warmed sufficiently so the cranking RPM can
             build up enough oil pressure to close the switch so the engine will start.

DURING OPERATION

     5-20. Operators should minimize short trips due to the impact on battery performance. Each time a vehicle
     is started, energy is extracted from the battery, leading to a deficit charge. A deficit charge is a condition
     where the battery drains at a faster rate than the alternator can replenish it. At normal temperatures, this
     energy is quickly replaced as the battery is recharged by the vehicle’s alternator. However, cold batteries
     don’t charge well. Cold-soaked batteries need about thirty minutes of driving before the batteries get warm
     enough to fully accept a fresh recharge from the alternator (20 minutes for wet cell, and longer for the other
     types).

                                                 WARNING

                    Operation of the main engine or auxiliary generator engine when
                    a vehicle is stationary exposes the crew to possible carbon
                    monoxide gas poisoning.

     5-21. During vehicle operation, adhere to the following guidelines:
          • Allow the engine to warm up to the recommended temperature for below-freezing operation
                according to the TM. The engine thermostat usually opens at 165°F (74°C) or 180°F (82°C).
          • Check the operation of instruments during warmup, especially the oil pressure gauges and warning
                lights.
          • Take advantage of all methods available to keep the battery at the recommended temperature. If the
                battery is warm enough to accept a charge (above 35°F [2°C]), the battery generator indicator should
                read in the high green immediately after starting.
          • Check and listen for any unusual noise from the alternator. Fan belts break at a high rate below 50°F.
                Inspect belts for cracks prior to cold weather operation; replace cracked belts. Adjustable length
                belts are available for emergency roadside repair for “V” belts. They come in ⅜, ½, and ¾ inch
                widths and require a connector tool to fit to size.
          • Before starting the engine, check the oil and fill it to the prescribed level. Once the engine starts,
                check the reading on the oil pressure gauge. Within 30 seconds after starting the engine, the oil
                pressure should register on the oil pressure gauge. For vehicles or equipment equipped with warning
                lights, wait 30 seconds for the oil light to turn off. If after 30 seconds the oil pressure gauge does
                not register the pressure or the oil warning light stays lit, stop the engine and investigate to determine
                the cause.

72  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                             Vehicle Operation

          • Frequently monitor the oil pressure gauge and warning light during operation in cold conditions.
                When using OEA 30 or OE/HDO SMPL lubricant, the low oil pressure warning lights may blink on
                and off between 500 RPM to 650 RPM when idling. They should not stay on at higher RPMs.

     5-22. Monitor engine temperature throughout operations. Operate vehicles at normal engine temperatures as
     indicated in the operator’s TM. Efforts to regulate engine temperatures can involve both chemical and
     mechanical solutions like—

          • Antifreeze.
          • Lubrication.
          • Heaters.
          • Winterfronts or radiator shutters.

                                                 CAUTION

                    An engine that fails to reach normal operating temperature or overheats
                    must be reported to maintenance personnel for correction. Failure to do
                    so may result in serious damage to the engine.

     5-23. Maintain engine temperatures by using engine compartment air inlet shutters or radiator covers to limit
     the cold from reaching the engine. To minimize cold weather effects on engine oil performance, operators
     should avoid operating vehicles until the engine has reached normal operating temperatures. It might be
     helpful to cover radiators that keep an engine cool. Shielding can be installed on tractors and generators with
     exposed engine blocks to keep engine heat contained in a compartment.

     5-24. Operating an engine at low temperatures can lead to several issues, including increased fuel
     consumption, dilution of engine oil by unburned fuel, and the buildup of sludge from water in the cylinders
     and crankcase. Drivers should try to exercise their vehicles during the half hour before convoy serials begin
     their line-up. Maintenance leaders should try to establish a short vehicle exercise track or route just outside
     the motor pool or around the base perimeter. At the completion of the 150-200 meter vehicle exercise track,
     operators should perform a during operations check under supervision before exiting the area. As needed, a
     forced air jet heater should be on standby to heat frozen brakes or pneumatic lines. Give extra attention to—

          • Engine temperature.
          • Transmission shifting.
          • Braking systems.
          • Pneumatic lines, and air compressor systems.
          • Fluid levels.
          • Heating equipment.
          • Tire pressure and tire chains.
          • Lights and safety equipment.
          • Trailer connections, electrical, and braking.
          • Electronics and communications systems.

IDLING

     5-25. Engine idling is done to warm and circulate lubricating oil. If the oil gets too cold, it can thicken to the
     point where it obstructs movement, preventing an engine from starting. However, a normal idle speed may
     not produce sufficient generator power for the batteries to overcome the cold resulting in a net battery
     discharge. To warm engine fluids and recover the battery charge, engines usually need to operate at higher-
     than-normal RPMs.

     5-26. Proper vehicle maintenance, float chargers, and winterization kits with engine heaters can help reduce
     starting problems. An engine heater is an electrical or mechanical heating device used to preheat engine fluids
     (antifreeze or lubricants) to ease engine starting. Diesel engines without heaters may need to be started and
     idled intermittently or continuously (depending on the ambient temperature) to maintain an acceptable state
     of readiness. See the appropriate vehicle TM for guidance.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  73


---

Chapter 5

     5-27. The commander and maintenance leaders determine starting and shutdown intervals to ensure engines
     do not run continuously (unless continuous running is the intent). Depending on the situation, concealment
     and noise discipline may have to be sacrificed to maintain readiness of the vehicles. Commanders must
     carefully consider fuel supplies when authorizing periodic engine starts. Factors including ambient or
     expected temperatures, vehicle condition, and readiness conditions influence scheduling.

             Note. Vehicle engines might be idled to keep lubricants and antifreeze from freezing but should
             not be idled just to operate personnel heaters.

     5-28. Vehicle engine idling is wasteful and may be hazardous. Idling an engine has detrimental effects on
     fuel supply, tactical missions, and safety for the following reasons:

          • Increases fuel consumption.
          • Discharges batteries when the generator or alternator fails to produce enough surplus energy to run

                the engine and maintain or restore battery power.
          • Causes engine wear and reduces the time between engine overhaul periods.
          • Causes glow plug or spark plug fouling, especially in two-stroke cycle engines like all-terrain

                vehicles, snowmobiles, and outboard motors.
          • Creates a carbon monoxide (CO) hazard.
          • Endangers concealment and camouflage.
          • Potentially causes engine overheating.
          • Potentially causes diesel “slobber” or “wet stack”. These terms refer to the unburned diesel fuel and

                exhaust that build up around exhaust pipes in cold weather during idling.

AUXILIARY ELECTRONIC EQUIPMENT

     5-29. Vehicles with command-and-control devices like radio, computer devices, mine-defeating attachments,
     motorized ring mounts, and other electrical devices require the engine to operate at approximately 1,200
     RPM to maintain a satisfactory battery charge. A battery with low charge is at risk for failure and freeze. To
     avoid malfunctions of auxiliary devices, ensure each device switch is on the OFF or standby position before
     starting the vehicle.

VEHICLE OPERATION SAFETY

     5-30. A licensed operator must be present in the driver’s compartment whenever the main and/or auxiliary
     engine is being operated. However, being licensed is not the only requirement. Units must verify the vehicle
     operator’s competency to drive in a cold environment (TC 21-305-20) and identify and provide any additional
     training required.

     5-31. Novice drivers must understand that extreme cold reduces traction. Even on dry surfaces, cold alters
     the performance of tire and tread rubber, as well as the hydraulics of suspension systems. Drivers should
     understand that the cold usually degrades the responsiveness of the steering and braking controls against
     driving surfaces.

     5-32. Driving on snow, ice, slush or frozen mud is very different than driving in rain or loose mud. Many
     novice drivers have little idea how differently a large vehicle handles from their personal vehicle in cold
     weather driving conditions. It’s important for drivers to receive practical road training to learn to—

          • Clear condensation from brake lines and test the brakes to ensure they function properly before
                operation. This procedure may require the use of electric or forced-air heaters.

          • Recognize the different braking systems along with their respective driving characteristics. This
                includes direct cable, power assist, positive pressure pneumatic, negative pressure, and engine
                compression breaking.

          • Never mix positive pressure or negative pressure towing vehicles to trailers with the opposite type
                braking system.

          • Use alcohol-based windshield deicer on side mirrors.
          • Install tire chains in slushy road conditions.
          • Manage tire pressure when driving on snow. (It is not effective to reduce tire pressure with radial

                tires.)

74  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                             Vehicle Operation

          • Use the engine brake (found on some large trucks) to decelerate on ice or black ice. (Wheel brakes
                are prone to locking up, resulting in an out-of-control skid.)

          • Reduce speed well before corners and take cornering very slowly.
          • Brake well in advance of the desired stop point and creep up to the actual stop point.
          • Use low gear or first gear to accelerate uphill from being stopped on an inclined road or slope.
          • Down shift into low gear whenever driving uphill or downhill.
          • Compensate for wide swings during turns and winding roads when towing trailers and weapon

                systems.
          • Recognize vehicle deficiencies that might expose personnel to carbon monoxide poisoning.

AFTER OPERATION

     5-33. Shutting down a vehicle in cold can have unexpected results. In principle, when preparing a vehicle for
     shutdown, place transmission and transfer shift levers in the neutral (not the park) position. This prepares the
     vehicle for the next start by preventing the gears from freezing in an engaged position. Wheel or tread chocks
     should always be used when parking a vehicle set in neutral gear. Maintenance leaders should tell operators
     and their supervisors when to initiate or cease cold weather parking precautions.

     5-34. During each after operations PMCS in cold weather, pay special attention to inspecting and servicing
     the system as follows:

          • Inspect oil pan, valve covers, gaskets, and any external units of the lubrication system for leaks;
                correct deficiencies or report them to maintenance. During periods of cold, it is not uncommon to
                observe leaks from various seals on hydraulic systems and oil-filled components upon start up.
                These leaks should stop after the component reaches normal operating temperature and the seal
                becomes soft and flexible. Reporting leaking components before allowing sufficient time for the
                seal to warm-up causes an undue burden on the maintenance system and unnecessary seal
                replacement.

          • Check engine oil and fill to the prescribed level. Oil consumption may be higher when using OEA-
                30 or OE/HDO-SMPL. Engines may run out of oil before the next maintenance check. Check
                frequently.

          • Check the radiator after shutdown to determine the coolant level. If the coolant level is low, add
                antifreeze to return the radiator to full level.

          • At the end of each operating period, for about five minutes prior to shut down, normalize the vehicle
                engine to allow the cylinder walls and pistons to retain a coating of cooled oil. This prevents damage
                at the time of restarting. It is done by lowering the engine revolution to the prescribed high idling
                speed (see item TM) and by maintaining it for about five minutes. This procedure is extremely
                important to prevent engine damage and is called for in many TMs.

          • Service the fuel tank at the end of the day’s activities to help keep water out of the fuel, as filling
                the tank drives out any moisture-laden air. Avoid having a partially filled tank stand overnight or
                during a long halt.

OPERATING VEHICLE SUB-COMPONENTS

     5-35. Sub-components cannot be overlooked in preparing for cold weather operations. Failure of these items
     can also lead to mission failure. Prepare for cold weather by referencing the TM for PMCS and operations
     instructions for operators, maintainers, and managers.

PERSONNEL HEATERS

     5-36. Operators should take measures to prepare their vehicle heaters as cold weather approaches. The
     primary threat to heater operation is dust buildup. Maintenance personnel should provide operators with
     instructions on the proper procedures for removing heater ducts to access the heater fan. Maintenance
     personnel should instruct operators on how to remove heater ducts to access the heater fan. Once exposed,
     operators can clean the inside of the heater, heater fan, heating radiators, and heating ducts.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  75


---

Chapter 5

                                                 CAUTION

                    Petroleum-fed heaters can reach temperatures of 300°F (149°C).

     5-37. Three-stage heaters, standard in light vehicles, use antifreeze to transfer heat through heating radiators.
     However, they lose their ability to adequately warm crews when temperatures drop below -40°F (-40°C).

     5-38. Most severe cold petroleum-fed heaters (common to armored vehicles or part of the winterization kit)
     use two-stage heater systems. To avoid carbon buildup or heater flooding, allow heaters to purge during shut
     down for at least five minutes before turning off the master switch (see the TM for details). This usually
     involves three steps: turn off the heater switch, wait until the unit working light goes out, then turn off the
     battery switch.

     5-39. Before the onset of cold weather, petroleum-burning heaters should be removed from vehicles and have
     their cylinders cleaned. If the cylinder is not cleaned, the creosote buildup will harden, interfere with heat
     radiation, and could erupt into a runaway fire that might destroy the heater unit.

AIR COMPRESSORS

     5-40. An air compressor is a device powered by an engine that compresses air to predetermined and
     controlled pressures. It is used in vehicle air brake, air-hydraulic brake, and CTIS. Condensed moisture can
     freeze within the compressor.

     5-41. Follow after-operations maintenance procedures by draining condensation from air compressor tanks.
     If the bleed valve is frozen, report it immediately to maintenance personnel. Some 900-series vehicles
     (M911/977), the FMTV (2½ ton and 5-ton trucks), palletized load system vehicles, and heavy equipment
     transport trucks use air driers in the air brake system. The system must be checked for frozen vapors in the
     drip tube.

     5-42. Alcohol evaporators, usually found near the air compressors of cargo trucks and semitrailers, should
     be filled with alcohol during operations in temperatures of -20°F (-29C) and below. These evaporators
     typically consist of plastic bottles of alcohol that are included in winterization kits. They are designed to draw
     water out of the air going into the compressor to prevent freezing. Check the container as a part of before,
     during, and after operations PMCS.

     5-43. Air compressors can either have their own self-lubricating system or rely on the engine’s lubrication
     system. No further preparation is necessary for engine-lubricated, air-cooled compressors, provided the
     engine crankcase has been filled with lubrication oil (OE/HDO-SCPL or OEA-30).

     5-44. The cooling system of liquid-cooled air compressors is connected to the cooling system of the vehicle
     engine. Drain, flush, and clean the compressor cooling system and inspect for leaks. Ensure all connections
     are in good condition. Tighten cylinder head bolts to correct torque tightness specified in the vehicle TM.
     The engine coolant system must be adequately protected with antifreeze compound. Inspect the compressor
     for leaks.

     5-45. To ensure air compressors function properly, operators must—
          • Check to see if the compressor is maintaining required pressure.
          • Examine that there are no coolant or oil leaks.
          • Listen for excessive noise.
          • Examine the compressor to ensure that it is in good condition, properly aligned with drive pulleys,
                and securely mounted.
          • Check for frozen vapors in the drip tube on equipment using air dryers.
          • Ensure that all water, oil, and air lines in the engine compartment are in good condition, securely
                fastened, and have no leaks.
          • Check oil in self-lubricated air compressors to determine proper condition and level.
          • Check alcohol evaporators, if equipped.

76  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                                             Vehicle Operation

POWER TAKE-OFF ASSEMBLIES

     5-46. A power takeoff uses hydraulic lines or a drive shaft to power auxiliary equipment affixed to a vehicle.
     The power takeoff and control linkage should be checked to ensure they are in good condition, securely
     mounted, and that seals are not leaking. The system’s connection assemblies are usually visible on the side
     of the transmission with a hydraulic pump mounted inside the transmission. They are sometimes mounted on
     the side of a transfer case. They provide a means for taking power from the engine to drive accessories or
     auxiliary equipment that may be attached to a vehicle. Power takeoff-driven auxiliary equipment includes
     winches, cranes, air compressors, hydraulic pumps, trench diggers, and earth boring machines.

     5-47. In freezing temperatures, ensure that the power takeoff is filled to the appropriate level with the correct
     lubricant. Because the power takeoff is operated from the transmission or transfer case, the lubricant should
     be that specified for cold temperatures. Drain the gear cases while the weather is still warm and fill with
     prescribed grade of cold-weather gear lubricant. In cold weather, improper lubricants will solidify, making
     operation of the power takeoff difficult or even impossible.

     5-48. Use the TM to inventory all component parts. Ensure that the transmission and/or breather and
     ventilation openings and lines are clear. Tighten all mounting bolts, power takeoff assembly screws, and bolts
     to the torque tightness specified in the TM.

RECOVERY EQUIPMENT

     5-49. Recovering damaged or disabled vehicles in cold and snowy conditions can be extremely dangerous.
     Take every precaution to maintain Soldier safety and ensure that equipment is functioning properly. Exercise
     care when towing cold-soaked vehicles. Drive trains may have to be disconnected to prevent further damage.

     5-50. For any cold weather recovery operation where a recovery vehicle must use more than half of its rated
     capability or capacity, a second recovery vehicle should be sent to reinforce the operation. Cold temperatures
     reduce the tensile strength of cables. It also thickens the viscosity of hydraulic fluid in recovery cranes, which
     creates greater resistance to motion. Recovery in mountainous, snowy, or icy terrain may not allow a single
     recovery vehicle to achieve good leverage. Vehicle recovery is often extremely difficult due to reduced
     mechanical capacity, ice, snow, and limited approach routes. The hardest recoveries will occur in wet-cold
     situations where vehicles have sunk into softening mud that the recovery vehicle must cross to reach the
     immobilized vehicle.

     5-51. In temperate weather environments, it is not uncommon for operators to let worn out or decomposed
     grease and lubricants accumulate on the working parts of recovery equipment. Before going into the cold,
     equipment should be wiped off, dried out, and cleaned of old, contaminated, or oxidized lubricants. While
     decupled, the item should be inspected by maintenance personnel. Three critical concerns of inspection are
     seals, hydraulic lines, and wire cables.

WINCH ASSEMBLY

     5-52. Read all caution plates and estimate a 25% reduction in winch capacity when determining load. For
     example, if the capacity of the winch is specified as 5,000 pounds on the winch caution plate, lower the rated
     capacity for cold weather operations to 3,750 pounds. This safety factor is essential to prevent damage to the
     winch. Use a snatch block or pulley whenever possible to reduce the load on the winch.

     5-53. Take the following steps when preparing a winch for operation:
          • Remove mud, dirt, and rust from the exterior of the winch.
          • Inspect cable for rust and apply lubricating oil, general purpose, preservative (military symbol
                PL-S).
          • Flush and clean gear housing and fill to level with recommended seasonal gear oil.
          • Inspect safety brake for satisfactory operation.
          • Check drum brake and adjust if necessary.
          • Check for frozen bleeder and selector valves, controls and linkage, and winch cable sheaves.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  77


---

Chapter 5

                                                 CAUTION:

                    When using the winch, operate the vehicle engine at a reasonable
                    speed as specified in the operator’s manual. High engine speeds risk
                    damaging the winch mechanism.

OUTRIGGER STABILITY

     5-54. Many vehicles equipped with cranes have outriggers to provide added stability. However, when
     operating on icy surfaces, the wheels and outriggers might not be sufficient to keep a vehicle from slipping
     sideways as the crane lifts and rotates while handling heavy items.
     5-55. Before the onset of freezing cold, vehicle supervisors and maintenance managers should inspect that
     each vehicle has a planned minimum number of tire chains (with elastic or rubber chain tighteners) and
     outrigger traction pads (local unit fabricated) with cleating for hard ice operations. Line supervisors should
     train crews to install vehicle chains and traction devises rapidly and safely under field conditions. In wet-cold
     weather, recovery vehicles with outriggers will need to carry reinforced pallets (local unit fabricated) that
     can disburse vehicle weight and prevent outrigger shoes from drilling into the mud.

78  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                 Chapter 6

                Small Arms Preventive Maintenance

    This chapter provides general information and recommendations on small arms preventive
    maintenance in a cold or arctic environment.

GENERAL EFFECTS OF COLD

     6-1. Operating and maintaining weapons in cold climates is much more of a challenge than in temperate
     environments. In the cold, metals and plastics become brittle and break more easily. Ice formed from
     breathing on the weapon can seize bolt movement, and cold ammunition is less effective when fired. Weapon
     parts are subject to high stress from rapid variations in temperature, and the shock of firing can break weapon
     components or render weapons unserviceable more rapidly. Identify weapon parts that are vulnerable to cold
     and more prone to breakage and have extra parts on hand before conducting operations.

     6-2. Firing a pistol while wearing arctic mittens increases the likelihood of dropping the weapon. The shock
     of the weapon hitting the ground is more likely to result in damage due to the metal’s weakened state, and
     falling into snow or mud can introduce a large quantity of ice or snow to the pistol’s inner workings.

     6-3. Machine gunners should strive to be selective and accurate in target engagement for multiple reasons. A
     sudden long burst of fire from a cold weapon will cause rapid uneven metal expansion. Such expansion can
     cause stress fractures, cracking, and may cause casehardened metals to break. Selective and accurate target
     engagement will also conserve more ammunition and help reduce the need for resupply, which is more
     complex in cold environments.

     6-4. Snow or ice plugged magazines can cause rifle malfunctions and breakage. Apply LAW to prevent
     bipods, movable sights, and other working components from freezing in position. Empty casings may cause
     weapon jams early in firing due to short recoil (bolt does not recoil fully to the rear), requiring immediate
     action as prescribed by the TM. This problem should stop as the metal warms. Buffers may also harden and
     freeze, which increases shock and causes rapid recoil (this increases the cyclic rate of fire and usually breaks
     parts).

     6-5. All internal parts and friction surfaces of machine guns should be coated with LAW when not prohibited
     by the TM. If lubricants for below freezing temperatures are not available, then the next best option is to fire
     the weapon cold and dry. Temperate weather lubricants used in extreme cold environment can gum up or
     freeze hard. A dry weapon has fewer misfires and malfunctions than a weapon with the wrong lubricant.

     6-6. Machine gun firing should consist of short, two or three round bursts fired at close intervals. After
     changing barrels, a hot barrel laid directly on snow or ice may warp or disappear in deep snow. Gunners or
     their assistants should carry a piece of wood with side ridges (to prevent the barrel from rolling out) for
     keeping hot or spare barrels off the snow.

     6-7. Weapon stabilization on snow or ice-covered ground is a challenge for crew-served weapons.
     Maintenance managers and weapon systems experts need to plan field expedient methods to keep weapons
     stable and employable. Where possible, good ideas should be tested under live fire field conditions to validate
     the solution. Consider the following when determining solutions:

          • Avoid altering how the weapon engages the enemy.
          • Keep weight to a minimum.
          • Avoid additional fuel or heating requirements.
          • Protect moving components and very hot parts of the weapon from snow and ice.
          • Seek command approval for deviations from procedures in doctrine or TMs.

     6-8. The greatest risk to a weapon is the cold effects on the metal itself. Rifle barrels are tempered to increase
     strength and resist warping under high heat and pressures. However, when tempered metal is exposed to
     freezing temperatures it can become brittle. The sudden increases in heat and pressure during discharge can
     stress the metal and create abnormally fast wear, or even small stress fractures. This is especially true of
     weapons fired in fully automatic mode. The use of weapon lubricants follows some of the same principles as

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  79


---

Chapter 6

     vehicle lubricants. At about 5°F (-15°C), operators and maintainers should transition to cold lubricants. Unit
     armorers should carry plenty of spare parts, especially those most prone to failure (firing pins, extractors,
     feed pawls, and so forth), and need to have a constant supply of cold weather lubricants.

             Note. Re-zero weapons when deploying from a temperate to a cold environment. Cold
             temperatures may cause a decrease in the burning rate of propellants, which can significantly
             change projectile trajectories. Re-zero with cold ammunition. Decreased burn rates will invalidate
             the previous zero of the weapons.

     6-9. When a weapon is brought inside a warm place, keeping it dry is the priority. It should be disassembled
     and wiped dry several times as it warms to room temperature. The alternative is to secure the weapon in an
     airtight anti-condensation container while it warms up.

     6-10. Cleaning and lubrication should be conducted inside a warm room and the weapon should be at room
     temperature, if possible. Weapons under storage should be cleaned and lubricated at least once every 90 days.
     Consider the following best practices to ensure weapons are always ready for use:

          • Apply a light coat of LAW to all functional parts.
          • Always work to keep a weapon dry. When moving a cold weapon into a warm place, condensation

                (moisture) will form in and on it. If possible, leave the weapons in a protected but cold area outside.
          • To minimize the condensation of moisture and freezing, keep the weapon covered when moving

                from warm to cold areas to allow gradual cooling. Use fabric not plastic covers.
          • Outside, unload and hand function the weapon every 30 minutes to help prevent freezing of

                functional parts.
          • Do not lay a warm weapon directly in snow or ice.
          • Keep the inside of magazines and ammunition dry. Moisture will freeze and cause malfunctions.
          • Do not lubricate ammunition.
          • Use muzzle cap, protective magazine bag, and an overall weapon cover to help protect the weapons.

                Use them whenever the tactical situation permits.

LUBRICANT APPLICATION

     6-11. DOD small arms multipurpose lubricant specifications require products to have cleaning, lubrication,
     and preservation properties. The DOD tests lubricants from a minimum flash point of 150°F (66°C), to a
     maximum pour point of -75°F (-59°C).

     6-12. Lubricants are designed to endure extreme heat and resist breakdown during the fast and repetitive
     cycling of automatic firearms, yet few can retain their viscosity when exposed to below freezing
     temperatures. In freezing weather, lubricants become gummy and begin to harden. As temperatures fall, some
     lubricants can even begin to freeze, rendering them almost as hard as epoxy. It is the responsibility of
     individual Soldiers, maintainers, and master gunners to know at what temperature their lubricants will fail
     and what the alternatives are.

     6-13. The solvent CLP is in fluid form down to -65°F (-54°C) and above, however at -10°F (-23°C) it begins
     to thicken enough to affect a weapon’s automatic cycling. Under cold conditions, even a small amount of
     CLP or lubricant, small arms (LSA) on moving parts can prevent some weapons from firing.

     6-14. Small arms repairer, armorers, and operators must be mindful that various weapons will have different
     lubrication requirements as temperatures drop. The exact temperature for any specific weapon’s transition to
     cold weather lubricants will differ. Planning for the transition of lubricants for cold weather operations should
     be communicated throughout the unit.

     6-15. Units should plan on converting from CLP or LSA to LAW once temperatures routinely fall below 5°F
     (-15°C) (Figure 6-1 and 6-2).

80  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                              Small Arms Preventive Maintenance

                  Figure 6-1. Cold weather weapon lubrication plan

                   Figure 6-2. Alternative lubrication plans for various weapons

6-16. Under cold conditions less than -10°F (-23°C), a small amount of CLP or LSA remaining on moving
parts can prevent weapons from firing. Ensure that all CLP or LSA is thoroughly removed from weapons
(preferably by using an SD-2 solvent) and apply LAW (MIL-PRF-14107D) prior to cold operations. LAW
has Teflon and synthetic additives that can be negatively affected by mixing them with other lubricants.

6-17. Apply a light coat of LAW to all functional parts. Soldiers should not use CLP, LSA, preservative
lubricants, special lubricating oils, automotive oils, grease (distilled petroleum or synthetic based), or rifle
bore cleaner (RBC) (unless specified by the TM) in difficult operational cold situations.

       Note. LSA and RBC are intended for use together. Never use RBC alone. It is a cleaner (not a
       lubricant or solvent) and does not have a lubricating property. RBC is designed to remove carbon
       buildup without stripping away the soaked-in oil in the way that SD-2 does. However, RBC
       decomposes CLP and LAW, and they are not compatible together in any application.

6-18. Exposed metal surfaces require more frequent applications of smaller amounts of lubrication to remain
fully functional in dry cold air. Whatever is used to lubricate a weapon in cold weather situations, it should
be used sparingly.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31                                        81


---

Chapter 6

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not use graphite on weapons. It accelerates galvanic corrosion on
                    alloys, and aluminum to steel (or iron) parts as they move against each
                    other.

WEAPON MALFUNCTIONS

     6-19. Cold adversely affects the functioning of small arms. Take care to correctly identify the problem and
     apply an appropriate corrective action.

SLUGGISHNESS

     6-20. A common weapons problem in cold weather is sluggish operation. Normal (not engineered for cold)
     lubricants thicken at low temperatures, causing stoppage or sluggish action of firearms. During the winter,
     weapons must be stripped completely and cleaned with an SD-2 to remove all lubricants and rust prevention
     compounds. Below 10°F (-12°C), LAW helps moving parts to slide better than CLP or LSA. LAW provides
     proper lubrication during the winter and helps minimize the freezing of snow and ice on weapons. Select the
     correct lubricant and clean off all regular gun oils before applying the appropriate cold weather lubricant.
     Avoid allowing different lubricants to become mixed on the surface of a weapon.

METAL BREAKAGE

     6-21. The hardened metal parts of automatic weapons are more brittle than soft metal in cold temperatures.
     When the weapon is fired at below freezing temperatures, parts will at first micro fracture and then suddenly
     break within the initial burst of fire. If possible, weapons should first be fired at a slow rate (short bursts) of
     fire to provide gradual warming of the weapon. Short bursts provide a less stressful uniform expansion of the
     weapon’s metals. Once the parts have warmed up, the rate of fire may be increased.

PLASTIC AND RESIN BREAKAGE

     6-22. Sudden changes in temperature can cause plastic parts to crack. Wrapping the weapon in a blanket or
     poncho before bringing it from a cold to a warm area helps warm it gradually.

JAMMING AND MISFIRE

     6-23. Snow and ice on ammunition can obstruct cyclic action of automatic weapons and increase the chances
     of a misfire. Ice build-up inside a weapon can foul its operation just as surely as a heavy carbon buildup.
     Worse, the snow or ice on ammunition can prevent a bolt from seating and result in a backfire. Regularly
     check magazines and ammunition belts to ensure that they are free of snow and ice. Keep muzzles covered,
     ejector ports closed, and do not lay weapons down on the ground.

     6-24. Do not lay a warm weapon directly in snow or ice. Automatic weapons with gas pistons must keep
     their cylinder vents or gas regulators clear of moisture or water that could freeze and stall the cyclic action.

     6-25. Blowing snow tends to get into working parts, sights, barrels, magazines, and ammunition, especially
     when moving in deep snow under combat conditions. The use of the muzzle cap, protective magazine bag,
     and an overall weapon cover will help protect weapons. Use them whenever the tactical situation permits but
     remove them prior to firing.

     6-26. Unload and conduct weapon function check every 30 minutes to help prevent freezing of functional
     parts. Be careful not to drop ammunition rounds into the snow.

AMMUNITION MALFUNCTION

     6-27. Cold weather affects the performance of small arms ammunition, artillery rounds, and rockets. The
     general considerations are—

          • Cold air is denser than temperate weather air. This creates greater drag and reduces range.

82  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                             Small Arms Preventive Maintenance

     • Severe cold slows chemical reaction processes, reducing the propulsion energy of a round exiting a
          tube or the pressure of flame exiting a nozzle.

     • Unpacked munitions moving from cold to warm areas are subject to the same condensation threat
          as weapons. Munitions usually cannot be lubricated to protect them from moisture corrosion.

6-28. Resupply in cold climates is also difficult, especially the resupply of heavy, bulky ammunition. Soldiers
must practice ammunition economy and fire discipline to reduce resupply requirements. Ammunition should
be kept at the same temperature as the weapon and should be carried in bandoleers. Loaded clips, magazines,
or single rounds dropped into the snow are often lost. Careful handling of ammunition is essential. Additional
ammunition should be protected in the pockets of the parka or rucksack.

6-29. Store ammunition in original containers for as long as possible. Try to keep storage containers raised
off the ground and covered with tarpaulins, ponchos, salvage tents, or any other material that affords
protection from the snow. Storage location of ammunition on the ground should be marked with poles to
assist with relocating it if the storage containers become snow covered.

6-30. Ammunition, clips, and magazines must be cleaned of all oil and preservative and must be checked
frequently. Remove all snow, ice and condensation. The most common cause of malfunctions and weapon
stoppage is ice and snow on the ammunition. Ammunition is prone to dragging snow and ice into a weapon.
Once it is removed from its packaging, it should be regularly checked to ensure it is free of ice and snow.
Keep cartridge containers, magazines, and ammunition drums closed to prevent the formation of rust or ice.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  83


---

                                                 Chapter 7

 Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire
                            Control Equipment

    Large weapon systems have unique vulnerabilities to cold. These weapons often face a range
    of maintenance issues when exposed to cold or arctic conditions. This chapter provides general
    considerations and recommendations to maintain these weapon systems operational in cold or
    arctic environment.

EFFECTS OF COLD WEATHER

     7-1. All large weapons systems have both unique system vulnerabilities and common concerns in the cold.
     This section provides a general overview of the issues that most commonly affect all large weapon systems.

WEAPON FUNCTION

     7-2. Like small arms, cold weather conditions can adversely affect the proper functioning and operation of
     weapons, ammunition, and fire-control equipment. Special care and procedures must be followed to keep
     them functioning properly, affecting all levels from the operator to maintenance facilities. The following
     problems or conditions affect the operation of weapons, ammunition, and fire-control weapon systems:

          • The increased viscosity of hydraulic or recoil fluids caused by lower temperatures offers greater
                resistance to motion. This results in stiffness of operation and shortening of the recoil cycle.

          • Over-lubricating in cold weather may cause parts to bind, resulting in misfires.
          • Handwheels on both elevating and traversing mechanisms require greater effort.
          • Gas-check pads in the breach of a weapon fail to seal perfectly and tend to deteriorate rapidly.

                Scoring of the gas-check seat is possible.
          • Cable insulation that is not designed for arctic conditions may fail when doubled or straightened.
          • Frozen handbrakes on towed weapons may be difficult to release; attempting to move the weapons

                without thawing may cause serious damage.
          • Equalizing bars and travel-locks of towed weapons must be covered or wrapped before towing over

                snow covered terrain. If the wrapping becomes wet, replace immediately to prevent freezing of both
                the wrapper and the protected mechanism.
          • In cross-country operations, use a prime mover with the same tread or track width as the towed
                weapon to ensure proper tracking. Towed weapons not tracking in the wheel furrows of the tractor
                vehicle will jump from side to side and make it hard to control driving.
          • Firing lanyards must be kept dry and covered to prevent freezing and breakage.
          • Cold air is denser than warm air, creating more resistance to a projectile passing through it, and
                more force pushing the projectile off course in a crosswind.

CLIMATIC CONDITIONS

     7-3. Severe conditions can interfere with the proper functioning of weapons, ammunition, and fire control
     equipment. Knowledge of these effects can minimize their impact on mission accomplishment.

     7-4. There may be an increased need for ammunition in cold weather depending on the type; cold
     temperatures adversely affect firing, effectiveness, and accuracy. Also, it is often necessary to fire several
     light-to-medium-zone rounds prior to maximum propellant charges to avoid stressing the hydraulic recoil
     mechanisms of mortars, cannons, or howitzers. Support units must be prepared to handle and transport the
     added volume of ammunition and deal with increased difficulty in preparing ammunition storage areas in
     extreme cold climates.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  85


---

Chapter 7

Operational Considerations

     7-5. In cold environments, Soldiers will not be able to freely carry cold-soaked weapons and optics in and
     out of heated buildings and shelters. Doing so will cause a condensation buildup, followed by water freezing
     that can lock down the weapon’s action or blind the optics with ice crystals.

     7-6. Leadership will need a plan to manage weapon serviceability as Soldiers move between the cold and
     heated environments. If it is determined that weapons are to be kept outside in the cold, Soldiers will need
     weapon security policies and procedures.

Cold-Dry Conditions

     7-7. Cold-dry weather causes sluggish motion and increased stress on moving parts lubricated by products
     manufactured for temperate climates. Rubberized parts and surfaces, as well as painted surfaces, are more
     likely to crack and break.

Transporting

     7-8. Vehicle operators must take extra precautions to cover weapon systems and ammunition before
     movement. The wind pressure created by a moving vehicle can force water into even the smallest spaces and
     crevices in cargo. Truck beds need to be equipped with a complete set of suspension hoops and tarps. Towed
     weapons should be covered with fitted tarps and coverings to protect gears, springs, recoil slides, and any
     moving components from rain and snow.

     7-9. Where weapons are to be mounted on open turrets, fitted covers can help to keep them from becoming
     clogged with ice. The advantage of a fitted cover is its ability to be quickly removed, allowing for rapid
     response to threats.

Frozen Surfaces

     7-10. Frozen ground alters the relationship between any weapon system and its ground placement. Firing of
     flat trajectory weapons, like tank guns, may cause the vehicle chassis to move away or slide backward from
     the direction of fire on frozen surfaces. Emplacing ground cleats for crew-served weapons (such as mortar
     baseplates and howitzer spades) becomes increasingly difficult and time consuming in frozen ground. The
     inability of frozen ground to effectively absorb the shock of weapons firing can lead to damage to the systems
     themselves and degrade accuracy.

     7-11. Most crew-served combat weapons need a natural base or gun platform to fire accurately. In warm
     weather, the ground provides a solid base and yet has enough resiliency to act as a shock absorber. If the
     weapon is emplaced on solid, frozen ground, there is no “give” that cushions the recoil or holds the weapon
     in place. Either all the shock of firing ends up being absorbed by the weapon itself, resulting in damage, or
     the hard slippery surface of the frozen ground allows the weapon to slide out of registration from its aiming
     points.

     7-12. Maintenance personnel need to anticipate support requirements to fabricate means to stabilize weapon
     systems. These ad hoc devices will have to be safe for the user and mitigate the effects that cold will have on
     weapon performance.

     7-13. Weapons mounted on vehicle platforms are well-suited for supplemental heating systems. Electrical
     engine oil or battery heating pads can be utilized to keep the oil in recoil mechanisms fluid.

Snow and Ice

     7-14. Exposed gears and racks, including the elevating arc, traversing rack, and pinions, can accumulate
     enough snow and ice to impede movement. Additionally, snow and ice can contaminate lubricants and
     ammunition creating a gunky paste that obstructs the movement of gears or moving parts.

     7-15. Operators need to be prepared for such challenges by keeping their weapons covered. The barrels of
     weapons should have covers. Tarps are ideal for protecting weapons and other equipment susceptible to
     corrosion or fluid contamination.

86  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

Magnetic Conditions

     7-16. When traveling north, it is crucial to be careful with magnetic instruments. These instruments can be
     affected by changes in declination and by the Aurora Borealis. Maintainers should understand that some
     issues with the equipment may not be due to damage, but rather because of its proximity to the magnetic
     poles.

FREEING A SEIZED WEAPON

     7-17. Before handling any weapon, always perform a thorough inspection to ensure whether it is loaded. If
     the weapon is found to be loaded, take appropriate precautions. Subsequently, the maintainer must ascertain
     whether the mechanism is jammed due to component failure, ice obstruction, or solidified lubricant. Avoid
     forcing a jammed mechanism to move. There are three primary methods for freeing weapons and their firing
     platforms, that are the application of:

          • Nontoxic antifreeze spray.
          • Direct heat.
          • SD-2 cleaner.

Deicing with Propylene Alcohol

     7-18. For de-icing helicopters and aircraft, use a non-toxic propylene-glycol water mixed solution. The
     military also uses this chemical as an antifreeze in commercial products under warranty as prescribed by the
     manufacturer’s instructions. Undiluted, it is used for temperatures of 370°F (188°C) down to -60°F (-51°C).
     Though non-toxic, it still must be used with a containment system to capture the used liquid so that it cannot
     seep into the ground and water table. Propylene-glycol based antifreezes shall not be mixed with ethylene-
     glycol based antifreeze or any other lubricant. Propylene-glycol based antifreezes are very seldom used in
     Army ground vehicles.

Deicing with Heat

     7-19. For ice-obstructed gear mechanisms, use direct heat to free frozen brake pads and clear moving parts.
     Space heaters, forced air heaters, propane heaters, and ducted air heaters (two stage heating) can be used to
     defrost immobilized gears and parts. Be careful not to fast heat or spot heat parts to temperatures above 90°F
     (32°C) because this may burn plastics, scorch parts or carbonize lubricants. As a field expedient solution, a
     tarp can be draped over a weapon system to funnel hot vehicle exhaust and warm the equipment. An exhaust
     pipe flexible hose extension will need to be obtained in advance.

Solidified Lubricant Removal

     7-20. If the lubricant (oil or grease) in a weapon system has frozen or solidified, it must be dissolved,
     removed, and replaced. Warm the weapon to above freezing first, then apply a penetrating oil to loosen the
     hardened lubricant, allowing some time for the oil to take effect. Once a hardened lubricant is loose, it is
     corrupted and must be removed. Removal of hardened lubricants requires the use of SD-2, followed by
     replacement of the correct cold weather lubricant in very moderate amounts. This process is mainly for large
     weapon systems but also applies to vehicles and small arms.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Take extreme care to ensure that moisture, snow, ice, and dirt are not
                    introduced into weapon system’s mechanisms during the changeover
                    process.

PROCEDURES FOR LUBRICATING WEAPONS

     7-21. It is important to keep machined surfaces clean and lubricated (but not over lubricated). Insufficient
     lubrication may cause rust to form on uncoated surfaces, increasing friction between rubbing surfaces and

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  87


---

Chapter 7

     impairing the functioning of the weapon. Excessive lubrication can impede the motion of the components or
     result in a buildup of gummy oil or solidified grease.

     7-22. Always use the appropriate type and grade of lubricant. The physical properties of oils and greases vary
     with changing temperatures. A lubricant designed for use at room temperature may become thick and
     unsatisfactory at below freezing temperatures or too thin to lubricate metal surfaces at high temperatures.
     Lubricants can also be selected because they are environmentally preferable.

     7-23. Field maintenance facilities winterize artillery weapons. Prior to issue, recoil mechanisms must be
     modified to ensure satisfactory operations at temperatures down to -65°F (-54°C). Typically, preparation of
     artillery requires special winterization of component parts. Follow instructions in LOs and TMs and consider
     these general guidelines:

          • Lubricants must be applied in smaller quantities more frequently. For example, a biweekly interval
                may need to be changed to a weekly reapplication.

          • Wide temperature range grease is recommended for use on artillery at all temperature ranges. Use
                wide temperature range when special lubricating grease is specified. It retains superior lubricating
                qualities at extremely low temperatures.

          • Petroleum based hydraulic fluid, identified as OHT or FRH, are recommended for use in hydraulic
                gears as well as in hydro-spring and hydro-pneumatic recoil mechanisms. These products effectively
                serve as the replacements for specialized recoil and light recoil oils used in low temperature
                operations. The transition to petroleum based hydraulic oil in recoil mechanisms is conducted
                according to the following procedures:
                ▪ Drain the existing recoil oil. Raise, lower, and rotate the mechanism to help remove the original
                     oil.
                ▪ Fill recoil mechanism with OHT or FRH as specified. Establish an oil reserve and install
                     mechanisms on carriage or mount.

     7-24. Replace preservative lubricating oil, bore cleaner, or normal lubricating oil with CLP in cold
     temperatures, except in the bores of mortars where copper remover and LAW may be used. To put a
     temporary finish on corroded exterior metal surfaces, use solid film lubricant except on moving parts and
     stocks. Always refer to the specific equipment TM for lubricants and cleaners that are for use in the bore of
     any weapon.

     7-25. Follow all federal, state, and local laws and regulations and unit operating procedures regarding the
     storage, transportation, and final disposition of all greases, oils, and solvents. Spills of these materials must
     be promptly cleaned up and reported via the chain of command according to the unit’s spill-plan.

HOWITZER, MORTAR, AND DIRECT FIRE WEAPONS

     7-26. Large-bore weapons require ground stabilization and recoil recovery mechanisms that keep them aimed
     at targets which the operators can’t see. From the operator perspective, tanks and self-propelled artillery
     cannon are well protected from the cold, although they may face issues related to condensation. Towed
     mortars and cannons and open vehicle mounted gun systems require additional attention to ensure they
     remain operational.

COMMON CONCERNS

                                                 CAUTION

                    Under cold conditions below -10°F (-23°C), a small amount of CLP or
                    LSA remaining on moving parts can prevent a weapon from firing.
                    Ensure that all CLP or LSA is thoroughly removed from the weapon and
                    apply LAW prior to cold operations.

     7-27. Most of the problems and precautions involving operations and maintenance of artillery pieces in the
     cold are the same as for weapons in general. However, consider the following aspects of artillery operations

88  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

     in cold regions:
          • Recoil oil indicators may show low readings when cold. Firing a few rounds warms the oil, elevating
                the indicator level.
          • Artillery tube bores require more frequent cleaning at low temperatures due to the increased residue
                left from the reduced combustion of propellant charges. However, bore cleaner can freeze in the
                chamber and prevent loading a round. CLP is an authorized alternative to bore cleaner and remains
                effective down to -10°F (-23°C). LAW is preferred below that temperature.
          • Special care is required for gascheck pads. A dry cloth is enough to clean the gascheck pad and
                electrical ring mechanism.
          • Adjustable recoil respirators should be left open as far as possible before firing the first round in
                cold weather. Respirators should be kept clear of ice and snow.
          • Congealed lubricants and grit hinder movement of elevation and traversing handwheels.
          • Some towed weapons use year-round lubricants, even in cold weather.
          • Only the ammunition needed for immediate use should be prepared for firing to prevent snow and
                ice from affecting the propellants.
          • On frozen surfaces, both stability and accuracy diminish at lower firing elevations.
          • The recommended choices for firing surfaces should be shallow muskeg (or bog), gravel, frozen
                ground, and ice, in that order. Waste lubricants can be applied to trails and spades that are used in
                snow and ice to prevent them from freezing to the ground. Laying tree boughs or straw under trails
                and spades can also help. These cushioning materials can be placed under firing jacks and firing
                platforms. Even with cushioning, firing from ice is one of the greatest causes of damage to artillery
                pieces in cold regions.
          • Crews wearing the bulky cold region uniform, particularly handwear, must practice drills and
                maintenance to gain proficiency in cold weather operations.

     7-28. Cold weather significantly impacts not only personnel and equipment, but also ammunition. The
     burning rates of various types of propellant charges are reduced by the cold. When cold-soaked ammunition
     is fired from a weapon that was zeroed under temperate conditions, the zero or targeting registration will
     change. Weapons that will be used in cold temperatures should be zeroed or registered in similar conditions.

     7-29. Cold weather can affect the accuracy of weapons and the performance of ammunition. Weapons that
     are fired from warmed turrets should be loaded with similarly warmed ammunition. Weapons that are fired
     from cold ambient temperatures should be loaded with ammunition that is also cold. The ammunition should
     be kept at the same temperature as the weapon. Additional ammunition should be stored in its shipping
     containers, covered, and stored off the ground and remain in its protective containers, boxes, or cans as much
     as possible.

     7-30. While cold, dry conditions do not drastically alter the terminal effects of direct fire weapons, deep
     snow-covered surfaces tend to diminish the blast effects of artillery and mortar rounds with point detonating
     fuses. The total effect is that range and burst effects drop off dramatically, requiring added tactical training
     in adjusting fire and operational planning for crews of indirect fire systems.

     7-31. At the firing point, condensation on a round that freezes in the breech can lead to a misfire. Icing can
     cause misfires, damage to the equipment, and injuries to crew members. The misfire procedures used in
     temperate climates for a round stuck in a howitzer must be modified for cold regions. Pouring a solution high
     in antifreeze down the tube can be effective in dislodging a stuck round. Another technique involves the use
     of hydraulic fluid, which does not freeze and acts like penetrating oil to help dislodge the stuck round.

             Note. The use of antifreeze or hydraulic fluid to free a live round obstruction must be supported
             by command policy or have the commander’s approval.

BREECH AND FIRING MECHANISMS

     7-32. A frozen breechblock (figure 7-1 on page 90) usually cannot be forced to move. If ice prevents opening
     or closing the breech, use a portable heater for thawing. Remove the breechblock and dry it thoroughly.
     Otherwise, keep the breech mechanism tightly covered.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  89


---

Chapter 7

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not use dry cleaning solvent, mineral spirits, paint thinner, or RBC
                    on gascheck pad. Simply wipe it clean and permit it to dry; do not
                    lubricate.

                          Figure 7-1. Cannon breechblock and obturator mechanism

     7-33. Clean all parts daily, except for gascheck pads, using dry cleaning solvent or mineral spirits (MIL-PRF-
     680, Degreasing Solvent) or lubricate as prescribed in the TM.

     7-34. Frozen manual and electric firing linkages render a weapon useless. Frozen solenoids do not close
     contacts for electric firing.

     7-35. After firing, the breech and firing mechanisms of weapons using fixed and semifixed ammunition
     should be disassembled, cleaned with dry-cleaning solvent or mineral spirits, dried, and oiled sparingly.
     Mechanisms on weapons using separate loading ammunition should be disassembled. All parts (except
     gascheck pads and electrical ring mechanisms) should be cleaned with RBC, dried, and oiled sparingly.

     7-36. The asbestos covering of Gerdom-type gas check pads becomes very brittle in cold temperatures. If the
     asbestos cracks and exposes the wire mesh, it can score the gascheck seat, making it impossible to repair. A
     new pad is required if wire is exposed.

     7-37. The breech and firing mechanisms must be completely disassembled for cleaning and lubrication.
     Clean all parts thoroughly, then apply a light film of LAW by wiping the surfaces with a clean cloth that has
     been wet with oil and thoroughly wrung out. Excessive lubrication of the firing mechanism can lead to
     misfires.

RECOIL MECHANISMS

     7-38. All hydro-pneumatic and hydro-spring recoil mechanisms should be filled with hydraulic fluid (either
     OHT or FRH) as specified. Monitor the length of recoil during cold weather firing. Take precautions to
     prevent snow, water, or dirt from entering the reservoir. Hydro-pneumatic mechanisms are affected by a
     reduction in gas pressure at low temperatures, as well as by the thickening of recoil oil.

                                                 WARNING

                    The possibility of injury to personnel or damage to equipment is
                    present when adjusting gas pressure in the recuperator. Therefore,
                    adjusting the gas pressure in the recuperator is a function of field
                    maintenance and sustainment maintenance support. The person in
                    charge of the unit is responsible for having the gas pressure in the
                    recuperator adjusted to correspond with the existing temperature
                    conditions.

90  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

     7-39. Care of recoil mechanisms is nearly the same during cold weather as it is under normal conditions.
     Operators must closely monitor the recoil mechanisms. In self-propelled guns, the recoil fluid surrounds the
     gun tube and is subject to a large range of temperature changes and subsequent pressure variations. It is
     important to monitor these pressure changes and adjust as necessary to maintain the required pressure range.

     7-40. The recoil cycle may take longer than usual while the oil is cold. As additional rounds are fired, the
     action gradually heats the recoil oil and thins it, restoring the cycle time to normal. A sticking recoil
     mechanism may result in severe damage to the weapon when it is fired; therefore—

          • Exercise the recoil mechanism frequently. The frequency of exercise depends on the existing
                temperature and becomes more frequent as the temperature decreases.

          • To prevent recoil parts from frost binding, exercise the recoil mechanism before firing whenever
                the weapon is exposed to freezing rain, windblown snow and ice, or changing temperatures.

          • Refer to pertinent weapon TMs for methods of exercising the recoil mechanism.

     7-41. If the recoil mechanism is equipped with an adjustable respirator, it should be fully opened when firing
     in low temperatures.

     7-42. Check the oil level of the recoil mechanism at the intervals prescribed in the applicable TM and
     whenever there is significant change in temperature. Inspect all partially filled hydraulic fluid containers to
     avoid the possibility of using contaminated fluid. Properly discard any contaminated fluid.

RECOIL SLIDES
