     7-43. Friction between recoil slides and guides absorbs an appreciable amount of recoil energy. Thickened
     or congealed lubricants increase friction, shorten recoil, and retard counter-recoil. Snow and condensation
     on the slides contaminate the lubricant and destroy its lubricating properties. To ensure proper recoil and
     counter-recoil action, remove the old lubricant from the slides every day by using SD-2 (or as a last resort,
     mineral spirits paint thinner). Smooth all surfaces and lubricate lightly.

     7-44. When exposed to windblown snow and ice, dry operation of the recoil slides and other exposed metal
     working surfaces may be necessary. Lubrication, however, should be applied during standby periods. Guns
     treated with dry lubricants need only be kept clean and free of ice and snow.

EQUILIBRATORS

     7-45. Equilibrators (figure 7-2) can be found on machine guns and cannons where most of weight is forward
     of the elevation pivot point. Equilibrators can be made of pull-springs, push-pistons, or wound-springs used
     to act as a counterbalance mechanism.

                                              Figure 7-2. Equilibrator

7-46. Clean, dry, and lightly lubricate the piston rods or tubes of equilibrators every day during cold weather
operations to prevent icing. Carefully examine and remove any corrosion or marring of the smooth, unpainted
surfaces with crocus cloth.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  91


---

Chapter 7

                                                 CAUTION

                    Some howitzers use nitrogen to pressurize the equilibrators. Equilibrator
                    pressures require frequent checks due to temperature fluctuations (see
                    operator’s TM). If a cold howitzer is brought into a warm shelter,
                    pressures will increase dramatically. If the elevation handwheel is then
                    released, the howitzer will elevate rapidly, possibly causing injury to the
                    user and damage to the equipment or the building.

     7-47. Use wide temperature change lubricant (MIL-PRF-81322) to lubricate the designated parts of the
     equilibrator at the intervals prescribed in the LOs and the TMs. Too much lubricant at cold temperatures may
     cause the gun to stay out of battery after firing. This is compounded if the pressure in the equilibrators has
     not been adjusted for cold temperatures. Wash the bearing thoroughly with dry-cleaning solvent or mineral
     spirits paint thinner, dry thoroughly, and lubricate. It is necessary to remove the equilibrator to wash the
     bearings properly.

     7-48. After daily cleaning and drying of the equilibrator piston rod or tube, protect the smooth, unpainted
     surfaces against corrosion by applying a light film of LAW. Wipe the metal surfaces with a clean cloth that
     has been wet with oil and thoroughly wrung out.

     7-49. Adjust the nitrogen pressure of pneumatic-type equilibrators to provide proper equalizing action. If the
     equilibrator is equipped with a low-temperature control, make the adjustment according to the temperature
     scale provided.

DAILY CARE

     7-50. Inspect weapon systems daily. Whenever possible, use gun covers and shelters for protection. The
     following points are important in providing maximum protection for weapons:

          • Keep all parts thoroughly clean.
          • Clean and oil the breech mechanism daily.
          • Lubricate sparingly.
          • Do not let snow and ice collect on moving parts.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Never use graphite on an aluminum receiver weapon. The graphite
                    reacts chemically with aluminum and will damage the weapon.

WEAPON BORES

     7-51. In extremely cold weather, weapon bores are more susceptible to increased impact loads and may crack
     due to metal brittleness. Constant inspection and care are required to prevent failure. Do not assume that a
     weapon is rugged and mechanically sound. Microfractures pose an ongoing concern for tank, mortar, and
     artillery crews. Mortars and cannons incur microfractures every time the tube is fired. When those
     microfractures merge into visible fractures, the tube becomes a threat to the crew. Ordnance weapons
     inspectors must determine if a mortar or cannon has reached the end of its useful service life and must be
     retired.

Daily Care

     7-52. In below freezing temperatures, wipe the bore dry daily and apply a light film of LAW. Exposed metal
     surfaces require more frequent applications of smaller amounts of lubrication to remain fully functional in
     the cold-dry air. Any lubricant used for weapons in cold weather situations should be applied sparingly. LSA
     and RBC are designed to be used together. Never use RBC alone; its residue has no lubricating or
     anticorrosive affect. The advantage of RBC is that it is mild enough that it doesn’t extract the oil that has

92         TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

     socked into the micro-fractures the way SD-2 would, which affords some protection from rusting caused by
     humidity.

     7-53. Ensure all CLP or LSA is thoroughly removed from a weapon before applying LAW. LAW has Teflon
     and synthetic additives that are degraded when mixed with other lubricants. Clean weapons with SD-2 during
     the change over from one lubricant to another. Do not use SD-2 on rubber, plastic, or sealed buffers. Apply
     a light coat of LAW to all functional parts. Soldiers should not use CLP, LSA, preservative lubricants, special
     lubricating oils, automotive oils, grease (petroleum or synthetic based), or RBC (unless specified by the TM)
     in difficult operational cold situations. Do not use SD-2 and then leave a weapon to sit without fresh
     lubrication.

Before Firing

     7-54. Before firing, wipe the bore and chamber dry. Clean and coat bore evacuator, muzzle brake, blast
     deflector, and counterweight as prescribed in the TM.

During Firing

     7-55. At every opportunity during firing, inspect the muzzle end of the tube, bore evacuator, muzzle brake,
     blast deflector, counterweight (as applicable), and the breech ring. Check for the development of cracks.

     7-56. In cold temperatures, metal can become brittle and more susceptible to failure when subjected to the
     impact loads experienced during weapon firing. The presence of cracks generally indicates materiel
     deficiencies or metal fatigue. However, it is important to note that tool marks can be mistaken for cracks, and
     some cracks may not be easily visible. If cracks are detected, stop firing immediately, and notify immediate
     supervisor and maintenance support.

After Firing

     7-57. Due to the colder temperatures, burnt artillery and mortar propellants are more likely to cool and
     accumulate rapidly, leading to the buildup of carbon or creosote. After each cleaning, ensure to wipe dry and
     apply a TM-approved lubricant. When using RBC, make sure to thoroughly clean the cannon bore after firing
     and for two consecutive days thereafter.

     7-58. CLP can be used as a cold weather bore cleaner and preservative down to -10°F (-23°C). LAW is
     preferred below that temperature. If LAW is not available, clean the bore evacuator, muzzle brake, bore, and
     chamber with RBC while the weapon is still warm but not too hot to be touched with bare hands. For
     temperatures below -20°F (-29°C), warm the solvent cleaning compound so that it is thin enough to use
     effectively. All cleaner residue must be wiped off. Any residue remaining in the tube will freeze and make
     firing dangerous. Complete the second and third cleaning of the bore and chamber after firing, as prescribed
     for mild and moderate conditions in the pertinent LOs and TMs.

CRADLE, SLEIGH, CARRIAGE, AND MOUNT

     7-59. Disassemble the mechanism according to unit policy to access all components. Thoroughly clean all
     parts, ensuring that all rust, dirt, and old lubricant are removed. After cleaning, apply the prescribed lubricant
     sparingly as prescribed in the LOs and TMs.

EXERCISING WEAPONS

     7-60. Weapons should be elevated and traversed at regular intervals to ensure they are operational when
     needed. The rammer should be cycled several times before loading rounds and at regular intervals to
     guarantee proper operation. Although recoil mechanisms can only move short distance in below freezing
     temperatures, always exercise the recoil mechanism before firing to ensure that no parts are frozen or iced
     up.

     7-61. Snow and ice particles frequently collect on the arcs and pinions and cake under pressure of the gears,
     which can interfere with elevating and traversing. Remove the snow by brushing vigorously with a stiff bristle
     or wire brush. After snow is removed, the parts should be left dry for firing or swabbed with a light application
     of LAW to permit smooth and easy operation and prevent rusting.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  93


---

Chapter 7

USE OF COVERS

     7-62. When materiel is idle for a period of time, it should be securely covered for protection. Wind will drive
     snow under the covers unless they are securely fastened.

     7-63. Prevent tents, tarps, or other types of fabric materiel from freezing to the ground. Make a footing of
     planks, brush, or matting (straw or hay can also be used). Pedestals, rails, outriggers, skids, generating units,
     and points of tripods that go into the ground can be covered with grease to prevent them from freezing to the
     ground. Seal exposed openings to ensure that parts are free of ice or snow. Keep the ends of canvas tarpaulins
     off the ground to prevent them from freezing to the ground.

TRAVEL

     7-64. Before starting a road march, make a thorough inspection and provide as much protection as possible
     for all parts as follows:

          • Ensure all covers are properly installed and securely lashed. If covers are inadequate, improvise by
                using canvas, burlap, or any other suitable or available material.

          • Perform all preventive maintenance operations and precautions prescribed in manuals pertinent to
                the materiel.

          • During travel, exercise extra caution when driving, as suspension assemblies become stiff in the
                cold and prone to breaking.

          • Wheeled vehicles and trailer systems must have chains available.

EMPLACEMENT

     7-65. Selecting and preparing weapon sites in ice and snow requires more consideration than when emplaced
     on bare, level ground. If ice and snow are melting, select a site that will not become mired or flooded.

     7-66. Prepare a platform utilizing pierced steel planking (materiel often used for the improvised airstrips),
     boards, brush, and matting at the designated spot. Maneuver the weapon onto the platform so it adequately
     supports both the wheels and firing jack float (or auxiliary firing jack platform). Prepare recoil pits and spade
     positions. When an artillery piece is to be fired from a soft, spongy, surface, a deeper recoil pit must be dug
     to prepare for the sinking of the weapon during firing.

     7-67. Coat all metal parts of the trails and spades that are in contact with snow or frozen ground with waste
     lubricant. This prevents freezing and facilitates the subsequent shifting of the trails or baseplates. Waste
     lubricants may be used on any parts (except rubber) that contact the ground.

     7-68. Special, large frost-spades or spade attachments may be improvised to suit local conditions. In hard,
     frozen ground, protect trails against buckling and breakage by placing logs between the spades and ground.
     This provides added resilience.

     7-69. The firing jack and its locking lug may become covered with ice and frozen mud in transit. Ice and
     mud must be entirely removed before the jack can be completely lowered and locked in firing position. Swab
     the exterior of the jack with grease, molybdenum disulfide (known as GMD) and see that all seals are tight
     and serviceable.

                                                 CAUTION

                    Do not pack grease in the jack housing.

     7-70. Aiming posts should not be driven into frozen ground. Make a hole with a pick or crowbar first. A 1½
     inch screw-auger bit mounted to a 2-3 foot metal bar topped with a T-bar can be constructed for use as a drill.
     Create a 9-12 inch hole for planting aiming posts into frozen ground. Ground supports such as tripods or flag
     stands can be used on top of frozen ground or ice to support aiming posts. Use of the aiming light, equipped
     with dry-cell batteries, is possible only for short periods. Batteries can be kept warm and serviceable by
     carrying them or the light next to the body.

94  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

MORTARS

                                                 WARNING

                    Condensation and moisture can make the disassembly of large mortars
                    dangerously slippery. Wrap a rope or strap around the breach cap neck
                    above the pinion ball before removing the tube from the base plate
                    trunnion socket. This rope or strap provides a reliable handhold. It is
                    critical during hang-fire procedures to hold the strap and not wrap it
                    around the wrist.

     7-71. All infantry mortars have certain characteristics in cold weather operation that are common to most
     systems (Figure 7-3). The following technical procedures have operator maintenance principles that overlap
     with crew tactical procedures. Otherwise, consult with the individual item TM.
     7-72. Mortars are predominantly comprised of bare metal. They cannot be handled without contacting the
     metal, unlike other infantry weapons with wooden or plastic handles and stocks. Hand protection must always
     be worn (contact gloves). The crew must keep their gloves or mittens on and avoid touching the metal surface
     with bare flesh.
     7-73. It is essential that mortar crewmen use anti-contact gloves when temperatures fall below 20°F (-7°C).
     These gloves help prevent cold burns while still providing enough dexterity. Gloves must fit tightly; when
     ammunition is dropped into the tube, it creates a vacuum that could suck a glove in and create a hazard.

      Figure 7-3. M25A1 components needing protection from cold and ice clogging

7-74. Breathing onto equipment like sights or on the mortar’s ballistic computer will cause fogging and
freezing. Gunners should practice operating the mortar without breathing on the optics. When not in use,
optical instruments should be stored in a sheltered location that is unheated, preferably in their cases. This
protects them against cold, condensation, and shock.

7-75. Use muzzle and sight covers when not firing the weapon to prevent snow and ice from entering the
tube. The mortar’s ballistic computer is programmed to accept temperatures down to -50°F (-46°C). This
automatically compensates for cold-induced slow burning of charges when computing firing data. A mortar’s
ballistic computer is not programmed for temperature inputs colder than -50°F (-46°C).

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  95


---

Chapter 7

                                                 CAUTION

                    Anticontact gloves, secured by a wrist strap, should be used when
                    handling muzzle-loaded mortar shells. Avoid wearing bulky or loose-
                    fitting gloves, as they may get pinched between the round and the bore
                    of the mortar tube. This can occur when the assistant gunner follows
                    through on dropping the round, damaging equipment and resulting in
                    injury or death.

     7-76. Practice employing a compensated sight picture as the standard method when firing from frozen
     surfaces. Aiming stakes placed in snow will inevitably become loose. Use sandbags or an anchoring device
     to secure them firmly in place once set.

     7-77. Baseplates become brittle when exposed to severe cold. This coupled with the tremendous shock that
     the baseplate receives when a round is fired (and the decreased ability of frozen ground to absorb shock)
     results in baseplates being more prone to breakage than normal. Frozen ground has limited resiliency to
     cushion the full shock of firing, putting greater stress upon the baseplate and other bracing parts of the
     weapon.

     7-78. When emplacing a mortar, select a position with vegetation on the ground surface whenever possible.
     Follow these techniques to place mortar on frozen ground:

          • The mortar’s baseplate must be solidly positioned to prevent sliding. It may be necessary to dig into
                the ground.

          • Coat the bottom with waste oil to prevent freezing to ground surfaces.
          • Use two personnel on the bipod when seating on ice or at high elevation (1,300 mils or higher).

     7-79. Placing brush matting or any organic material under the baseplate is one field expedient method to
     prevent the baseplate from cracking. The matting should be thick enough to serve as a shock absorber, but
     not so thick that it causes the baseplate to bounce out of position.

     7-80. Another method of positioning is to place bags of dry sand or snow beneath the baseplate. The sandbags
     provide the weapon with a solid, yet resilient, shock-absorbing base. The baseplate is best seated by firing at
     a quadrant elevation of 1,200 mils using a middle charge. If the baseplate must be seated in snow, coat the
     bottom with waste lubricant to prevent freezing to the ground.

     7-81. Avoid using maximum or near-maximum charges if the mortar baseplates are seated on a frozen
     surface. Even when the baseplate appears to be properly seated when firing at elevations below 900 mils, the
     crew can expect the mortar to shift to the rear and even collapse.

     7-82. Cold affects mortar ammunition in the same way as other types of ammunition. Firing tables may be
     used provided the proper range corrections, based on cold weather conditions, are established through
     experience. Consult the applicable ammunition data sheets and applicable firing tables for charge restrictions
     at low temperatures. The following guidelines usually apply to ammunition in a winter warfare environment:

          • For best results, ammunition and mortars tubes should be about the same temperature.
          • Due to incomplete burning of propellants, expect a decrease in achieved range versus the plotted

                range. This decrease may be as much as 10% at -10°F (-23°C) and 20% at -40°F (-40°C).
          • Keep the ammunition and tube dry. Only open as many rounds as required for the current fire

                mission and use the provided tube cover afterward.
          • Due to incomplete burning of propellants, dry swab the tube after every tenth round or after every

                fire-for-effect. Swab bores thoroughly after each mission to remove any excess propellants or
                condensation.
          • Boresight frequently.

96  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                  Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

                                                 WARNING

                    When firing at low temperatures, double misfire wait-times due to
                    the possibility of delayed ignition. The new 60mm and 81mm
                    mortars employ a trigger mechanism that allows the firing pin to
                    be re-cocked and fired. With these mortars, misfire times do not
                    have to be increased.

     7-83. The metal used for mortar tubes becomes increasingly brittle in cold conditions. This increased
     brittleness leads to higher rates of microfracturing when the tube is fired. Mortar units should have their tubes
     inspected before conducting cold weather live fire operations to ensure the tubes will remain safe throughout
     the operation. It is important to inform the weapons inspectors that the weapon will be used in severe cold
     conditions.

     7-84. At the end of cold operations, mortar tubes should be returned to the weapons inspector to verify that
     the cold conditions have not caused significant accelerated degradation. Inform the weapons inspectors that
     the weapon has been subjected to live fire in severe cold conditions.

     7-85. Sight-mounts are prone to breaking if the baseplate is not solidly positioned. Remove the sight each
     time before firing until the baseplate is settled.

     7-86. Mortars present practically no lubrication or ice fog problems but are very susceptible to outer surface
     condensation. Allow at least 30 minutes before cleaning after the mortar is moved from a cold to a warm
     location. This helps avoid condensation from mixing with cleaning solutions and lubricants.

     7-87. Use LAW instead of general-purpose lubricant (also known as GPL) for lubricant and bore cleaning of
     the newer 60mm, 81mm, and 120mm mortars when the temperatures fall below 10°F (-12°C). The bore
     should be kept dry, and lubricant kept away from the firing pin. All moving parts and the bore should be
     checked for snow and ice before firing. Otherwise, use a heavy cloth muzzle bag to keep snow out of the
     bore. Never lubricate mortar shock absorbers.

             Note. Do not use CLP in the bore of a mortar tube. CLP may congeal and cause the tube to crack.

     7-88. Mortar gunners and assistant gunners typically adopt a standing position to avoid contact with the cold
     ground. In this position, they must properly execute round-firing and be conscious of added noise problems
     inherent with firing the mortar in the cold, dry air. Ear protection is essential.

TURRET STORED MUNITIONS

     7-89. Temperature changes can have major effects on tank and artillery ammunition. Lubrication and
     breakage problems are not as frequent as those for towed artillery pieces because most of the weapon’s
     working parts are enclosed in a warm turret.

     7-90. Ammunition stored inside the turret will be warm and have the same general ballistic characteristics as
     ammunition fired in temperate climates. Propellants tend to burn slower in the cold, reducing the velocity of
     projectiles. This effects cannon accuracy when gunners switch ammunition between that stored in a warm
     turret verses rounds stored outside in very cold weather. The weapon of an enclosed turret is usually zeroed
     with warmed ammunition. However, as a tank or armored artillery moves on to its next event, picking up
     fresh rounds can cause inconsistent trajectory performance. When cold ammunition is fired, the powder burns
     slowly causing completely different ballistic characteristics, rendering the initial zero useless.

     7-91. If possible, ammunition brought in from outside should be allowed to warm in the turret before firing.
     In a combat situation this may not be practical if ammunition needs to be used immediately. Temperature
     limitations are not stamped on all individual items of ammunition, but they can be found in the ammunition
     data sheets. Gunner must have their own data for cold ammunition or be prepared to quickly re-zero the
     weapon. In either case, make a sight adjustment or ensure the weapon is zeroed for the temperature in that it
     is being fired.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  97


---

Chapter 7

MISSILES AND ROCKETS

     7-92. Rockets and missiles differ from each other by their targeting systems. Rockets are dependent on the
     launcher’s targeting systems to function. Then once fired, rockets usually just follow a ballistic trajectory.
     Missiles usually have internal guidance systems that allow them to alter or adjust their flight path en route to
     the target. Both weapon types are very susceptible to ice and condensation in their optics, electronics, and
     lubricants. In all other regards, rocket and missile systems are like any other type of artillery system when it
     comes to cold weather maintenance. Consult the TMs applicable to a particular rocket or missile to determine
     exact firing limits.

ROCKETS

     7-93. A rocket is a self-propelled, unguided projectile. Rockets generally operate satisfactorily in cold
     weather (freezing cold, down to -40°F [-40°C]). Still, particular attention must be taken by handlers,
     transporters, and operators to keep connectors, rockets, and rocket pods dry. If gets into the crevices of
     components, it can freeze and result in ice expansion that can damage sensitive circuitry.

MISSILES

     7-94. A missile is a self-propelled projectile with a guidance system capable of altering direction of flight
     from a ballistic path. Consult the specific TM for cold weather storage, transport, and operation.

                                                 WARNING

                    Double the back blast area of all missile systems in cold weather.

Light Missiles

                                                 CAUTION

                    Electrically powered tracking and guidance systems encounter
                    difficulties in obtaining an effective electrical ground when firing from
                    frozen surfaces. This may be overcome through training or the use of
                    expedient grounding techniques.

     7-95. Light missiles are designed to be carried or loaded by an individual Soldier. These missiles are often
     guided by optical equipment that is susceptible to condensation build up on the lenses. This buildup can
     hamper line of sight missile target acquisition and tracking. However, this issue is not significant for radar
     guided missiles.
     7-96. If a missile round is delivered in a sealed container, do not open the seals of a cold-soaked missile in a
     warm shelter. The higher humidity can condense on or inside the optics of the guidance system, making the
     missile unserviceable.
     7-97. Fire control optics on a launcher need protection against condensation. Moisture that collects on the
     eyepiece can impair the effectiveness of the fire control system. When a gunner initially tries to gain a sight
     picture, moisture from breath and body heat can cause condensation on the lens. To prevent this issue, use a
     protective mask with the winterization kit installed. An anti-condensation container must be used if the sights
     are taken into a warm shelter or building.
     7-98. The rising heat of the engine may distort the view through cooled thermal imaging night sights mounted
     on vehicles. Missile system operators should position the vehicle so the missile is aiming away from the
     engine or position the engine downwind from the optical sights.

98  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

     7-99. Turning on the launch drive motors after firing the weapon in temperatures at 30°F (-1°C) or lower will
     cause ice fog. Depending on the direction of the wind, the ice fog may hinder the gunners’ ability to track
     targets after firing, attempt second-round engagements, or acquire new targets.

     7-100. Wing nuts on the battery of the missile guidance set can freeze in place and then pop off when the
     battery is loaded. Prevent seized nuts by twisting each one before loading the battery.

     7-101. Rubber eye shields on optical and night sights freeze, collect ice, and crack, leaving optics vulnerable
     to ice and snow. Snow and ice can also cause poor electrical connections on clamping surfaces on the
     traversing unit, sights, and missiles.

Artillery Missiles

     7-102. Artillery missiles, including field (surface-to-surface) and air defense (surface-to-air) types, are
     usually stored or transported in containers or sealed pods (Figure 7-4). The most significant cold complication
     is related to ice covering the electrical connecters, sockets, or container pressure vents. Storage facilities must
     avoid placing containers and pods on the ground where water can accumulate and freeze around low-mounted
     connectors, sockets, or pressure vents. Precautions are also needed to cover the connectors, sockets, or
     pressure vents if they are top mounted. Ammunition storage sites need to rely on the specific TM guidance
     for each missile type and avoid applying a generic policy to all missile handling.

   Figure 7-4. Protect shipping vents and connectors from over pressure and moisture freeze

     7-103. When transporting artillery missiles contained within sealed pods or containers, the orientation of the
     warhead during transit is less critical than the positioning of exposed connectors, sockets, and pressure vents.
     A pre-launch or detonation would more likely be attributed to impacts from displaced rocks striking the
     exposed electrical connectors or the launch squib, rather than directly involving the warhead itself. During
     transport in cold wet or snowy weather, the pressure build-up facing the direction of travel can force water
     and moisture into the sockets and vents of exposed (and even capped) components.

     7-104. Transporting a full load of artillery missiles creates a higher center of gravity for trucks. A risk
     assessment must be conducted for truck movement on ice and snow-covered roads. Commanders might lower
     the risk of accidents by reducing the load height and center of gravity.

     7-105. Soldiers must exercise caution when performing PMCS or moving heavy artillery missiles while
     wearing bulky ECWCS. Gloves are necessary for such operations but can significantly impair finger
     sensitivity and manipulation, complicating tasks like trying to set tie-downs or connecting electronic cables.
     Bulky clothing can also interfere with the clarity of hand and arm signals.

FIRE CONTROL EQUIPMENT

     7-106. Sighting and fire control equipment operate effectively at below freezing temperatures if they are
     properly winterized and certain adjustments are made. Maintaining fire control equipment in cold weather
     can pose challenges, particularly when repair shop facilities are scarce.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  99


---

Chapter 7

PRECAUTIONS

     7-107. Prepare equipment to operate at the lowest temperatures expected. Thoroughly inspect and winterize
     all equipment before the onset of cold weather.

     7-108. Lubricate fire control materiel sparingly as directed by the LO. In cold weather, a thin film of oil is
     more effective for lubricating fire control mechanisms than a heavy application. It also provides adequate
     corrosion protection. When the LO for fire control materiel specifies grease, lubricate sparingly with grease,
     instrument and aircraft (GIA), MIL-PRF-23827 (Grease, Aircraft and Instrument, Gear and Actuator Screw
     [NATO, G-354]).

     7-109. Do not suddenly transfer sighting and fire control materiel between cold and warm temperatures. This
     can cause condensation that can cloud optics and rust internal parts. Use anti-condensation containers as
     prescribed in Chapter 2.

     7-110. Avoid creating severe bends in interconnecting cables. All electrical cables should be removed
     periodically from under accumulated snow. This eliminates locating and digging out cables when preparing
     to shift the emplacement. Use a cable reel to take up cable when shifting positions and take care not to allow
     kinks to form. Use stake markers to define the cable paths; this helps locate interconnecting cables for repairs
     and prevents heavy equipment from running over them.

FOGGING OF EYEPIECES

                                                 CAUTION

                    Never pour alcohol directly on the lens surfaces, as excess alcohol will
                    damage the lens sealing compound.

     7-111. Avoid breathing on the eyepieces when using optical instruments in cold weather. The moisture in
     warm breath condenses on the lenses and turns to frost. This frost will fog the eyepieces and make it
     impossible to see through them.

     7-112. There is no effective antifogging solution available for eyepieces of optical instruments in low
     temperatures. Some solutions can prevent fogging, but they often leave streaks on the lens and make
     observation difficult.

     7-113. The cold weather mask is the preferred method of keeping breath away from eyepieces. However, any
     type of face mask is useful if it directs the breath away from lenses or absorbs moisture from the breath.

     7-114. A serviceable face mask can be constructed from various materials, including cloth, a woolen scarf,
     or gauze tied across the face just below the eyes. A mask made from any of these materials not only protects
     the operator’s face from the wind but also deflects the breath from the lens. Change the mask periodically to
     prevent the face from freezing.

     7-115. When using a range finder, draping a blanket over the operator and part of the tube effectively
     increases the observation time from 2-3 minutes to around 20 minutes before the eyepiece fogs up. Clean
     optical surfaces with tissue lens paper moistened with a few drops of optical lens liquid cleaning compound.

             Note. Ethyl alcohol can be used as a substitute for lens cleaning compound. If neither lens cleaning
             compound nor alcohol is available, use dry lens paper. To create a swab, wrap lens paper around
             the end of a small stick or sliver of wood. Dip the swab in optical lens liquid cleaning compound,
             shake off the excess, and clean lens. Wipe away any excess compound with lens paper, rubbing
             from the center outward in a spiral pattern.

100  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                        Large Crew-Served Weapon Systems, Munitions, and Fire Control Equipment

PURGING

     7-116. Most sighting and fire control instruments are filled with dry nitrogen to prevent accumulation of
     moisture inside the instruments. Mechanics purge and charge sighting and fire control equipment according
     to the applicable TMs or when condensation is evident in the instrument.

     7-117. The gunner’s primary sight on the M1 series tank features a defroster that does not automatically shut
     off after clearing the sight’s day window. Excessive heat generated can crack the window.

POWERED SYSTEMS

     7-118. While some fire control systems use unpowered devices, most newer systems require a power source,
     either from batteries or external sources. Batteries begin to lose their effectiveness as temperatures drop. At
     lower temperatures, operators may be required to discontinue battery operation and connect the power cable
     and adapter to a vehicle auxiliary power receptacle.

     7-119. Dry batteries designed for use in temperate zones are likely to lose considerable electrical capacity
     due to decreased chemical activity at lower temperatures. These batteries are capable of operating equipment
     effectively at low temperatures if their internal temperature is kept high enough to permit normal chemical
     activity. When armed warmed to approximately 70°F (21°C), these batteries can retain sufficient heat for a
     considerable duration before replacement is necessary. The period of use depends on the rate that heat is
     dissipated from the battery. Battery life can be extended by insulating them from cold conducting surfaces
     with nonconductive materials.

     7-120. There are several methods Soldiers can use to reduce heat loss when carrying replacement batteries.
     After warming, place the batteries in bags lined with kapok or spun-glass fiber materials, wrap them in
     woolen clothing, or carry them close to the body. Under certain conditions, it can help to carry the batteries
     separately from the equipment by using a connecting cord and plug. This arrangement may require certain
     modifications to the equipment to allow for the installation of the connecting cord and plug. Usually, these
     modifications are minor and readily accomplished by the unit.

     7-121. If replacement batteries can be carried in a vehicle, a well-insulated box that has small heater elements
     powered from the vehicle battery will ensure maximum usable life of the batteries without heat loss.

PROTECTION OF TUBE EXTENSIONS AND EYEPIECES

     7-122. Snow can collect in uncovered eyepieces and tube sunshades or extensions, rendering instruments
     inoperable until the snow is removed. To effectively clear the snow, use a small, stiff brush or small rubber
     bulb equipped with a nozzle. Do not try to blow the snow out of these parts or wipe it out with gloves or bare
     hands, as some snow particles may melt and freeze on the lenses, resulting in further complications. A
     temporary method of keeping snow out of eyepieces and tube extensions is to put loose wads of tissue lens
     paper in them when the instrument is not in use. These wads can be removed easily. Take care to prevent the
     tissues from becoming wet and freezing the tube.

LEVEL VIALS

     7-123. In temperatures below -40°F (-40°C), the level vials on sighting and fire control equipment can
     experience sluggish movement, elongated bubbles, and bubble separation. Sluggish bubble movement and
     elongated bubbles do not affect the precision of the device; however, bubble separation does affect accuracy.
     When bubble separation occurs, briskly rub the top surface of the level vial and the bubbles will merge into
     a single bubble.

COMPASSES, BINOCULARS, AND OTHER OPTICAL DEVICES

     7-124. The liquid in the lensatic compass thickens in the cold. The heavier liquid slows the action of the
     compass and may make it inaccurate. Carry this type of compass near the body in the inner clothing to keep
     the liquid warm and thin. The dry-type compasses are not affected by cold weather.

     7-125. Cold weather does not affect binoculars and other liquid-free optical instruments. However,
     condensation does form when these instruments are taken from cold air into warm air. To prevent this issue,
     leave instruments outside or use anti-condensation containers.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  101


---

Chapter 7

     7-126. Cover equipment like telescopes when they are not in use. If a cover cannot be made to fit the whole
     instrument, use bag-type cloth covers to go over the eyepieces and tube extensions. Cloth covers are
     preferable to airtight covers (such as the leather covers provided for some instruments). Cloth covers allow
     air circulation in contact with the lens, preventing condensation when the instrument is exposed to lower
     temperatures. Cloth bag covers can be made with a spring, elastic, or drawstring at the opening to hold them
     in place and allow for quick and easy removal.

     7-127. When temperatures drop, the pressure on the hydraulic accumulator of the independent thermal viewer
     must be lowered, or its pump will work too hard and wear out quickly. The image transfer assembly also
     needs attention when the seasons change to preclude excessive purging and desiccant changes.

102  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                 Chapter 8

          Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

    Electronic devices such as communications equipment, computing devices, optics, and robotics
    are highly susceptible to cold temperatures. This chapter discusses cold-induced issues related
    to communications equipment, computers, power generation, and electronic devices and
    mitigation techniques to prevent equipment damage and ensure proper operation.

COLD WEATHER THREATS

     8-1. The functionality of electronics in cold weather environments is influenced by more than just
     temperature; certain climatic conditions can affect this equipment as well. In addition to the steps provided
     in the TM, equipment operators must be able to recognize each of these effects to maximize equipment
     performance and accurately report functional problems. Maintainers and operators should refer to the item-
     specific TM that is the primary source for maintenance procedures.

     8-2. Cold weather regions exhibit different weather characteristics compared to mild or temperate weather
     regions. Cold can alter the performance of electromagnetic energy and battery power consumption.
     Communications equipment, including antennas, can be adversely affected by any of the following:

          • Rapid temperature changes.
          • Obstructed visibility.
          • Stabilizing or securing equipment to the ground to erect systems or masts.
          • Electrical current grounding problems.
          • Static (excess energy ionization) buildup.
          • Radio noise (radio frequency static emissions) interference.
          • Snow or ice (weight) accumulation on antennas and wires.
          • Magnetic storms (frequency blackouts).
          • Accuracy reduction of global positioning devices.
          • Lower temperature limits of materials.

COLD-DRY AIR

     8-3. Cold-soaked equipment is especially difficult to assemble in cold-dry air. The use of bulky gloves or
     mittens by operators and maintenance personnel further complicates the process. Heightened attention to
     detail, patience, and special preparation to prevent damage is imperative. This includes working with tight
     connections, electrical contacts, inflexible cables, power cords, grounding, control knobs, rubber covers,
     binding posts, and various other moving parts. When temperatures reach -40°F (-40°C), the storage battery
     used in radio sets becomes inoperative, leading to the loss of codes and requires reloading after warming.

     8-4. The combination of dry air, wind, and cold can cause considerable buildup of static electricity on non-
     conducting surfaces. This buildup can be a hazard to operators and technicians, as well as to equipment.

     8-5. Temperature inversions that occur on the coldest days in cold regions significantly degrade the strength
     of frequency modulation communications signals. A temperature inversion occurs when, as altitude
     increases, the temperature drops to a certain point, where it levels off or even reverses before resuming its
     downward trend. These abnormal temperature zones can obstruct, scatter, or reflect radio transmissions. This
     must be considered when planning troop placements and determining the locations of mission command
     centers.

FROZEN SURFACES

     8-6. Frozen surfaces act as heat sinks that can rapidly cool equipment. Communications equipment should
     be kept off the ground and frozen surfaces. Adequate grounding is important for many pieces of
     communications equipment but can be very difficult to achieve on frozen surfaces.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  103


---

Chapter 8

             Note. Standard grounding systems will require considerable effort to construct, and in some cases,
             it can be impossible. One of the most effective methods for penetrating frozen ground is to use
             shaped charges in combination with water saturated salt or soil backfill poured over the grounding
             device.

     8-7. Antenna systems may be difficult to anchor in frozen ground, and ropes can freeze in cold environments.
     Mountain pitons are good alternative anchors for guy ropes in severe cold. As weather warms and the ground
     softens these alternative anchors need to be monitored and replaced with standard stakes.

     8-8. The extraction of grounding devices is likely possible only following seasonal thawing. Use existing
     grounding options like pipes, established grounds, and buried steel if possible. Ensure existing grounds are
     not conduits for gas or flammable liquids before use.

SNOW AND ICE

     8-9. Snow and ice can get into any unprotected openings in electronic equipment; use any equipment covers
     provided.

     8-10. As snow and ice accumulated on wires begins to thaw, the water will run down and work its way into
     splices or connections. Install drip loops as pictured in Figure 8-1 to prevent the risk of electrical shorts
     caused by dripping water. A drip loop consists of a downward curve in the wire followed by an upward curve,
     which allows water to collect and drip before it reaches the connections. A drip loop provides the added
     advantage of providing slack in the line to accommodate wire shrinkage as the wire cools. Overhead
     connections (especially power connections) are required to accommodate cold contraction of wires.

                                                     Figure 8-1. Drip loop

     8-11. Other common issues regarding snow and ice include—
          • Signal attenuation (absorption of energy reducing signal strength) due to ice on antennas.
          • The loss of small items dropped in the snow. Units should conduct more frequent inventories of
                radio sets and antenna kits.

EFFECTS OF COLD ON ELECTRICAL AND ELECTRONIC DEVICES

     8-12. Severe cold can affect the materials used to manufacture most communications and information
     systems equipment and make it unsafe or unsuitable for use. Most electrical and electronic equipment is
     resistant to the effects of cold temperatures down to about -4°F (-20°C), provided it stays dry. However, there
     are regions of the world where temperatures can reach -58°F (-50°C) for days at a time or fluctuate by 35°F
     (20°C) on a single day. While this TM primarily discusses only the effects of low temperatures on equipment,
     other cold weather conditions like low humidity, wind, and snow and ice formation that can impact electrical
     equipment. It is important to follow the equipment TM or manufacturers’ instructions.

104  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                       Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

BATTERY FAILURE

     8-13. Battery failure is a reoccurring issue for electrical and electronic items operated in the cold.
     Rechargeable batteries, specifically nickel-cadmium and lithium batteries, do not last as long in temperatures
     below 32°F (0°C). The charge rate for cold-soaked batteries is also slower. Military non-rechargeable
     batteries (usually lithium type) are developed to operate in temperatures of -22°F (-30°C) to 131°F (55°C)
     with a storage temperature range of -40°F (-40°C) to 158°F (70°C). It is important to prevent lithium batteries
     from becoming fully depleted, which could lead to a negative charge and thermal runaway.

INTERNAL CIRCUITS

     8-14. As temperatures decrease, changes can occur in the resistance and capacitance of electrical and
     electronic components resulting in timing changes on integrated circuits, waveform changes, and changes to
     other electrical properties. This can affect the behavior of sensitive electronic equipment.

     8-15. There are precautions that can be taken to mitigate condensation damage to electronic equipment. Cold-
     soaked electronic equipment that is not ruggedized or sealed should be heated or blown out with hot air. This
     assures the evaporation of internal condensation and moisture before power is applied.

DUST

     8-16. If an item is covered in dust, the dust can retain condensation crystals even after the item is warmed.
     To mitigate dust, an information technology specialist should conduct regular cleaning (such as bi-monthly)
     by blowing out the dust. Placing a towel lightly misted with lubricating oil over the cooling intake vents can
     help to reduce dust from being sucked into electronic equipment (especially COTS computing equipment).

POWER-UP FRACTURING

     8-17. Most electronic equipment produces its own heat, typically reaching temperatures between 70°F (21°C)
     and 80°F (27°C). Be mindful of cold-soaked equipment that has been powered off and cooled to sub-freezing
     temperatures. If this equipment is not preheated, the sudden temperature change during power-up can lead to
     cracks in internal components or pop and shatter crystals, diodes, and light emitting diodes. This is a more
     serious risk for COTS equipment, where ambient temperature should be above freezing. Maintainers and
     suppliers should be ready for the increased workload due to power-up heat shock.

CONTRACTING AND WARPING

     8-18. Severe cold temperatures can cause various components of electronic equipment to contract, just as
     warm temperatures cause expansion. This contraction can lead to issues including warping of keyboard keys
     and failure of touch pads. Contraction is a major cause of equipment failure for devices using liquid crystal
     technology.

TRANSFORMERS

     8-19. Frost accumulated on non-ruggedized electrical equipment, circuit boards, or electronic computing
     equipment must be removed before connecting the equipment to power, even if the device is turned off.
     When plugged in, these devices will immediately enter standby mode in preparation for full power up.
     Operators should always assume that power supply transformers are on.

GROUNDING

     8-20. Properly grounding generators and communications equipment is a critical task that becomes more
     difficult in frozen ground conditions. Cold substantially increases soil resistance and impedes earth
     grounding. It is essential to drive ground rods or install ground plates below the frost line whenever possible
     to ensure effective grounding. In situations where there are no accessible buried objects for grounding, like
     underground pipes or building frameworks, drive multiple ground rods as deeply as possible. The most
     effective tactical grounding system for use in frozen ground is the surface wire ground. This system consists
     of pins connected with cable to form a grounding grid. Ensure that the pins are spaced at least two rod lengths
     apart. Alternatively, a trench can be dug to insert a horizontal rod or conductor. The trench should be filled
     with salt water, which will improve conductivity when frozen. Typically, the maximum grounding resistance

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  105


---

Chapter 8

     that can be achieved with a six-foot grounding rod or the surface wire ground system is about 3,000 ohms.
     For guidance, refer to Communications-Electronics Command (CECOM) Technical Report 98-6.

SPECIALLY DESIGNED GROUNDING EQUIPMENT

     8-21. Sealed, waterproof, or ruggedized electronic modules are usually not susceptible to the same general
     freezing condensation problems of exposed components. Likewise, electronic equipment that is kept and
     used in heated shelters typically does not require special precautions. Refer to the item-specific TM for
     exceptions.

OFFICE COMPUTERS

     8-22. Most computers are not designed to operate at extremely low temperatures and may become unreliable
     or unavailable for use (0°F [-18°C] for larger workstations, -30°F [-34°C] for small handheld devices). Liquid
     crystal displays (LCDs) are particularly susceptible to cold weather. Do not force frozen knobs, dials, or
     switches. Electronic computer equipment used in a field environment such as computers, printers, peripheral
     devices, and radios that have blower vents or exposed circuit boards are at risk of condensation and freezing
     on both the circuit boards and within the accumulated dust.

INFORMATION TO DECISION DISPLAY EQUIPMENT

                                                 CAUTION

                    When removing a monitor, display, or TV from cold storage, do NOT
                    plug it in immediately. Allow the device to acclimate to the ambient
                    temperature of the room for 20-45 minutes. Keep the item secured in an
                    anti-condensation container while the item stabilizes to room
                    temperature.

     8-23. Maintainers and operational staff must be aware of the various display technologies used throughout
     command, including plasma display panels (PDP), LCD screens, light emitting diode (LED) displays, and
     projectors. Cold temperatures can significantly impact the operating capabilities for these types of equipment.
     Generally, ruggedized military versions of display or projection equipment can handle temperatures down to
     -4°F (-20C) without requiring built-in heating mechanisms. However, most COTS displays and projectors
     are not rated for use below 32°F (0°C).

     8-24. The main vulnerability of displays and projectors in cold weather is due to the strict design tolerances
     of their construction and the reaction of supporting materials in the display screen or electrical circuitry. Cold
     temperatures can cause the expansion and contraction of the materials used in the screen and chassis,
     potentially pushing them outside of their designed limits. Additionally, humidity and condensation pose
     significant threats to both projectors and displays. Store the equipment in airtight cases to mitigate the risk
     of condensation buildup. Maintenance supervisors should establish defined policies within the standard
     operating procedure (SOP) for the proper operation and storage of display and projector devices.

     8-25. When preparing to utilize any PDP, LCD, or LED display that was stored at temperatures below 40°F
     (4°C), it is essential to avoid rapid reheating. Avoid power-up electrical heat surges by gradually pre-warming
     the item. Wrapping the item in a clingy, airtight cellophane can serve as an anti-condensation container until
     the device adjusts to the warmer ambient temperature. These procedures will help preserve the crystalline
     character of the display, mitigate condensation on electronic components, and prevent water streaks from
     forming on the interior surface of the screen.

PLASMA DISPLAY PANNELS AND SCREENS

     8-26. The PDP consists of a matrix made up of very small fluorescent tubes that contain red, green, and blue
     phosphors. The active gases used in plasma screens are xenon and neon, both of which exhibit resistance to
     freezing in extremely low temperatures. Cold weather freeze damage is usually related more to the quality of
     the electronics manufacturing or condensation on the component materials. Unless specifically stated
     otherwise in the manufacturer’s instructions or the TM—

106  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                       Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

          • PDP operating temperature range is between 50°F (10°C) to 104°F (40°C).
          • PDP storage temperature range is between -4°F (-20C) to 113°F (45°C).
          • COTS PDPs are sensitive to altitudes above 9,000 feet (3,000 meters).

Liquid Crystal Based Technology

                                                 CAUTION

                    Never touch or apply pressure to a cold LCD screen.

     8-27. Supply personnel must plan to store liquid crystal-based equipment within the lowest common
     temperature range. Additionally, operators should dismount and store displays and monitors within the
     temperature range approved by unit policy. Unless specified otherwise by the manufacturer’s instructions or
     the TM, the safest temperatures for LCD devices generally fall within the following ranges:

          • Ruggedized LCD operating and storage temperatures at -4°F (-20C) to 140°F (60°C).
          • Non-ruggedized LCD operating temperatures at 40°F (4°C) to 90°F (32°C).
          • Non-ruggedized LCD storage temperature at 40°F (4°C) to 100°F (38°C).

             Note. The temperatures listed above for LCDs are only intended for planning purposes. Actual
             performance characteristics of a specific item will vary significantly.

     8-28. Manufacturer limitations on cold storage of large screen LCD monitors (television sized) varies greatly.
     Some are rated to handle storage temperatures as low as -15°F (-26°C) to -20°F (-28°C), while others can
     hardly be stored at temperatures below 40°F (4°C).

     8-29. As the liquid crystal cools to temperatures below 50°F (10°C), the image refresh rate becomes sluggish
     to the point where its display quality is active but blurred. Image blurring occurs for all LCDs around 40°F
     (4°C) and below depending on the exact chemical composition of the liquid crystal. The effects of low
     temperatures are usually reversible if the crystal does not freeze solid. However, at temperatures around -67°F
     (-55°C), the liquid crystal can freeze solid, breaking the crystalline substructure. Once frozen for a few days,
     damage becomes irreversible. (Do not confuse LCD freezing with water freezing.)

     8-30. LCD screens are backlit by either a florescent tube (old technology) or an LED. When powered on in
     cold weather conditions (below 50°F[10°C]), an LCD screen may initially be faint and hard to read.
     Additionally, the refresh rate for motion pictures may be reduced and result in fragmented images. However,
     if the screen remains powered on, the display should improve in a short time due to self-warming. If the
     broken image or sluggish performance persists after the screen has reached about 32°F (0°C) to 50°F (10°C),
     then the item needs to be turned in per unit policy.

     8-31. Operators must use anti-condensation containers to store and transfer LCD items from a cold vehicle
     into a heated shelter. For COTS items, wrap displays in a towel, felt, or fleece blanket to protect screens from
     scratching when not in use. Store any LCD item in an airtight anti-condensation container, cellophane wrap,
     or a self-sealing plastic bag.

     8-32. When operators return an LCD monitor to a cold environment vehicle or platform after off-duty storage,
     it should be removed from the container, connected, and powered up as soon as possible to avoid the effects
     of low temperature. Make sure the vehicle’s motor is running before connecting an LCD item to a vehicle.

Light Emitting Diode

     8-33. LCD-LED screens use liquid crystals with an LED transistor material that glows for screen
     backlighting. The light emitting diode itself will not freeze since it is already solid. The threat occurs when
     power is applied to the diode. The sudden rapid heat expansion can fracture (pop) a diode. When possible,
     pre-warm electronics before turning them on. The key issue is the durability of every component that makes
     up the LED. Otherwise, the care of LED display items is the same as any item made with liquid crystal
     technology.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  107


---

Chapter 8

Digital Light Processing Projectors

     8-34. Digital light processing projectors consist of six major components, including a light source, optics,
     color filters, a digital micromirror device, electronics, and a projection lens. These projectors function at
     temperatures from -40°F (-40°C) to 158°F (70°C).

VISIBILITY AND ELECTROMAGNETIC CONDITIONS

     8-35. Polar regions of the Earth where cold weather occurs are prone to two issues that affect electronic
     equipment: reduced visibility and electromagnetic disturbances. Prolonged hours of darkness affect
     communications equipment operation and maintenance in several ways:

          • Inventory, assembly, and disassembly in the dark are challenging and time consuming, especially
                when wearing required gloves or mittens.

          • High-frequency wave propagation deteriorates significantly as darkness falls due to changes
                occurring in the ionosphere.

          • Effective high frequency communications between stations that are 100 kilometers or more apart
                may be limited to six hours.

          • Lower frequency assignments (that need longer antennas) are also required.

     8-36. The Aurora Borealis can cause static noise, suppress signals, and cause unusual wave propagation in
     radio communications. While electromagnetic interference does not damage equipment, planners must
     anticipate this degradation in choosing unit locations. Fading and severe static can cause speech secure
     devices to lose signal, requiring numerous retransmissions of long messages.

     8-37. Units reportedly use Aurora activity to enhance the range of high-frequency radio communications by
     reflecting directional signals off the light fields. However, this innovative use of conditions is the exception.
     The greater magnetic declination angles encountered when moving further north considerably affect radio
     communications positioning and orientation of directional antennas. These problems increase the need for
     strict supervision.

COMMUNICATIONS AND ELECTRONIC SYSTEMS

     8-38. Cold weather operations have always been concerned about communications and power generation.
     Now, with the advent of microcircuits, operators must be concerned about low-power components within
     these high-power systems. A few quick points:

          • Carry small batteries inside your clothes to keep them warm. Reactivate cold soaked batteries by
                warming them under your clothes.

          • If a radio set must be set up outside, put it in a sheltered place. A wind block (for example a lean-
                to) helps protect the equipment from direct exposure to cold air.

          • Use poles or tree limbs to raise cables above the ground to keep them from freezing to the ground.
                If you can't get cables off the ground, keep them out from under the snow. Pull them free after every
                snowfall.

          • Check antenna systems often and remove snow, ice, or slush that might diminish your signal or
                create a falling ice hazard.

          • Put frost shields over microphones. If you don't have a shield, or your handset doesn't have a place
                to fit one, a piece of plastic (like a battery bag) will work.

          • Remove all snow, ice, water, and dirt from cable connectors before connecting to prevent breaking
                them or having a poor connection.

          • Rubber and rubber compounds become stiff and brittle as temperatures drop. In cold weather, cables
                and wire should be flexed slowly and carefully to keep them from cracking and breaking.

          • When applying lubricant, make sure not to over-lubricate. Lubricants can become stiff in cold
                weather, hindering their effectiveness. When using lubricant in the cold, check to ensure it hasn’t
                gotten stiff and apply frequently. Use lighter lubricants if available.

          • Plugs, jacks, keys, shafts, bearings, dials, and switches can malfunction due to contraction of metal
                parts in severe cold. Check them often and keep them warm and clean.

          • Make sure all motors and fans operate freely. Snow and ice buildup can shut down critical fans and
                potentially damage motors.

108  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                       Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

          • Make sure all knobs and controls move easily. Stiff controls might indicate a frozen moisture
                problem.

          • Any equipment that generates heat during operation will draw in cold air as the equipment cools.
                When heated equipment contacts extremely cold air, there is a risk that glass, plastic, and ceramic
                parts may break. Allow hot equipment adequate time to cool down before taking it out of shelter
                into the cold.

RADIO SYSTEMS

     8-39. Handle all communications/electronic equipment carefully when cold. A radio dropped on cold ground
     or thrown into the back of a vehicle can be damaged easily. Control knobs that may be sluggish or even
     frozen require careful handling; do not attempt to force them.

                                                 CAUTION

                    To prevent damage to equipment left installed in a cold-soaked vehicle,
                    allow the equipment to come up to operating temperature before turning
                    on the power.

     8-40. Even well-maintained equipment requires considerable warm-up time. For example, 15 to 20 minutes
     warm-up may be required before voice transmission is possible. Plan to use innovative solutions such as
     chemical heat pads and Styrofoam to keep equipment (especially speech secure devices) as warm as possible
     and operational. Try to keep radios and switchboards off the ground and away from exterior tent or shelter
     walls.

     8-41. Equipment can suffer damage if it is improperly positioned too close to heat sources like stoves or
     heaters. Electronic components and insulators are at risk of melting or burning, and some batteries can even
     explode if placed too near a heat source.

     8-42. Lithium batteries perform particularly well in cold weather and do not require warming at temperatures
     above -20°F (-29°C). Dry cell batteries should be kept warm until needed. Extra batteries can replace cold
     batteries in use; after warming, they can be used again. Batteries should not be installed in cold-soaked
     equipment. Plastic pins on battery connections get brittle in the cold; install them carefully.

     8-43. Each user of a portable man packed radio should carry the handset inside at least one layer of clothing
     to prevent the push-to-talk button from freezing. This requirement, combined with the operator wearing a
     parka hood, can severely impair the ability to hear incoming transmissions. This may be overcome by using
     external speakers. Use plastic coverings (such as battery packaging) and moisture shields to cover the
     mouthpieces of handsets and keep moisture from the operator’s breath from freezing them. Placing a frozen
     handset directly against lips or ears can cause physical injury.

     8-44. Spare connectors, cables, handsets, and antennas should be readily available for replacement when
     failures occur. Metals and plastic become remarkably brittle and may crack and break easily in the cold.
     When using tape on components, use friction tape rather than plastic tape, which loses its adhesiveness in the
     cold.

     8-45. Vehicular radios require careful attention. Small physical shocks can damage whip antennas when they
     are in the upright position. Allow radios to warm up for about three to four minutes on low power before
     transmitting or switching to high power settings. The vehicle for the radio being used should be cycled
     periodically according to its TM when temperatures dip below -30°F (-34°C). The vehicle, when cycled,
     should be high idled at approximately 1,200rpm (or as prescribed by the vehicle TM). Otherwise, the high
     cold weather discharge rate necessary to operate the vehicle, radio, and the heater will quickly wear out the
     vehicle batteries.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  109


---

Chapter 8

ANTENNA SYSTEMS

     8-46. Proper assembly of antennas is often overlooked, especially in cold and dark conditions. Shortcuts and
     failure to follow prescribed instructions often result in interrupted or degraded communications and damage
     to equipment. Antenna masts must be erected with enough mast sections to ensure necessary height.

     8-47. Standard stakes are often ineffective in frozen ground. Cold-weather stakes can be ordered for antenna
     masts, but they do not hold as well when the ground thaws since they are slimmer than standard stakes. Heavy
     12-inch steel tent pins, short segments of rebar (reinforcing steel rods), and tree and rock tie offs are also
     acceptable options for anchoring antennas. Another method for erecting a radio antenna is to carefully guide
     the mast alongside a tree and secure it to the trunk using one of the attached guy lines. Be careful not to
     damage any of the elements, cables, or mast sections while erecting the antenna.

     8-48. Proper inventory of unused hardware is essential since parts are easily lost in snow and darkness. Be
     careful not to let moisture accumulate due to condensation or precipitation and ground out the antenna.

             Note. Prior to assembly, expose the antenna components to the cold and inspect them for ice or
             ice crystal buildup due to condensation and freezing. This prevents problems later with
             disassembly.

     8-49. Keep the mast sections clean and free of foreign matter. Lubricate the male and female ends of each
     mast section only with silicone lubricant. After applying the silicone and joining the sections, back off the
     joints by approximately one turn as a precaution against sticking.

     8-50. The RG213 coaxial cable (rated to work at 167°F to -40°F [75°C to -40°C]) is recommended over RG-8
     cable; the latter becomes brittle and cracks at temperatures below -20°F (-29°C). Operators should have spare
     cable connectors and adaptors on hand since damage and loss are common. Loop and tape coaxial antenna
     cable near the top of the mast to ease pressure on the connector. Tape the cable at intervals along the mast to
     prevent the whipping action of the wind from causing damage. Tap the mast periodically to shake free snow
     and frost accumulation, which can degrade transmission signal strength. Make guy lines in a manner to
     prevent tripping on the antenna guys and disabling the antenna. Use cold-weather tape when assembling the
     antenna; normal tape becomes less adhesive in sub-freezing temperatures.

     8-51. Keep ceramic bowls dry, as water may collect in them during warm weather. When temperatures drop,
     the accumulated water can freeze, causing the more brittle glass to crack. Applying silicone where the two
     bowl halves join helps in sealing against further cold damage.

     8-52. High-frequency and long-wire antennas can approach 75 meters in length depending on the frequencies
     used. Attention to measurement is critical. The same applies to frequency modulation antennas.

     8-53. Orienting an azimuth is important and often difficult because of the requirement to compute large
     declination angles in northern latitudes. Directional antennas are often required to compensate for other
     conditions causing range degradation.

WIRE AND CABLING SYSTEMS

     8-54. It is significantly easier to lay wire over long distances in deep snow with over-snow vehicles. However,
     these vehicles can also pose a serious threat to the wire systems. Their skis and tracks can damage surface-
     laid wire and cable, often dragging away large sections and cutting critical circuits. Standard wire-laying
     techniques, ties, and tagging apply in cold regions as well as in temperate zones. Aerial laying is
     recommended when tactically feasible. Burying wires is desirable, but it is often difficult or impossible in
     frozen ground and snow. Retrieving wire in cold regions is tedious and usually results in excessive salvage
     work due to ice and traffic damage. Stringing wire overhead is preferred because the wire will not freeze to
     the ground.

     8-55. Wire insulation is often brittle, and impedance increases in snow or damp conditions. Splices performed
     in the cold are often done improperly, and fewer can be allowed for continued use of serviced wire. A slack
     factor of 30% is recommended for wire-laying teams over the 20% specified in the TM. This allows for cold
     weather shrinkage (unless the wire is already cold-soaked).

110  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                       Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

     8-56. Reeling wire and cable should be done carefully; reeled wire freezes into a coiled shape and should be
     warmed up before unreeling. The use of bigger coils also reduces the chances of pinches or breaks.

POWER GENERATION OPERATIONS

     8-57. Units must inspect their mobile electric power (MEP) generator sets thoroughly and prepare them for
     cold weather operations according to the TMs and local procedures. Follow generator set lubricating orders
     that specify the appropriate lubricants to use in cold weather. Winterization kits for the 5-kilowatt to 60-
     kilowatt tactical quiet generators (TQG) are available for temperatures of -25°F (-32°C) down to -50°F
     (-46°C). They can be requisitioned as individual kits by national stock number and kilowatt size. Read the
     generator set’s TM section on cold weather procedures outlined in “Operation under Unusual Conditions.”
     Perform the tasks below to ensure satisfactory operation during cold weather:

          • Generator sets can be stored at temperatures down to -60°F (-51°C).
          • Winterize the generator set by using the correct cold-weather lubricants. Read the generator set’s

                LO. Temperate weather lubricants are the prominent cause for cold weather generator failure.
          • An optional winterization kit is available for installation in cold weather climates. Field-level

                maintainers with MOS 91D (Power Generation Equipment Repairer) are authorized to install the
                kits.
          • Keep the batteries clean, fully charged, and protected from freezing. If possible, store the batteries
                in a warmer environment and install them only when the equipment is ready for use.
          • Use the correct fuel for the anticipated temperature range. Ensure the fuel is clean and free of water
                contamination.
          • Keep the generator set and surrounding area free of ice and snow.
          • Soldiers should allow generator sets to reach their normal operating temperature before applying a
                full load to reduce the possibility of engine damage.
          • Follow the correct shut down procedures detailed in the generator set’s TM.
          • Keep fuel containers closed to prevent snow and moisture from entering. Water in the fuel can freeze
                the fuel lines. Military generator sets have filters that remove impurities and some water, but too
                much water decreases their effectiveness.
          • Keep the fuel tank full to prevent moisture intrusion, condensation, and water accumulation.
          • Inspect, drain, and clean fuel filter and water separators daily and at shutdown to prevent icing.
          • Grounding generators sets properly is an important safety precaution regardless of the ambient
                temperature. When the ground is frozen and the standard ground rod assembly (see basic issue items
                in the TM) cannot penetrate, Soldiers can use the surface wire grounding kit; national stock number
                5820-01-263-1760.
          • The surface wire grounding kit contains a sledgehammer, two 10-foot steel connector cables with
                75 ampere connecting clips, 15 10" steel grounding pegs, and 75-feet of steel grounding cable.
                Follow the kit’s instructions and refer to CECOM TR 98-6.
          • Units can bury a ground plate in warmer weather and mark its location for future use. Use a plate at
                least 2 feet square of 1/4-inch steel or 1/16” thick nonferrous metal (except aluminum), buried deep
                enough to reach moist soil. When needed, connect the plate to the generator set’s ground terminal
                lug with #6 AWG wire.

             Note: Remember, any ground electrode (rod, peg or plate) should penetrate deep enough to contact
             moist soil below the frost line. See CECOM TR 98-6 for grounding procedures in Arctic areas.
             Units can also use an existing underground metal pipe for a satisfactory earth ground. Though it
             too must reach below the frost line.

TACTICAL ELECTRICAL PRODUCTION AND DISTRIBUTION

     8-58. Low temperatures can affect the materials used in electrical equipment. Generator sets can be started
     and will operate at temperatures down to -25°F (-32°C) without requiring a winterization kit. However,
     starting diesel engines becomes challenging at these temperatures. In general—

          • Severe cold weather can impair the function of circuit breakers in electrical equipment.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  111


---

Chapter 8

          • Metals and plastics can lose ductility and become brittle to varying degrees in very low
                temperatures. These effects can compromise their ability to withstand transportation shock or rough
                handling. Elastomer (rubber or flexible plastic) components such as cabling and seals become much
                less flexible and brittle at low temperatures.

          • Loss of flexibility in cold can lead to inadequate sealing of electrical enclosures.
          • Wire and power cable insulation can become brittle as temperatures decrease. Cable damage can

                then occur when installing them at low temperatures. If practical, store cables at 50°F or above for
                1 day before installing them in cold weather.

     8-59. Other conditions often prevalent during cold weather operations can affect equipment, such as low
     humidity, wind, and snow and ice formation. MEPs generator sets will operate at elevations up to 4000 feet
     above sea level without special adjustment or reduction in capacity. At elevations above 4000 feet, the
     kilowatt rating drops by 3.5% for each additional 1000-foot increment. Information about operating generator
     sets at higher altitudes is printed on the data plate. Refer to the TM for information on a given generator set
     model.

     8-60. Operators and maintainers must perform cold weather maintenance on all MEP generators and power
     distribution illumination system electrical (PDISE) equipment. Refer to the generator set TM for specific
     procedures for cold weather operation and follow these general guidelines:

          • Install winterization kits before deployment to cold climate areas.
