          • The majority of DOD standard generator sets use liquid-cooled diesel engines. Operators must use

                an antifreeze solution to protect generator sets at the lowest temperature expected.
          • Ensure the engine’s starting batteries are fully charged to prevent freezing.
          • In certain conditions, ice fog can form due to engine exhaust when temperature is at or below -25°F

                (-32°C). To eliminate or reduce ice fog, the operator can attach an extension tube to the generator
                set’s exhaust pipe to direct the exhaust into the snow.
          • For safety, Soldiers must not touch the cold metal parts and enclosures with bare hands to prevent a
                cold injury.

MOBILE ELECTRIC POWER SYSTEMS

     8-61. Each family of generators has distinct characteristics and features that operators and maintainers need
     to understand to operate, troubleshoot, and maintain them effectively. MEP generators are the DOD standard
     family of power generator sets. They are designed to operate in ambient temperatures as low as -25°F (-32°C)
     without requiring specialized winterization equipment. The Army uses several DOD standard families of
     MEP generators along with their associated power distribution equipment:

          • Military tactical generator (MTG).
          • TQG.
          • advanced medium mobile power sources (AMMPS).
          • power unit/power plant is a combination of a trailer-mounted TQG or AMMPS.
          • Deployable Power Generation and Distribution equipment.
          • PDISE.

Military Tactical Generator

     8-62. The 2-kilowatt MTG sets (models MEP-501A and MEP-531A) are self-contained, skid mounted,
     portable units. They are equipped with the controls, instruments, and accessories necessary for operation as
     single units. The generator set consists of a diesel engine, direct-drive alternating current (AC) alternator,
     speed governing system, fuel system, 24V direct current auxiliary cold weather starting system, and generator
     control system. The generator sets are portable and require a four-person lift. They can be stacked three high
     while in storage.

     8-63. The 2-kilowatt MTG has a built-in cold weather starting system for temperatures at 23°F (-5°C) down
     to -50°F (-45°C). These generators do not have protective enclosures for main components.

     8-64. MTGs provide electric power to—
          • Modern burner unit of the mobile kitchen trailer.
          • RQ-7A tactical unmanned aerial vehicle.

112  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                       Electrical Equipment and Electronic Devices

          • Enhanced position location reporting system.
          • Assault hose system.
          • Woodworking set.
          • High mobility artillery rocket system.
          • Prophet signal intelligence system.
          • Tactical messaging system.
          • Maintenance tent lights and battery charging system.
          • Lights, laptops, and miscellaneous support equipment.

Tactical Quiet Generators

     8-65. The 3-kilowatt TQG sets (models MEP-831A and MEP-832A) are self-contained, skid mounted,
     portable units. They are equipped with controls, instruments, and accessories necessary for operation. The
     generator sets consist of a diesel engine, permanent magnet AC generator, control box assembly, output/load
     panel, primary and auxiliary fuel systems, enclosure cooling and ventilation system, engine exhaust
     assembly, and a 24V direct current battery. The generator sets are designed to be used with any equipment
     requiring a small source of AC power and operate in a “Hot and Basic” climatic condition range of -25°F
     (-32°C) to 120°F (49°C). The generator sets are portable and require a six-person lift.

     8-66. These are two configurations of the 3-kilowatt TQG:
          • 120V and 120V/240V AC 60 hertz.
          • 120V and 120V/240V AC 400 hertz.

     8-67. The 3-kilowatt TQG is equipped with cold weather engine starting aids:
          • Integral air intake heaters are mounted in the intake manifold. These heaters raise the air temperature
                in the combustion chamber to assist with ignition when the air temperature drops below 21°F (-6°C)
                to -25°F (-32°C).
          • An optional winterization kit is available and designed to heat the coolant and engine block when
                temperatures ranges between -25°F (-32°C) to -50°F (-46°C).
          • Ether is used to help start the engine because of its low (320 °F [160 °C]) auto ignition temperature.

Advanced Medium Mobile Power Sources

     8-68. AMMPS are a standard family of reliable, efficient, light weight, electronically controlled, diesel-
     fueled power systems to support tactical units in the battlefield. They will eventually replace all comparably
     sized TQGs. AMMPS features include the following:

          • Available in 5-kilowatt, 10-kilowatt, 15-kilowatt, 30-kilowatt, and 60-kilowatt.
          • Skid and trailer mounted.
          • Consume less fuel, require fewer maintenance hours, have significantly better failure rates, and

                allow for multiple sets paralleling while providing high quality power in extreme operational and
                environmental conditions.
          • Electronically controlled with digital Soldier interfaces.
          • Environmental Protection Agency compliant engines to reduce harmful emissions.
          • Use diesel fuel, DF-2D, DF-1D, jet fuel JP-5, and JP-8.
          • Operate in temperatures ranging from -25°F to 125°F and can withstand humidity, sand, dust, and
                rain.
          • AMMPS generators can be paralleled to increase power output or connected to create a microgrid
                without the need for external control devices.
          • AMMPS is equipped with fuel-fired standby heater that warms the coolant when temperatures range
                from -25°F (-32°C) to -50°F (-46°C). This winterization kit starts automatically based on the
                temperature and regulates the heat automatically. Its digital control system activates the “ready to
                crank” indicator when the heater has completed its cycle.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  113


---

Chapter 8

POWER DISTRIBUTION AND ILLUMINATION SYSTEMS, ELECTRICAL

     8-69. The principal power distribution system for the Army is called the PDISE. PDISE is basically a more
     rugged version of home distribution boxes/panels (with various size circuits and circuit overload protection),
     plus the connecting cables and a utility subsystem.

     8-70. This equipment is designed to operate at temperatures as low as -25°F (-32°C). To prevent moisture
     intrusion, ensure the electrical feeder system and distribution center enclosure lids are closed and latched.
     When coiling load cables or making connections to electrical loads, form large loops since the power cables
     are more difficult to bend in below freezing temperatures.

     8-71. PDISE characteristics:
          • Man-portable electrical distribution system for safe, all-weather electrical power distribution from
                generator sets to multiple users (like vehicles, shelters, field unit complexes, and tents).
          • Comes in four sizes of distribution boxes (40, 60, 100, and 200 Amps), connecting cables, and
                assemblage of utility extension cords, branch circuits, and lighting equipment.
          • Supports mobility by enabling quick assemble/dissemble.
          • Various sizes allow selection to match generator sets from 5-kilowatt to 100-kilowatt.

                                                Appendix A

                Cold Weather Policy Considerations

    This appendix provides planning and policy considerations that commanders and unit leaders
    may want to use in developing unit policy documents. This appendix should be modified to
    supplement the unit’s unique mission, equipment, and local policies. As appropriate, some of
    the points can be incorporated into a unit SOP. Some of the preparation functions mentioned
    below will require months of lead time to properly execute.

STAFF RESPONSIBILITIES

     A-1. The staff will need to conduct analysis to produce unit policies in advance of a unit mission involving
     cold weather environments:

          • Develop risk analysis and threat management policy upon notification of a unit move into a severe
                weather environment. All units use DD Form 2977 (Deliberate Risk Assessment Worksheet). This
                risk assessment policy, and supporting worksheets should at least cover—
                ▪ Medical operations.
                ▪ Maintenance operations.
                ▪ Velocity of supplies, and support constraints.
                ▪ Changes in safety policy and procedures.
                ▪ Security procedures.
                ▪ Limitations to operational and training activities, categorized according to the severity of the
                     weather.

          • Identify gaps between authorized organizational equipment and supplemental materiel requirements
                for each of the following cold weather categories:
                ▪ Arid high temperature, desert can be around 90°F (32°C) or greater during the summer days,
                     with some below freezing winter nights, and day-to-night fluctuations of 20°F (11°C) to over
                     60°F (16°C).
                ▪ Wet cold is about 39°F to 20°F (4°C to -6°C).
                ▪ Dry cold is about 19°F to -4°F (-7°C to -20°C).
                ▪ Intense cold is about -5°F to -24°F (-21°C to -31°C).
                ▪ Extreme cold is about -25°F to -40°F (-32°C to -40°C).
                ▪ Hazardous cold is about -40°F (-40°C) and below.

          • Establish a date or situation (trigger event) when the unit converts to cold weather operations. This
                plan should specify a limited time to complete the conversion once the process begins.

          • Develop and establish the unit’s severe weather maintenance policy. Focus on additional duty
                staffing and severe weather tasks, adjusted for each weather category.

          • Establish command policy for the field use of COTS cold weather protection equipment, clothing,
                and devices. Consider added space, transportation, maintenance, and fuel requirements for the
                additional non-standard equipment.

          • Set unit severe weather PPE policy, adjusted for each weather category. Anticipate requirements in
                each severe weather category and develop a survival kit and survival bag list for each one.

          • Establish command policy on the use of survival gear.
          • Establish policy for the site supervisor to communicate once every four hours with the motor pool

                duty medic, duty roster combat lifesaver in the motor pool, or the senior medically trained Soldier
                supporting post security guard.
          • Inquire after each Soldier with prior cold injuries, and then about any new injuries (preferably
                delegated to the medical noncommissioned officer (NCO) in charge [known as NCOIC], or the
                senior medical NCO, if available).
          • Establish planned quality controls and inspections of the commander’s unit severe weather policies.
          • Conduct after action review of severe weather preparations and training events. Use feedback to
                adjust in planning and policies ahead of deployment or mission.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  115


---

Appendix A

TRAINING

     A-2. Unit leadership is responsible for training Soldiers on individual and collective tasks in cold weather
     conditions:

          • Identify the mission focused—
                ▪ Tactical training priorities.
                ▪ Maintenance training priorities.
                ▪ Medical training priorities.
                ▪ Movement training priorities.

          • Draft training curriculum to cross-train and certify select Soldiers (junior enlisted) from each platoon
                to support unit maintenance. The training should focus on cold weather conditions that require
                Soldiers to be in PPE. The following are several areas to train on:
                ▪ Battery technology and maintenance.
                ▪ Power generator operation and repair.
                ▪ Powertrain disassembly, repair, and rebuild.
                ▪ Vehicle fleet safe engine starting and auxiliary power supply procedures.
                ▪ Vehicle heater operation, maintenance, and inventory administration.
                ▪ Shelter heater operation, maintenance, and inventory administration.
                ▪ Base camp or cantonment area sanitation.

          • Conduct a recertification of all previously qualified combat lifesavers with the emphasis on cold
                weather conditions.

          • Ensure that Soldiers receive proper training in environmental awareness and comply with HAZMAT
                regulations. Soldiers must be able to recognize all HAZMAT that they routinely handle and their
                proper disposal. More importantly, they must know how to contain and mitigate emergency
                situations (like HAZMAT spills) in severe weather conditions.

          • Train operators on adherence to procedures outlined in the TMs and LOs for their equipment.
          • Administer and oversee the licensing of Soldiers to operate field heater equipment.
          • Administer and oversee driver’s refresher training in ice and snow driving.
          • Ensure operators are trained and licensed on assigned equipment, weapon, or vehicles.

LOGISTICS PREPARATION FOR SEVERE WEATHER

     A-3. Senior staff logistics leaders need to help establish and implement the commander’s policies and
     leadership procedures that enable the unit to function in cold weather:

          • Establish a default cold weather standard, with the unit prepared to function in wet and dry cold
                conditions at temperatures down to 5°F (-15°C).

          • Establish a date or trigger event when the unit converts to cold weather operations.
          • Develop a contingency plan for logistics activities within the unit to convert the unit to being

                operational in all levels of cold weather extremes on order.
          • Identify where the unit can draw ECWCS items.
          • Inspect 100% of the ECWCS levels 1 through 5 and wet weather gear of all Soldiers in the fall.

                Shortages should be reported and resolved before conducting training or missions in cold weather.
          • Perform 100% inventory of all tents and tent heater systems (especially component parts) well in

                advance of the winter to replenish shortages.
          • Identify PPE not suitable for severe weather conditions and recommend alternatives to the

                commander.
          • Procure COTS PPE before severe weather missions as directed by the commander.
          • Inventory all COTS equipment before winter. Draft maintenance procedures and lifecycle parts lists

                for all unit COTS items that might be used in the field. Account for additional demands to support
                the use of COTS items. Identify and account for all unit COTS that use or are powered by—
                ▪ Petroleum.
                ▪ Commercial electrical power.
                ▪ Batteries.
                ▪ Lubricants.

116  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                   Cold Weather Policy Considerations

          • Notify the senior medical NCO of scheduled activities that will probably occur under severe weather
                conditions.

          • Coordinate with food services to provide supplemental hot/cold beverages, snacks, and hot food
                regularly at work and training sites. The supplemental rations plan works best when broken up into
                different groupings (a matrix) for each of the weather categories.

          • Encourage Soldiers to notify their leaders of missing and unserviceable initial issue items and
                individual equipment.

          • Arrange for comprehensive sanitation facilities.
          • Organize heated overnight storage for electronics (radios, computers, and monitors) of parked

                vehicles.
          • Provide electrical power connections to winterization kits that keep engines and batteries of parked

                vehicles warmed.

MEDICAL ACTIVITIES

     A-4. Cold weather conditions place an additional workload on medical Soldiers. Consider the following for
     the prevention and treatment of cold weather injuries:

          • Establish and administer quality control policy and procedures for the presence and proper
                resourcing of medical support.

          • Establish a battle buddy system to monitor health and welfare practices during severe weather
                conditions (temperatures below 35°F [2°C]).

          • Have medical support immediately available where there is significant risk to Soldiers.
          • Maintain current and detailed documentation of cold weather injuries in Soldier records. During

                duty periods with cold weather, all Soldiers who have sustained an injury should report to a special
                “post duty sick call” before being released for the day or from an outside duty event.
          • Delegate a medical NCO to make inquiries and write a root cause report on any serious cold weather
                injuries. Give the report to the unit medical officer for consideration when making recommendations
                to the commander. The findings might warrant making changes to the unit cold weather policy.
                Perform reports in accordance with AR 385-10, DA Pam 385-40, and local command policy.
          • Coordinate for medical coverage to be routinely available for—
                ▪ Sick-call clinic.
                ▪ Training sites.
                ▪ Maintenance areas and wash racks.
                ▪ Rest areas and base camps.
                ▪ Convoy and movement activities.
                ▪ POL distribution activities.
                ▪ Munitions distribution activities.
                ▪ Guard posts or other long-lasting outside duties.
          • Establish a duty roster of NCO combat lifesavers who have received special training and are certified
                by the brigade surgeon to recognize and treat cold weather injuries.
          • Actively identify and proactively monitor specific Soldiers who are new to the cold or have
                experienced prior cold injuries.
          • Establish policy for senior NCOs to check Soldiers for proper articles of ECWCS, rain gear, survival
                or first aid gear, correct gloves, and foot gear appropriate for the cold or wet.
          • Ensure that combat lifesavers are certified in severe-weather-related first aid and rehydration and
                maintain proficiency in mitigating and stabilizing cold injuries.
          • Verify that medical staff is documenting all cold injuries and inserting the information into Soldier
                records.
          • Conduct after action reviews of severe weather preparations and training for medical events; use the
                feedback to adjust preparations for deployment.

MAINTENANCE ACTIVITIES

     A-5. Cold weather conditions place an additional workload on maintenance Soldiers. The following policies
     may help mitigate the impact of the cold on maintenance operations:

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  117


---

Appendix A

          • Establish procedures for the operation and maintenance of unit equipment in severe weather
                conditions.

          • Verify that supervisors have both the leadership and technical skills to oversee the proper
                performance of cold weather unplanned maintenance and planned services.

          • Maintain a current list of winterization kits and POL products needed for transitioning the unit to
                cold weather operations.

          • Perform a serviceability inspection of all vehicle heaters well in advance of the winter.
          • Perform an inventory of all vehicle winterization kits and tire chains (including rubber tighteners)

                well in advance of the winter.
          • Manage winterization kits, shelters, and heaters as they become available through supply channels.

                Ensure the kits are correctly installed and that operators understand their proper use and
                maintenance.
          • Perform cold weather maintenance and troubleshooting according to appropriate TMs, LOs, and
                SOPs.
          • Operate vehicles and equipment according to the cold weather sections of appropriate TMs and
                special instructions given by maintenance personnel.
          • Assist operators with vehicle and equipment starting using maintenance auxiliary power resources.

VEHICLE OPERATOR RESPONSIBILITIES

     A-6. Equipment operators and maintenance personnel must comply with the operational checks outlined in
     vehicle TMs and include the procedures listed below.

BEFORE OPERATIONS

     A-7. Operators should anticipate an increase in the time required to ready vehicles and equipment for use:
          • Check to ensure that brakes are not frozen and can be released. The use of portable heating
                equipment may be required.
          • Check springs for cracking due to metal brittleness.
          • Ensure tracks/wheels do not freeze to the ground by placing matting under the tires.
          • Remove built up dirt and ice from the suspension system.
          • Ensure track tension has been adjusted for cold operations by increasing the amount of slack.
          • Check the vehicle operator manual for inflation pressure. Ensure tires are inflated properly for
                operation. Deflate tires to normal pressure if pressure was increased to compensate for extended
                parking. Do not over inflate tires.
          • Ensure all CO2 fire extinguishers have been winterized according to the appropriate fire extinguisher
                TB.
          • Prevent snow or ice from entering crankcase when adding lubricants to an engine.
          • Ensure cooling systems are protected with the correct antifreeze compound.
          • Ensure vehicle is fueled with the proper grade arctic fuel or JP-8 as specified by the TM or
                appropriate TB.
          • If engine is difficult to start, check spark plugs for ice coating due to condensation. For diesel
                engines, check battery for proper charge to ensure glow plug operation.
          • Check batteries to ensure that they are fully charged by measuring the specific gravity of the
                electrolyte or performing a conductance test.
          • Roll and store tarps for ease of employment after operations.

DURING OPERATIONS

     A-8. Operators should review the quick-lookup list of checks at regular intervals during the operation of
     vehicles and equipment. Along with TM checks, the operator should consider additional points that require
     close observation during cold weather operations:

          • Do not tow-start vehicles.
          • Avoid powering up radios, LCD, or LED electrical equipment until it is first warmed up to an above-

                freezing temperature. (Once powered up or turned on, such electronics may be allowed to cool.)

118  TM 4−33.31  12 December 2025


---

                                                                                                   Cold Weather Policy Considerations

          • Drive vehicles around the 150-meter warm-up course at under 15 miles per hour (25 kilometers per
                hour) and then do a during operations check before leaving the unit area.

          • Start vehicles periodically to maintain readiness at temperatures of -20°F (-29°C) to -60°F (-51°C).
                (This practice can be avoided if any other means of keeping engines operable, like engine heaters
                or external heaters, are available.)

          • Avoid idling engines if possible. This practice tends to waste fuel and adds to engine maintenance
                cost.

          • Ensure engine heaters are used properly and maintained according to the appropriate TM.
          • Operate vehicles not equipped with engine or battery heaters at prescribed intervals. Also ensure

                that portable duct type heaters are available for engine compartment preheating.
          • Check chassis and body components for ice, mud, and snow buildup. Remove buildup at every

                opportunity to ensure proper ground clearance and to prevent interference with moving components.
          • Check vehicle chains to ensure they are serviceable and properly fit the tires. As chains become

                loose, retighten them to prevent damaged vehicle parts.
          • Ensure engine oil pressure is maintained for safe engine operation.
          • Maintain normal engine operating temperature through the adjustment of engine compartment inlet

                shutters or radiator covers.
          • Run the engine at a high enough rpm for long enough to recharge batteries and recover lost energy

                from the initial starting.

AFTER OPERATIONS

     A-9. Operators must not only follow after operations PMCS procedures but also give attention to how they
     park the equipment, keep electrical devices protected from freezing, and prevent snow and condensation from
     freezing onto the equipment:

          • At the end of each operating period, and for five minutes prior to shut down, run the vehicle engine
                to normalize its mechanical working and retain oil on cylinder walls.

          • Do not park vehicles with the brakes set because they may freeze and not release. Use chock blocks
                to hold vehicles in place.

          • Never park vehicles overnight where snow has melted that day; tracks and wheels could freeze to
                the ground.

          • Park with tracks and wheels on brush, cardboard, or any similar material to prevent direct contact
                with the ground.

          • Protect radiators from wind, snow, and ice buildup.
          • Ensure the fuel tank is topped off to prevent condensation buildup.
          • Drain air tanks.
          • Electronics equipment with LCDs and sensitive components should be removed from vehicles and

                stored in a heated room, warm tent, or a warm vehicle. Be careful to avoid condensation buildup.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  119

                                                Appendix B

                              Electrolyte Spills

    Electrolyte spills are serious events. Proper accident management is a critical skill for anyone
    handling batteries. This appendix covers basic electrolyte spill procedures and first aid
    instructions.

FIRST AID INSTRUCTIONS

     B-1. Before battery maintenance operations, personnel should identify and write down the electrolyte being
     handled for emergency reference. In an event that personnel are exposed unprotected to electrolyte (battery
     acid), whether liquid, GEL, or powder, follow these instructions immediately:

          • Move unresponsive personnel to fresh air and provide cardiopulmonary resuscitation (known as
                CPR) if needed. Obtain medical attention.

          • Eye contact. Immediately flush eyes with water and continue to flush for 15 minutes. Hold eyes
                open. The first few seconds after contact are critical, and immediate flushing of the eyes may prevent
                damage. An eyewash fountain is preferred, however, an eyewash hose or any other source of clean
                water should be used in an emergency.

          • Skin contact. Begin flushing the area with large quantities of clean water immediately.
          • Contaminated clothing. Remove and discard any article of clothing that is contaminated with

                electrolyte. Do not assume spilled chemicals on clothing are not harmful. The contaminated clothing
                can expose skin to chemical burning. Exposure of contaminant through the skin may cause severe
                pain, damage to organs, and could result in death.
          • Ingestion. Do not induce vomiting. Dilute by giving water. If available, give 2 to 4 cups of milk. Do
                not give anything by mouth to an unconscious person. After flushing, seek immediate medical
                attention without delay. Provide medical personnel with the name of the type of contaminant or
                chemical.

             Note. Consult the precautionary warnings on the product label for full first aid information.
             Provide the label information to the attending physician.

SPILL OR LEAK PROCEDURES

     B-2. Electrolytes are HAZMAT. Consult with the local installation HAZMAT agency for policies on
     establishing battery or electrolyte storage and handling areas:

          • Storage and handling areas should be equipped with proper containment to capture and neutralize
                spills. In addition, these areas should be equipped with eyewash stations and safety showers.

          • Wear acid resistant clothing, boots, gloves, and a face shield when working with batteries and
                electrolyte.

          • Stop the flow of material and contain/absorb small spills with dry sand, earth, or vermiculite. Do
                not use combustible materials.

          • If possible, carefully absorb and neutralize the spill with soda ash, sodium bicarbonate, lime, sand,
                or earth.

          • Baking soda and household ammonia solution are two substances that can neutralize the acid
                contained in battery electrolytes. Have these on hand to clean up shop spills (not for first aid use).
                Mix equal parts of either one with water to create a solution that neutralizes the battery acid.

          • If water alone is used, cautiously dilute with water.
          • Do not allow discharge of un-neutralized acid into waterways, sewers, basements, or confined areas.
          • Place neutralized slurry in sealed containers and dispose of as hazardous waste as applicable.

     B-3. After neutralization and testing, large water-diluted spills should be managed in accordance with local,
     state, and federal requirements. Consult the state environmental agency and/or the federal Environmental
     Protection Agency.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  121

                                                Appendix C

                                Jump Starting

    It is common in cold situations for a vehicle to need its battery power augmented during initial
    startup. This appendix provides limited and general information on equipment start assistance
    through the jump start method.

COMMAND POLICY

     C-1. Maintainers can expect that most cold-soaked vehicles will require assistance with starting. The
     commander should state in the unit maintenance and tactical SOP under what circumstances an operator can
     slave start a vehicle. Commanders should consider establishing startup procedures that protect equipment.
     The SOP should also include defined trigger events where maintainers may receive additional manpower to
     perform maintenance, and equipment operators will start all vehicles, equipment, and devices to prevent cold-
     soak status.

INITIAL STARTING EFFORT

     C-2. Supervisors of operators must know that operators understand and use maintainer-approved procedures
     for starting cold engines. Cold starting may or may not be the same as starting in temperate weather
     conditions. Each vehicle or power plant must be treated as unique in the cold:

          • Performed preventive maintenance as prescribed by the specific item TM and the unit SOP.
          • Disconnect any external or supplemental power sources, unless otherwise specified by the specific

                item TM.
          • Turn off or disconnect any equipment pulling power from the batteries aside from the engine.
          • Once all TM procedures and unit policies are met, the operator may attempt to start the engine.

     C-3. After the third unsuccessful attempt to start a vehicle using its own battery power, contact the
     maintenance team for assistance.

PRIOR TO AUXILIARY POWER ASSIST STARTUP

     C-4. Vehicles or batteries require preparation before attempting an auxiliary power assist startup in cold
     weather conditions. Along with the procedures specified in the applicable TM, maintainers should pay very
     close attention to the following cold weather starting considerations:

          • Wear gloves that protect from electrolyte contact and electrical shock.
          • Wear eye protection.
          • Check that batteries fluids are not frozen. If ice is found or suspected in a battery cell, stop the

                process. Remove and replace frozen batteries.
          • Check to see if a minimal current is present. A good indicator is if the lights turn on or the horn

                works.
          • Confirm that batteries are of the same voltage.
          • Check negative posts to ensure they are properly grounded.
          • Check all battery posts and ensure they are free of corrosion buildup. Ensure cables are secured

                snuggly.
          • Make sure that vehicles are not touching.
          • Make sure that all master switches, ignition switches, accessories, and communications equipment

                are off for both vehicles.
          • Put both vehicles in either park or neutral with brakes on. The brake setting only applies during

                starting (otherwise, in cold, do not apply parking brakes to just park a vehicle).
          • Attach cables to terminals, first to the dead vehicle followed by the vehicle with power.
          • Start the engine of the vehicle supplying the auxiliary power.
          • Increase the engine idling speed of the powered vehicle to higher than normal.
          • Wait 15 minutes for the battery to charge before trying to start, if possible.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  123


---

Appendix C

AUXILIARY POWER RECEPTACLE

     C-5. The auxiliary power receptacle is used to start a vehicle when otherwise good batteries are unable to
     supply enough starting current. The auxiliary power receptacle (Figure C-1) is an electrical outlet located at
     a readily accessible place on the vehicle. It receives current from other vehicles. This provides direct boosting
     of the cold vehicle current when starting an engine in cold weather.

                                                Appendix D

          Properties of Petroleum, Oils, and Lubricants

    This appendix provides planning information for commanders and staff pending their arrival
    in theater. The information herein represents what Soldiers and the unit must be prepared to
    deal with. It is unlikely that Soldiers will encounter all these products.

PETROLEUM

     D-1. The Army adopted the SFP, which can be found in AR 70-12. This is focused on a single kerosene-
     based fuel for use in all equipment. Soldiers should be familiar with alternative fuels for their equipment
     when operating in temperatures below -35°F (-37°C).

             Note. The properties of POL products in this appendix are derived from military performance
             specifications that represent minimal capabilities, and not the full capabilities of any specific
             product. Units should consult with the theater Joint Petroleum Office, in coordination with the
             Army Petroleum Center for specific information on a POL product made available in theater.

             Within this appendix, flash point refers to the temperature at which the vapors of the liquid will
             ignite when there is a source of ignition. Pour point refers to the lowest temperature at which a
             fuel or mixed fluid can remain totally liquid, below which the first components of the fluid freeze
             or crystalize into a slush.

JET PROPELLANT 8 (JP-8)

     D-2. This fuel type is a kerosene-based aviation fuel blended with three additives. Under AR 70-12, JP-8 is
     the primary fuel for land-based air and ground forces in all theaters of operation outside the CONUS. For
     cold temperature environments, use it in accordance with MIL-DTL-83133. JP-8 is also used in CONUS
     when a fuel with a freeze point below -40°F (-40°C) is required (otherwise F-24 is the primary fuel used).

     D-3. JP-8 technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: MIL-DTL-83133.
          • NATO Code Number F-34.
          • Special Characteristics. There are three additives for JP-8:
                ▪ Fuel System Icing Inhibitor; MIL-DTL-85470; NATO# S-1745.
                ▪ Corrosion Inhibitor/Lubricity Improver (CI/LI); MIL-PRF-25017; S-1747.
                ▪ Static dissipater additive.
          • Flash point: 100°F (38°C), minimum.
          • Freezing point: -53°F (-47°C), maximum.

             Note. If JP-8 is not available outside the CONUS, the policy from the theater Joint Petroleum
             Office, in coordination with the Army Petroleum Center, provides guidance on the use of the
             secondary fuels or any deviation from the SFP.

FUEL 24 (F-24)

     D-4. F-24 is the primary fuel used for air and land-based forces in CONUS (except when a fuel with a freeze
     point below -40°F [-40°C] is required, then JP-8 is used). This fuel has a NATO designation of F-24 and is
     defined in STANAG 3747/AFLP-3747 as meeting all performance requirements of the American Society for
     Testing and Materials (ASTM), International D1655 grade Jet A. It must contain the three military additives
     Fuel System Icing Inhibitor, CI/LI, and static dissipater additive.

     D-5. The F-24 conversion in CONUS is a supply chain initiative to increase fuel sources available, increase
     operational flexibility, and increase procurement competition to reduce costs. The key difference between

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  125

Appendix D

     JP-8 and F-24 is freeze point. Otherwise, JP-8 and F-24 are completely compatible fuels for all CONUS
     operations. The Army Petroleum Center will monitor all product testing and stock rotation requirements.
     However, field testing requirements are as directed in the appropriate TM.

     D-6. F-24 technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: NATO Standard, STANAG 3747/AFLP-3747.
          • NATO Code Number F-24.
          • Special Characteristics. There are three mandatory additives for F-24:
                ▪ Fuel System Icing Inhibitor.
                ▪ CI/LI.
                ▪ Static dissipater additive.
          • Flash Point: 100°F (38°C), minimum.
          • Freeze Point: -40°F (-40°C) maximum.

COMMERCIAL JET FUEL

     D-7. ASTM-D1655 is the formulating specifications for purchases of “A” series aviation turbine fuel under
     contract. These specifications are international standards developed by ASTM International. The “A” series
     fuels are essentially identical to F-24 and JP-8, except that they do not necessarily contain the three military
     required additives found in F-24 or JP-8.

Jet A

     D-8. This grade of fuel is only available within the U.S. where temperatures are expected to be relatively
     temperate. Commercial airlines have used this fuel for other than domestic flights when considering its freeze
     point and adjustment to flight pattern.

     D-9. Jet A technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: ASTM-D1655.
          • Flash Point: 100°F (38°C), minimum.
          • Freeze Point: -40°F (-40°C), maximum.

Jet A-1

     D-10. This is the grade of commercial aviation turbine fuel available worldwide. This fuel can serve as an
     alternative to diesel fuel. Not recommended for use in ground equipment for more than one tankful without
     the addition of CI/LI.

     D-11. Jet A-1 technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: ASTM-D1655.
          • Flash Point: 100°F (38°C), minimum.
          • Freeze Point: -52.6°F (-47°C), maximum.

Jet B

     D-12. Jet B is a 70% naphtha–30% kerosene-based fuel used for its enhanced cold-weather performance.

     D-13. Jet B technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: ASTM-D6615.
          • Flash Point: Due to the volatility of the fuel, flash point is not tested. Jet B will flash at extremely
                low temperatures.
          • Freeze Point: -58°F (-50°C), maximum.

Jet Propellant 5 (JP-5)

     D-14. JP-5 is a unique military aviation fuel for use on Navy ships. Due to Navy shipboard safety
     requirements, it is required to have a substantially higher flash point than commercial aviation turbine fuels.

Jet Propellant 4 (JP-4)

     D-16. JP-4 is a naphtha-based fuel. JP-4 is classified as inactive. This fuel is not considered to be an
     acceptable fuel in ground vehicles. The chief difference between JP-4 and Jet B is that JP-4 contains the three
     mandatory additives while Jet-B does not unless requested during procurement. Where prevailing low
     temperatures warrant a fuel with very low temperature operability, United States Army Combat Capabilities
     Development Command recommends using JP-4.

     D-17. JP-4 technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: MIL-DTL-5624.
          • NATO Code: F-40.
          • Flash Point: Due to the volatility of the fuel, flash point is not tested. JP-4 will flash at extremely
                low temperatures.
          • Freeze Point: -74°F (-58°C), maximum.

NATO Fuel

     D-18. Alternate and emergency ground fuels available with NATO are listed in STANAG 1135.

Commercial Diesel

     D-19. Commercially available diesel fuel will be used as the primary fuel for all mobile and stationary
     compression ignition and turbine engine powered ground materiel in theaters of operation where the SFP
     cannot be or has not yet been implemented.

     D-20. Diesel fuel technical data:
          • Definitive Standard: ASTM D975.
          • Flash Point: Number 1-D = 100°F (38°C) / Number 2-D = 126°F (52°C) minimum.
          • Special Characteristics: The performance characteristics of diesel fuels vary greatly due to the broad
                variety of regional standards and seasonal blends.

OILS AND LUBRICANTS

     D-21. Different lubricating oils of various grades have been historically used in engine crank cases by the
     military. This is changing with the introduction of an OE/HDO, termed the SMPL. The old oils were
     identified by the SOE international classification for crude oil-based lubricants. Crude oil products are
     characterized by a limited temperature range relative to synthetic oils.

ENGINE OILS

     D-22. OE/HDO-SMPL is recommended for extreme cold conditions to help deliver quick starts and fast
     lubrication. This lubricant is usually approved for use in engines, power steering systems, and
     automatic/powershift transmissions (based on the item-specific TM). SMPL is intended to be a direct
     replacement for previous applications requiring SAE 10W or arctic engine oil. It is necessary to consult the
     LO prior to use. It is not recommended for two-cycle or aviation engines unless specifically approved by the
     TM. The use of OEA or OE/HDO-SMPL may void the warranty in some transmission applications. As a
     general recommendation, when switching from OE/HDO 15/40, OE/HDO-40, OE/HDO-30, or OE/HDO-10
     to SMPL, the oil drain interval may be doubled.

TRANSMISSION OILS

     D-24. Low temperatures can adversely impact the performance of transmission oils and greases used in parts
     of military equipment other than the engine. OE/HDO-SMPL or OEA 30 can be used for automatic
     transmissions and hydraulic and power steering systems. Both are compatible with most types of automatic
     transmission fluids (including DEXRON III). OE/HDO-SMPL and OEA-30 can be mixed with other
     transmission fluids, but that may degrade the low temperature properties or other performance limits of the
     fluid.

     D-25. Some manufacturers for manual transmissions, transfers, differentials, and final drives may require
     different lubricants in specific applications.

     D-26. 15W/40 is the preferred oil for semiautomatic transmissions (including in all Caterpillar manufactured
     transmissions, and for 4500SP Allison transmissions for M1074 and M1075 model trucks) when
     temperatures permit.

WEAPON LUBRICANTS

     D-30. Unless otherwise specified by MIL-PRF, the chemical formula of the oil is the prerogative of the
     contractor. All articles submitted to the Government must fully meet the operating, interface, support and
     ownership, and environmental standards that are specified in the MIL-PRF or procurement requirements. For
     example, some COTS synthetic oil versions of general-purpose lubricants have an operating range of over
     500ºF (260°C) down to below -65ºF (-54°C). Deployment planners can only rely upon published generic
     information. However, maintenance leaders have the option to rely on product capability from data sheets
     for the products as they become available.

     D-31. Lubricating Oil, General Purpose, Preservative (Water Displacing, Low Temperature), is a crude-oil-
     based weapon systems lubricant for low temperatures. It is intended for lubrication and protection against
     corrosion of certain small arms and automatic weapons. It may also be used whenever a general purpose,
     water displacing, low temperature lubricating oil is required for other items besides weapons. This
     preservative oil should not be used to protect the fuel system or combustion chamber of engines. Technical
     data is as follows:

          • Standard: MIL-PRF-32033.
          • Military symbol: PL-S
          • NATO Code: O-190.

          • Lower limitation: -49°F (-45°C).

          • Pour Point: -60°F (-51°C), maximum.

     D-32. Cleaner, lubricant, and preservative (CLP) is the primary lubricant for small arms, crew-served, and
     artillery weapon systems. It is a multipurpose product that performs three functions of breaking up carbon
     residue, lubricating metals as a penetrating oil, and repressing corrosion. Technical data is as follows:

          • Standard: MIL-PRF-63460.
          • Military symbol: CLP.
          • NATO Code: S-758.
          • Storage temperatures: n/a.
          • Upper useful limit: 160°F (71°C).

                                                 WARNING

                    DO NOT use CLP with any other lubricant. CLP when mixed with
                    other lubricants may break down into gum and cause weapon
                    malfunction or damage the weapon.

     D-33. Lubricant, Small Arms, Semi Fluid, Automatic Weapons is strictly a lubricant. It is a petroleum-based
     (non-synthetic) lubricant mixed with a combination of detergent, oxidation inhibitor, and corrosion
     inhibitors. It provides oxidation and corrosion resistance as well as rust protection and anti-wear properties.
     Technical data is as follows:

     lubricant with polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE, better known by the trade name Teflon®) molding powder
     added for improved lubrication. It is used on several large caliber weapons systems, various aircraft, naval
     ships, and grenade launchers. It is authorized for use on rifles, pistols, and as a cleaning and sharpening oil
     for bayonets. The lubricant must be shaken well before use as the Teflon® is suspended in the petroleum.

          • Standard: MIL-PRF-14107.

GREASE LUBRICANTS

     D-36. The function of grease is to adhere to an item and provide lubrication, regardless of position or open
     exposure. The upper and lower limits are general functional temperature regions that most equipment is
     engineered to accept. The properties of the grease changes beyond those temperatures and may become too
     thick or thin to maintain its optimum protection. At the dropping point or freeze point, the grease ceases to
     be functional. The dropping point represents when a grease becomes so hot that it could fall free of the item
     it is supposed to be protecting. The petroleum component runs like water, and the thickener burns such that
     it can no longer gel with the lubricant oils. At the freeze point, the lubricants begin to crystalize into a solid,
     leaving the thickener to be damaged by the friction.

     D-37. The technical data for some different grease lubricants is listed below:

          • Grease, Automotive and Artillery:
                ▪ Standard: MIL-PRF-10924.
                ▪ Military symbol: GAA.
                ▪ NATO Code: G-403.
                ▪ Upper Limit: 356ºF (180°C).
                ▪ Lower Limit: -65ºF (-54°C).
                ▪ Dropping point: 428°F (220°C), minimum.
                ▪ Freeze Point: n/a.

                ▪ Military symbol: WTR [wide temperature range].
                ▪ NATO Code: G-395.
                ▪ Upper Limit: 350°F (177°C).
                ▪ Lower Limit: -65°F (-54°C).
                ▪ Dropping point: 450°F (232°C), minimum.

     D-40. Hydraulic Fluid, Rust Inhibited, Fire Resistant, Synthetic Hydrocarbon Base is a corrosion inhibited,
     synthetic based hydraulic using polymer, alpha-olefins that are up to 70% more fire resistant than OHT. It is
     intended for use in recoil mechanisms and hydraulic systems of military ground vehicles and equipment.
     However, not all vehicles and equipment have been revised to accommodate FRH, in part because FRH does
     not perform well at low temperatures:

          • Standard: MIL-PRF- 46170.

     D-48. Cleaning Compound, Solvent (for the bore of weapons) or RBC, is designed specifically for cleaning
     out carbon and powder from the chamber and bore of a weapon. It is not a lubricant or a degreaser. It works
     best for tough cleaning after heavy shooting (weapons must be lubricated after using RBC):

          • Standard: MIL-PRF-372.
          • Military symbol: RBC.
          • NATO Code: none.
          • Storage temperatures: -65°F (-54°C) to 77°F (25°C).

     D-49. The solvents under MIL-PRF-680 are petroleum distillate degreasing solvents used for degreasing
     machine parts in equipment maintenance. They are also known as “mineral spirits” or “petroleum spirits.”
     Degreasing Solvent, Type II, High Flash Point is used for weapons or lubricated parts cleaning during the
     change over from one lubricant to another. As a dry-cleaning solvent, it will remove all petroleum and
     synthetic lubricants, including penetrating oils that have soaked into the micro fractures of the metal, leaving
     a totally dry weapon or part. SD-2 does not remove rust and oxidation. Do not use SD-2 on rubber, plastic,
     or sealed buffers:

          • Standard: MIL-PRF-680.
          • Military symbol: SD-2.
          • NATO Code: S-753.
          • Storage temperatures: n/a.
          • Flash point (range): 140°F to 198°F (60°C to 92°C).
          • Upper useful limit: n/a.
          • Lower useful limit: n/a.
          • Freeze point: n/a.

                                           Appendix E

             Environmental Protection Concerns

E-1. Despite the difficulties cold weather may present to unit operations and maintenance, leaders must
remember that units are required to comply with federal, state, local, and host-nation environmental
regulations. Accumulated waste fluid should be disposed of through a waste oil recovery program unless
prohibited by local law. The references for federal regulations are promulgated by the U.S. Environmental
Protection Agency under Public Law 94-580, the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act of 1976.

E-2. Many cold weather areas contain fragile environmental ecosystems that could be damaged significantly
by spills or improper hazardous waste and materials disposal. Increased fuel consumption coupled with the
use of special oils, lubricants, batteries, and the failure of equipment from low temperatures increases the
possibility of environmental damage.

E-3. Considering the increased risk, it is important that leaders conduct unit environmental self-assessments
prior to deploying to a cold weather area. The self-assessment looks at the following: management, waste oil
storage, HAZMAT/hazardous wastes, solid waste management, spill prevention, recycle program, wash
racks, and land management. They must ensure that the resulting risks are made part of the unit environmental
awareness training program.

E-4. GTA 05-08-002 provides information on the environmental risk management process, including the
steps of the process, hazard probability, hazard severity, and how to determine the risk category.

12 December 2025  TM 4−33.31  135
